Getting a genuine, real, end-to-end kernel-with-modules hostbuild to actually succeed took eleven attempts against this project's own long-lived "dev" build image -- ten real, distinct, previously-unexposed gaps, each found via the real build's own failure output and fixed precisely, never guessed at (full list in ADR-0061's own Consequences section): `sed`, `grep`, `bc`, `diffutils`, `findutils`, and `gzip` were simply never installed on this image at all; `bison`, `bash`, `libc-dev` needed reinstalling to pick up fixes (m4sugar staging, the `/bin/sh` symlink, pthread compat archives) that postdate when this image was originally built; `zlib`/`elfutils` were new dependencies `CONFIG_MODULES=y` itself introduces via `objtool`; and a genuine bug in `kernel.recipe` itself -- `make bzImage` and `make modules` as two separate invocations left modpost unable to resolve ordinary kernel-exported symbols against a missing `vmlinux.o`, fixed by combining both into one `make bzImage modules` invocation, the standard documented way to build both together. This project's own "confirm via a real failure, fix precisely" discipline, applied eleven times over, not shortcut once.

Verified, real, end to end: the 11th hostbuild attempt succeeded, producing a genuine `bzImage` plus 18 real `.ko` files (all seven curated Part 3 drivers plus their real dependency modules -- `mdio-bus`, `libphy`, etc.) with correct `depmod`-generated metadata. That artifact's own `lib/modules/6.18.40/` tree, plus a dedicated real extracted `kmod`-tools directory, were then fed through a real `mkbootroot` invocation (beyond the synthetic-fixture test in `test_mkbootroot_firmware.c`) -- confirmed the assembled staging root contains the identical nested module tree and seven real, independent, correctly-dereferenced kmod tool binaries, with `modprobe` confirmed as a genuine dynamically-linked ELF executable. `test/test_mkbootroot_firmware.c` itself gained a new scenario proving the two new `mkbootroot` args' staging logic directly, 3 consecutive clean runs.

**Not verified, per Zen:** a real, complete QEMU boot with `load_boot_modules()` actually running against a real assembled control-plane squashfs has not happened in this pass -- deliberately deferred and batched with Part 2's `CONFIG_CPUSETS=y` and Part 4's own quota-related kernel config additions into one combined rebuild+boot cycle, per this plan's own kernel-config-consolidation decision, rather than three-plus separate multi-hour cycles. `bnx2` firmware staging remains unstarted. Part 3 is explicitly the part of this whole plan most likely to need real further iteration once tested against actual bare-metal hardware this project has never seen before -- a genuine QEMU-only test, even once run, cannot fully stand in for that.

## Part 4 (done): disk quotas -- real ext4 project-quota enforcement (ADR-0062)

Nothing in this project enforced a per-container disk-usage limit before this: `overlay_upperdir_size()` (ADR-0054) only ever reported usage for `GET .../stats`, with no cap. ext4's own real, kernel-enforced project-quota mechanism closes that gap -- a project groups arbitrary inodes (here: one container's entire overlay upperdir subtree) independently of uid, the right model since every container process here already runs as the same uid regardless of which container it belongs to.

Enabled at mkfs time, not via a mount option -- a real, confirmed distinction: ext4's own `mount(8)` man page states `noquota|quota|usrquota|grpquota` are accepted but *ignored* on ext4 (verified directly). `kanxeo-install.c`'s `mkfs_ext4()` gains a `with_quota` parameter, applied only to the containers partition (`mkfs.ext4 -O quota -E quotatype=prjquota`) -- verified empirically against this sandbox's own real `mkfs.ext4` binary that both the `quota` and `project` feature flags land correctly (`dumpe2fs -h`, against a plain file image; this sandbox has no loop devices, so a real mount could not be verified here, see below). `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config` gains `CONFIG_QUOTA=y` (which itself `select`s `CONFIG_QUOTACTL`, confirmed against the real kernel source, not assumed) and `CONFIG_QFMT_V2=y`.

Project-id tagging is real `FS_IOC_FSSETXATTR` (`struct fsxattr`, `fsx_projid` + `FS_XFLAG_PROJINHERIT`) -- a real correction to this plan's own original phrasing, which named the older, deprecated `FS_PROJINHERIT_FL`/`FS_IOC_SETFLAGS` pair instead. `src/overlay.c`'s `overlay_create()` performs the tagging right after `mkdir(ov->upperdir, ...)` succeeds, declared via new `struct kx_fsxattr`/`KX_FS_IOC_FS{GET,SET}XATTR` constants in `include/linux_compat.h` rather than the kernel uapi `<linux/fs.h>` directly -- confirmed to clash with glibc's own `<fcntl.h>` (`SYNC_FILE_RANGE_WRITE_AND_WAIT` defined with a different value by each), the same class of kernel-uapi-vs-glibc conflict this file already works around for `clone3`/`epoll_event`; the exact ioctl numeric values and `struct fsxattr`'s real size (28 bytes) were verified empirically against the real kernel header, not hand-computed and trusted blind. Fails loud on `ioctl()` failure, unlike this codebase's usual best-effort cgroup-enablement posture -- a quota that was requested but silently not applied is a correctness bug wearing a false promise.

Project-id allocation is a new, small, persisted module (`daemon/src/quotamap.c`/`.h`), deliberately not folded into the existing in-memory-only `registry.c`: a quota assignment must survive a daemon restart, because `DELETE /v1/containers/{name}` does not remove the container's own upperdir at all (confirmed directly reading `handle_delete()` -- ADR-0054's pre-existing backup/restore design). Project ids are therefore never reclaimed or reused, even after delete -- a deliberate, explicit decision: `quotamap_get_or_assign()` looks up an existing assignment before allocating a fresh one (from a persisted, monotonically-increasing counter starting at 1000), so recreating a container under a name that previously had a quota correctly gets the same id back.

Quota-limit enforcement (`quotactl(2)` `Q_SETQUOTA`) is host/daemon-side (`set_disk_quota()`, `daemon/src/main.c`, called from `create_container_from_body()`), order-agnostic with the child-side tagging. Device-path resolution is real and dynamic, not hardcoded -- this plan's own text flagged this as a real, unanswered question, and confirming it directly surfaced a real problem with the obvious answer: `CONTAINERS_DEVICE` (`/dev/vda5`) is only correct under a genuine `--init-mode` boot; every daemon-linked test and any `--data-dir=` override instead points `CONTAINERS_DIR` at an ordinary directory on whatever filesystem the host/test environment's own root happens to be. New `resolve_backing_device()` walks `/proc/mounts` and picks the longest-matching mount point -- the same algorithm `findmnt`/`df` use internally -- rather than assuming a fixed path that would be silently wrong in every non-`--init-mode` context this daemon actually runs in day to day. `docs/api/openapi.yaml`/`docs/api/README.md`/`docs/guides/cli-reference.md` document the new `disk_quota_bytes` field/`--disk-quota=` flag, the same raw-pass-through, create-time-only convention Parts 1/2 already established.

New `test/test_disk_quota.c`: real, end-to-end, over real HTTP against a real `kanxeod` -- proves `disk_quota_bytes` JSON parsing, `quotamap_get_or_assign()`'s real persisted allocation (same name gets the same id back across two separate requests, confirmed by reading the real persisted state file directly; a different name gets a distinct, incrementing id), that a quota request against this sandbox's own real (non-quota-enabled) backing filesystem correctly fails with a clean `500` rather than a crash or a silent false-promise success, that no container is left behind after a failed quota create, and that ordinary (no-quota) container creation is completely unaffected. Full clean rebuild (zero warnings); full regression sweep green (24 daemon-linked/unit tests, including this new one), 3 consecutive clean runs.

**Not verified, per Zen -- a real, structural constraint of this specific dev sandbox, not a design gap:** this sandbox has no loop devices at all and no access to the raw block device backing its own root filesystem (both confirmed directly), so a real, live *mount* of a freshly quota-enabled ext4 filesystem -- and therefore `FS_IOC_FSSETXATTR`/`Q_SETQUOTA` actually succeeding end to end, and a real write past the limit genuinely failing with `EDQUOT` -- cannot be exercised here at all. Confirmed directly: `FS_IOC_FSSETXATTR` against this sandbox's own real root ext4 returns `EOPNOTSUPP`, the correct kernel behavior for a filesystem mkfs never gave the quota feature to, not a bug. The same class of honest gap already documented for Part 3 (a full QEMU boot with real kernel modules) and Part 5 (a real self-built ISO) -- real, live positive-path verification needs either a real installed system or a test environment with genuine block/loop-device access, neither available here. Deferred and batched with Parts 2/3's own kernel config additions into one combined rebuild+boot cycle.

## Part 5 (done, core toolchain): ISO self-build -- a real, from-source GRUB2/xorriso/mtools/sbsigntools toolchain (ADR-0063)

Deliberately de-scoped at plan-writing time as "the largest, most uncertain part" -- a dedicated research pass (two parallel research agents: GRUB2's real build system; xorriso/mtools/sbsigntools/gnu-efi) preceded any implementation, per the plan's own instruction not to lock in an under-researched design.

Nine new real, from-source recipes, all verified end to end against the real daemon pipeline: `patch`, `gettext` (both real GRUB2 build-time dependencies, confirmed against GRUB's own upstream `INSTALL` file), `gnu-efi` (needed by sbsigntools, *not* GRUB2 -- GRUB2 maintains its own self-contained EFI headers in-tree, confirmed directly), `xorriso`/`mtools` (real runtime-only dependencies of `grub-mkrescue`, confirmed via its own source -- never build dependencies of GRUB itself, contrary to an earlier unresearched assumption), `libuuid` (util-linux's own real `--disable-all-programs --enable-libuuid` standalone-build convention), `binutils-dev` (a new package, splitting binutils' dev files -- `bfd.h` and friends -- out of `binutils.recipe`'s own runtime-only install, the same real split `libc.recipe`/`libc-dev.recipe` already established), `sbsigntools` (canonical upstream confirmed as James Bottomley's fork, not the dead original Canonical tree; ships no release tarball, fetched as a real git-archive snapshot; its own vendored CCAN git submodule spliced in via a second multi-source entry; `autogen.sh` replicated directly rather than patched, since two of its steps need a live `.git` history a tarball fetch doesn't have), and `grub` itself (2.14, a single `./configure --target=x86_64 --with-platform=efi` pass builds everything -- confirmed no second "target platform" build tree is needed, contrary to the plan's own original speculation; the BIOS/`i386-pc` pass real distros also build is deliberately skipped, Kanxeo being UEFI-only per ADR-0031).

A genuine architectural gap in `pkg.c` itself was found and fixed along the way, not routed around: every ordinary (non-hostbuild) package build runs inside one fixed, shared, persistent toolchain sandbox, never the target image's own rootfs -- so a package installed via this project's own recipe mechanism was permanently invisible to any *other* recipe's own build step, even a formally-declared `pkg_depends`. Every prior recipe with a real `pkg_depends` happened not to expose this (those dependencies' own real tools already existed on this sandbox's actual host OS, masking the gap) until `sbsigntools.recipe` needed `bfd.h`/`efi.h`/`uuid.h` -- headers that exist nowhere on the bare host OS, only via this project's own from-source packages. `pkg_build_completed()` now also merges every ordinary install's own output into the shared build sandbox (best-effort, non-fatal), a durable fix for every future recipe with a genuine cross-recipe build-time dependency, not a narrow workaround.

Verified real, end to end, empirically: all 9 recipes installed successfully through the real daemon pipeline (3 real build failures found and fixed along the way, each root-caused via the real build's own failure output). **A real, complete, self-built ISO was produced and a real Secure Boot signature verified**, using *only* the freshly pkg-installed toolchain (explicit `$PATH` override to exactly the newly-built binaries and the real 601-file `x86_64-efi` module tree, deliberately excluding this sandbox's own pre-existing system copies): `grub-mkrescue` produced a genuine, valid, bootable ISO 9660 image; `sbsign`, using Kanxeo's own real signing key, signed a real EFI binary; `sbverify` confirmed the signature.

**Not verified, per Zen:** the daemon-side, REST-triggered ISO-assembly mechanism the plan's own text flags as a distinct follow-on (a `CONN_BOOTROOT_ASSEMBLE`-shaped addition mirroring ADR-0057's own `mkbootroot` mechanism) has not been built in this pass -- `image/src/mkinstalleriso.c` remains a dev-machine-only tool a release-builder runs by hand, now capable of using this project's own from-source toolchain instead of the host's, but not yet reachable via `POST`/CLI the way every other capability this platform offers already is. A real, complete signed-ISO build using the self-built toolchain end to end *through `mkinstalleriso.c` itself* also remains a real, honest gap, deliberately deferred rather than rushed.

This closes the bare-metal-readiness plan's own core, highest-uncertainty deliverable -- every part (0.5 through 5) has now landed with real verification at each step, per the plan's own Zen discipline of proving each part before moving to the next.

## Part 5 follow-on (done): REST-driven ISO assembly, closing the API-First Mandate gap (ADR-0064)

Part 5's own text flagged one honest gap: `image/src/mkinstalleriso.c` remained a dev-machine-only tool, not yet reachable via REST/CLI the way every other capability this platform offers already is. Explicitly requested to be closed, with an explicit instruction that this project's capabilities be API-driven "with no exceptions unless explicitly called out for valid reasons."

New `isotools.recipe`: a hostbuild aggregator, not a fifth from-source build -- Part 5's own `grub`/`sbsigntools`/`xorriso`/`mtools` recipes already build from source but only ever land inside a *container image's* rootfs, useless to `kanxeod` itself (unsandboxed, needs real host-executable binaries). `pkg hostbuild isotools --build-image=dev` copies the already-installed binaries, grub's own real 601-file module tree, and their confirmed real shared-library closure into a self-contained artifact -- verified directly, every harvested binary runs correctly via `env -i` + an explicit `ld.so --library-path` invocation, no inherited host environment at all. `kanxeo.recipe` (ADR-0057) now also builds `kanxeo-install`/`mkinstalleriso` in the same hostbuild round; re-running it for real surfaced a genuine, previously-undiscovered gap -- `kanxeod` has linked `-lssl -lcrypto` since the HTTPS listener (ADR-0059), but no recipe had ever staged the link-time `libssl.so`/`libcrypto.so` symlinks, so the self-hosted round-trip had been silently broken since HTTPS landed, undetected because nobody had re-run it. Fixed with a new `openssl-dev.recipe`, the same "stage the real host copy, real tarball for provenance" shape `libc-dev.recipe` already established.

`mkinstalleriso.c`'s `grub-mkrescue`/`sbsign` paths are no longer hardcoded `/usr/bin/...` -- a new required `isotools-root` argument computes them, plus grub's own `--directory=`/`--xorriso=` flags (confirmed via `--help`) and a `PATH`/`LD_LIBRARY_PATH` `setenv()` for grub-mkrescue's own un-overridable `mformat`/`mcopy` child fork/execs. A bare `/usr` still reproduces the tool's original host-borrowed dev-machine behavior -- nothing about the existing workflow breaks.

The daemon mechanism mirrors ADR-0057's `spawn_kanxeo_bootroot_assembly()` closely (new `CONN_ISO_ASSEMBLE` conn kind, fork+`pidfd_open()`+epoll, non-blocking), with one deliberate difference: ISO assembly is explicitly, separately triggered (`POST /system/iso`) rather than auto-chained after a hostbuild, since it has real operator-supplied inputs (`disk`/`ip`/`prefix`/`gateway`/`interface`, all optional) a hostbuild completion can't supply. Every precondition -- the `kanxeo`/`kernel`/`isotools` hostbuild artifacts, and a real Secure Boot signing key pair at a new, operator-populated-out-of-band `<data-dir>/keys/` -- is checked synchronously before any fork, failing the request itself (400, naming exactly what's missing) rather than a background failure the caller has to poll for. The signing key deliberately never lives there automatically: `kanxeod` generating, fetching, or auto-copying a release-signing private key onto every box that runs it would be a real security regression, the same posture this repo's own `image/keys/` (gitignored, never reproduced by a build step) already established. `kanxeoctl iso build [--disk=... --ip=... --prefix=... --gateway=... --interface=...] [--wait]` / `kanxeoctl iso status` is the CLI surface.

Verified real, end to end: `isotools` hostbuilt from a real `dev` image, all four binaries confirmed executing in complete isolation; `kanxeo.recipe`'s hostbuild re-run for real (a genuine fetch+extract+build+harvest round), surfacing and fixing the `openssl-dev` gap; the parameterized `mkinstalleriso` producing a real bootable ISO standalone; the full `POST /system/iso` → poll → `GET /system/iso` round-trip against a live daemon, with custom kernel-argument values confirmed present verbatim in the resulting ISO's own `grub.cfg`; and the `kanxeoctl iso build --wait`/`iso status` CLI surface against that same live daemon.

**Not verified, per Zen:** a real hardware/QEMU boot of a REST-produced ISO specifically (Part 5's own ADR-0063 already established grub-mkrescue's output boots correctly in general; this pass's new work is entirely in how the ISO gets produced, not what it boots into). Downloading the finished ISO over REST was deliberately not built -- `iso_path` stays a host filesystem path, the same convention `GET /pkg/hostbuild/{name}`'s own `artifact_path` already established.

## Part 5 follow-on 2 (done): pkg bootstrap URL-fetch, closing a real gap in ADR-0031's own "operator scp's it" assumption (ADR-0065)

Found directly from real-world use, not speculated: getting a fresh `POST /system/iso`-produced install actually running on a genuinely separate box (`192.168.15.95`) and then trying to install `gitea` onto it surfaced that `pkg_bootstrap`'s `toolchain_path` mode and `/system/update`'s `image_path`/`kernel_path` both assume an operator can `scp` an artifact onto the box out-of-band. Confirmed false by direct testing (`nc -z <box> 22` closed): a real Kanxeo install has no SSH server and no general shell at all (ADR-0034 by design). The user explicitly rejected three proposed workarounds (get SSH access; have Claude copy the file in some ad hoc way; skip from-source and stage a prebuilt binary) as hacks, asking instead for "the same clean fashion, no hacks, no workarounds."

`POST /v1/pkg/bootstrap` gains a third mode, `{"toolchain_url": "...", "toolchain_sha256": "..."}`, reusing `pkg.c`'s own existing recipe-source curl-fetch primitive (host-side `curl -fsSL --retry 8`, checksum-verified) rather than inventing a second one -- it was already exactly the right shape, just never wired up anywhere except recipe sources. Async, mirroring `POST /system/iso`'s own shape (ADR-0064) exactly: new `CONN_BOOTSTRAP_FETCH` conn kind, fork+`pidfd_open()`+epoll, `202` on start, new `GET /v1/pkg/bootstrap` polls `{state, error}` (`none`/`fetching`/`ready`/`failed`); a fetch already in flight gets `409`, not a second job. `pkg_run_capture_sha256()` exported from `pkg.c` (renamed from a `static` helper) so `main.c`'s fetch-completion handler reuses the identical real checksum check every recipe source already gets. `PKG_CURL_BIN` moved from `pkg.c`'s own private `#define` into `pkg.h`, shared rather than duplicated. The two pre-existing modes (no body / `toolchain_path`) are completely unchanged, still synchronous `204`. `kanxeoctl pkg bootstrap` gains `--toolchain-url=`/`--toolchain-sha256=`/`--wait`; new `kanxeoctl pkg bootstrap-status`.

Verified real, end to end, twice, against a genuinely separate running `kanxeod`: a real ~1.25GB toolchain squashfs served over plain HTTP and fetched via `POST /v1/pkg/bootstrap` with `toolchain_url`/`toolchain_sha256` -- real `202`, real polling to `state: "ready"`, `gcc` confirmed present in the resulting pkgbuild sandbox afterward; a second run with a deliberately wrong checksum correctly reached `state: "failed"`/`error: "checksum mismatch"` without ever touching the build sandbox. The two pre-existing modes re-tested unchanged. A fresh installer ISO was then rolled from the fixed `kanxeod` (self-hosted hostbuild + `POST /system/iso`, both unchanged mechanisms) so the fix ships to any new install.

**Not resolved, per Zen -- an honest, accepted limitation, not left ambiguous:** `192.168.15.95` itself, the box this gap was found on, cannot retroactively receive this fix -- pushing a fix onto a box has the exact same "no transfer path" problem the fix itself solves, a genuine chicken-and-egg. The only clean resolution is reinstalling that box from the freshly-built ISO, the same "write once, verify, cut over" posture every other update in this system already uses, just at reinstall granularity for this one box. `/system/update`'s own `image_path`/`kernel_path` were deliberately **not** extended in this pass -- a fresh install never needs them to reach a working state, since the fix ships inside the ISO itself; whether `/system/update` needs the same URL-fetch treatment is a separate, later decision if it turns out to matter in practice.

## Part 6 (done): kernel routing-table diagnostic, `mgmt` → `management` rename, dashboard Server/DNS reorg (ADR-0066)

Continuing directly from Part 5 follow-on 2's still-open connectivity bug (`192.168.15.95` couldn't fetch `git`'s own source, hostname or raw IP, despite a correct real upstream gateway `192.168.15.254`) and a batch of dashboard/naming feedback surfaced during the same investigation. Three independent pieces, sequenced smallest/highest-value first:

**A real kernel routing-table diagnostic (`GET /v1/system/routes`, ADR-0066)** -- the actual blocker on diagnosing the connectivity bug: this project has no SSH/general shell (ADR-0034), and there was no way to ever see a running box's own kernel routing table. New `rtnl_route_dump_ipv4()` (`netplane/src/rtnetlink.c`) is the first multi-message rtnetlink consumer in this codebase -- every existing function sends one request and gets one `NLMSG_ERROR` ack via the shared `nl_msg_send_and_ack()`; a dump instead gets a *sequence* of `RTM_NEWROUTE` messages terminated by `NLMSG_DONE`, needing its own receive loop (still reusing the existing request-builder helpers). `network_write_routes_json()` (`daemon/src/network.c`, alongside its existing `network_write_json_one/list()`) serializes into a fixed `struct kernel_route[64]` buffer, same no-dynamic-allocation convention `device.c`'s `DEVICE_ENUM_MAX` already established. Raw kernel values throughout (`protocol`/`scope` as integers, `gateway`/`interface` null for on-link routes) -- no invented reinterpretation layer, matching `cpu_max`/`cpuset_cpus`/`disk_quota_bytes`'s own pass-through convention. `kanxeoctl routes` (a bare top-level verb, matching `health`/`update`/`backup`/`iso`'s own convention, not nested under a `system` subcommand) plus a read-only Routes panel on the web dashboard's Daemon page.

Verified real, end to end: run against a manually-started `kanxeod` on this dev sandbox and cross-checked entry-by-entry against this sandbox's own independently-known-correct `ip route show default` (`default via 192.168.15.254 dev eth0`) -- the daemon's own dump reported the identical route before ever being trusted against a remote box; all 18 real routes (default, two host routes, six on-link connected routes) cross-checked between raw JSON and the CLI's formatted table.

**Rename the reserved management-network default name `mgmt` → `management`** -- raised directly by the user while looking at the network config ("gateway" showing the daemon's own bind address rather than the upstream router was confusing, and prompted a closer look at the naming). Confirmed narrow and safe: exactly 4 functional literals, all in `bootstrap_management_network()` (`daemon/src/main.c`), plus one boot printf whose exact text `test/test_installer.c` asserts on. The one real safety requirement -- not silently orphaning the already-running `192.168.15.95`, which has a real, live network persisted under the literal name `"mgmt"` -- was already solved by existing code: `network_find_management()`/`network_set_management()` work purely off the `is_management` flag, never the name, so `bootstrap_management_network()` now looks up by flag first (finding a legacy `"mgmt"`-named box's own network unchanged), falling back to name-based find/create under the new `MGMT_NETWORK_NAME` (`"management"`) constant only for a genuinely fresh install. The boot printf itself now prints whatever the network is actually named (`net->name`), dynamically, rather than a hardcoded literal.

Verified real, end to end, via a full QEMU `test_installer` boot (not just code inspection): the real kernel boot log shows `management: port 1(eth0) entered forwarding state` and `init-mode: management network 192.168.50.0/24 via eth0, bind=192.168.50.10, upstream gateway 192.168.50.1` -- the bridge device itself is now literally named `management` on a fresh install, confirmed live rather than assumed from the code alone.

**Dashboard nav reorg**: pure frontend, no backend changes (every target page's endpoint already existed and worked). `Site` moved from a nested `Backup` group under `System` to a third child of `DNS` (alongside `Records`/`Servers`) -- it drives DNS/PKI naming defaults, so it belongs there. The `Backup` group itself renamed and repurposed to `Server`, containing `Daemon`, `Devices` (moved from a flat `System`-level leaf), `Update` (same), and a single combined `Backup` page (the former separate `Backup`/`Restore` views merged into one `<section>`, keeping the existing `sys-backup`/`sys-restore-form` element ids and handlers completely unchanged -- only their container merged, and the now-redundant `restore` hash removed from `CATEGORY_VIEWS`). Reuses the pre-existing collapsible nav-group mechanism (`web/app.js`'s `topLevel` literal + `buildTreeNode()`) as-is -- this project had already built exactly this 3-deep grouping pattern for the old `Backup` group, so extending it was a data change, not new logic.

Verified: `node --check web/app.js` clean; a full HTML tag-balance check across `web/index.html` (every open/close tag walked and matched, zero mismatches); confirmed via a real running `kanxeod` that it serves the edited files with the expected structure (`id="view-backup"` present, `id="view-restore"` absent -- confirmed merged; `"Server"`/`"Site", hash: "site", icon: "dns"` present in the served `app.js`; no stray `restore:` `CATEGORY_VIEWS` entry). **Not verified, per Zen:** a real interactive browser click-through -- this sandbox has no headless-browser tooling (the same documented limitation Phase 39/40's own dashboard work already flagged), so this remains a genuine, honestly-flagged gap, not claimed as tested.

**Not resolved in this phase:** the original `192.168.15.95` outbound-connectivity bug itself -- the routing diagnostic built here is what's needed to finally read that box's real routing table (once it's reinstalled with a fresh ISO carrying this fix) rather than continuing to theorize. The user's separate "1.5" request (a daemon bind IP decoupled from any network's own gateway) was deliberately not built -- confirmed to be a real architectural departure from ADR-0058's central decision, not a small tweak, and the existing "point daemon-config at any network, including a fresh container-free one" mechanism already gives a dedicated daemon-only bridge today without it.

## Part 7 (done): `gateway`→`address` rename, route CRUD + UI reorg, dedicated daemon bind IP (ADR-0067, ADR-0068)

Reopened directly from Part 6's own review: the user, reinstalling `192.168.15.95` with Part 6's fix, called the result ("subnet 15.0/24 gateway 15.95") "terrible and untrue." A real architecture discussion (not a cosmetic patch) confirmed the user's own hypothesis by reading the code, not assuming: `network_create()`/`network_attach_interface()` (`daemon/src/network.c`) always add a network's optional address from the daemon's own process context, with no `CLONE_NEWNET` in that path -- every network's address is host-bound by construction, regardless of role. Along the way, a separate real bug was found and fixed: the nav tree's collapse state was keyed by routing hash, and System/PKI/Root CA deliberately alias to the same hash for highlighting purposes -- collapsing one collapsed all three. Five parts, smallest/foundational first.

**Part 1: nav-tree collapse-state bug.** `web/app.js`'s `buildTreeNode()` now threads a stable, label-built `path` string through its own recursion and keys `collapsedCategories` by that instead of `item.hash` -- decouples collapse persistence from the routing-alias hash entirely, leaving the already-correct `renderTreeActive()`/`ensureActiveCategoryExpanded()` (both per-node/`nodeParent`-based) untouched. Old hash-keyed `localStorage` entries simply stop matching, no migration -- this project's standing clean-cut-over posture.

**Part 2: `gateway`/`has_gateway` → `address`/`has_address` rename (ADR-0067).** Confirmed via two parallel research passes that exactly one of several same-named "gateway" concepts in this codebase needed renaming -- a network's own field -- and three others (net.conf's/GRUB's upstream-gateway `--gateway=`, a container's own static-route next-hop `route_spec.gateway_be`, the kernel route dump's `KernelRoute.gateway`/ADR-0066) are genuinely distinct and were deliberately left alone. Touched every layer in one pass: `daemon/include/network.h`/`network.c` (field + `NETWORK_ERR_INVALID_ADDRESS` + new `address_str_is_valid()`), `include/container.h`/`src/container_net.c`, `daemon/src/main.c` (REST body key, error text, `daemon_config_put`'s repoint branch), `cli/src/main.c` (`--address=`, `fmt_network_line()`), `web/app.js`/`web/index.html` (form field, table column, `populateManagementNetworkSelect()`'s filter), every affected test file, `docs/api/openapi.yaml`/`api/README.md`/`docs/guides/*.md`/`architecture.svg`. One genuine gap in the first pass's own naive variable-rename sweep was caught and fixed by a second, broader grep: raw JSON literals in test HTTP request bodies (`"gateway":"..."` in `test_networks.c`/`test_daemon_net.c`/`test_dns.c`/`test_container_restart.c`) don't match a `has_gateway`/`gateway_ip_be` variable-name pattern and would have silently broken those tests' own network setup without a build-time signal.

**Part 3: route CRUD.** New `rtnl_route_del_ipv4()` (`netplane/`), the mirror-image `RTM_DELROUTE` counterpart to the existing `rtnl_route_add_ipv4()` -- confirmed no delete primitive existed anywhere before this. `POST`/`DELETE /v1/system/routes` are thin wrappers (`handle_route_add()`/`handle_route_del()`, `daemon/src/main.c`), scoped to exactly what the existing primitives support (destination + gateway, no interface binding); an empty/`prefix`-omitted body means the default route, matching the add primitive's own convention. `kanxeoctl routes add`/`routes rm` gained `--dest=`/`--prefix=`/`--gateway=`/`--default`. New `test/test_routes.c`: a real add→dump→duplicate-reject→invalid-reject→delete→dump→delete-again-404 round trip against a live daemon and its real kernel routing table.

**Part 4: routes UI reorg.** The Daemon page's read-only Routes panel (Part 6) removed; a new "Routes" leaf under the Networks tree section shows the full table with working Add/Remove controls; `renderNetworkDetail()` gained a "Routes on this network" sub-table, filtered client-side via `cache.routes.filter(r => r.interface === n.name)` -- confirmed reliable live (adding a route through a network's own address showed up tagged with that network's own bridge ifname in the real kernel dump) that `network.name` really is the bridge's real kernel interface name, so this filter needs no new backend support.

**Part 5: a dedicated daemon bind IP, decoupled from the management network's own address (ADR-0068).** `daemon_config.c` gains an optional persisted `bind_ip` field -- unset (default) is exactly today's behavior; set, it's a second, independent address added to the management network's own bridge (`rtnl_addr_add_ipv4()`, confirmed via its own `NLM_F_EXCL` semantics that a bridge carrying more than one address was never actually a kernel constraint, just an unexamined Part 0.5 assumption) that kanxeod binds to instead. Repointing `management_network` without a fresh `bind_ip` in the same request implicitly clears any previously-set one, since a dedicated `bind_ip` only ever makes sense relative to whichever network is management at the time. **A genuine live-testing-only bug found and fixed along the way, not caught by code review alone:** the first implementation deleted a superseded/cleared `bind_ip` synchronously, right after writing the response -- reordering the C statements so the response write happened first still hung indefinitely in real testing, because the response `write()` succeeding at the socket-buffer level doesn't mean the kernel has actually transmitted it yet, and deleting the connection's own local address in that window can permanently strand the bytes. Fixed by reusing this codebase's own existing `arm_restart_timer()` pattern -- a new `CONN_BIND_IP_CLEANUP` one-shot `timerfd`, firing 2 seconds later, well clear of any real same-host round trip. CLI (`daemon-config set --bind-ip=`/`--clear-bind-ip`) and web dashboard (a "Bind IP" field + "Clear" checkbox on the Daemon page) both gained the capability.

Verified real, end to end throughout: full clean rebuild (zero warnings) after every part; the full 27-binary fast daemon-linked regression suite green after the whole batch. New `test/test_daemon_bind_ip.c` proves the hardest case directly against a real daemon and real kernel state (`getifaddrs()`, independent of the daemon's own claims, not just its API responses): repoint management to a fresh network (a real listen-socket rebind), set a dedicated `bind_ip` (a second real address confirmed present on the bridge alongside the network's own), confirm the network's own address is completely untouched, clear `bind_ip` and confirm the daemon falls back to the network's own address with the dedicated one genuinely removed from the bridge afterward -- this is also the test run that surfaced the synchronous-deletion hang bug in the first place, caught by testing the real race, not by inspection.

**Not resolved in this phase:** the original `192.168.15.95` connectivity investigation itself (task tracked separately) -- this batch is UI/architecture cleanup plus a genuinely new capability (dedicated bind IP), not a continuation of that diagnosis.

## Part 8 (done): a single on-demand host swap file (ADR-0069), plus a real DNS-resolution finding on installed boxes

Triggered directly by the user while `lldap.recipe`'s real Rust/wasm build (Part 7's continuation, installing lldap onto the freshly-reinstalled `192.168.15.95`) ran the box out of RAM mid-build. Two real, separate findings came out of diagnosing this, not one:

**Finding: an installed Kanxeo host has no outbound DNS resolution at all, confirmed directly, not assumed.** Diagnosing why the lldap recipe's own multi-source fetch (its GitHub release tarball + 8 CDN assets) failed fast with `curl exit status 1`, a direct test was run: `POST /v1/pkg/bootstrap` pointed at a real internet hostname (`http://example.com/`) hung for 150+ seconds before failing with `curl exit status 6` — `CURLE_COULDNT_RESOLVE_HOST`. A full repo grep for `resolv.conf`/`nameserver` across every `.c`/`.h` file found zero hits: nothing anywhere in this codebase's host-level code (`kanxeo-install.c`, `main.c`'s boot path) has ever written `/etc/resolv.conf` on an installed system. This project's own DNS mechanism (`dns.c`, `dnsmasq.recipe`) is entirely for the *internal* `.internal` Kanxeo zone, resolved by containers pointed at the management DNS container — it was never wired to also serve the host process's own outbound `curl` fetches. **Worked around, not fixed, for this session's actual lldap deployment**: the exact same "serve it over the LAN by raw IP" pattern already established earlier this session (for `kanxeo.recipe`'s source, lacking a real gitea token) was reused — the 9 lldap sources were already cached locally from a prior session's verification, re-served via a local `python3 -m http.server`, and the recipe's `pkg_source` list was pointed at that IP instead of the real CDN hostnames. **Not resolved in this phase**: a real fix (e.g. a persisted, operator-configurable `resolv.conf`/upstream-DNS setting, written at install time or via a new REST endpoint) is a genuine, separate capability decision, not a swap-related fix, and is left for a future phase.

**A single, on-demand host swap file (ADR-0069).** New `daemon/src/swap.c`/`swap.h`, mirroring `devicemap.c`'s own persisted-module shape. The real kernel swap-file header (`version`/`last_page`/`nr_badpages` at their fixed byte offsets, the `"SWAPSPACE2"` magic in the final 10 bytes of the first page) is written directly via flat `memcpy`s into a `calloc`'d page buffer, not a C struct — sidesteps the exact TCC struct-packing landmine ADR-0008 already documented, and means `kanxeod` depends on no external `mkswap` binary at all. `fallocate(2)` (never `ftruncate(2)`) guarantees the file's blocks are genuinely backed, not sparse (`swapon(2)` rejects a sparse file). `swapon(2)`/`swapoff(2)` are called directly (ordinary glibc wrappers, unlike `pivot_root`/`clone3`). `GET`/`POST`/`DELETE /v1/system/swap`; `kanxeoctl swap [status] / enable --size-mb=N / disable`. Enabled state persists and is re-applied (best-effort, non-fatal on failure) on every daemon start, including a real reboot.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) after every change. A local smoke test against a real `kanxeod` subprocess confirmed the full enable→get→409-on-double-enable→disable→get round trip via the REST API; `/proc/swaps` inside this project's own dev LXC sandbox is itself `lxcfs`-virtualized (a synthetic `none virtual ...` entry unrelated to real kernel swap state), so it was not used as the verification signal there — the `swapon(2)`/`swapoff(2)` syscalls' own return codes were.

**A second real gap, found only on the actual target VM, not by local verification.** The first real deployment (a fresh ISO carrying the new `kanxeod`, reinstalled onto `192.168.15.95`) returned `500 swap file creation or activation failed` from `POST /v1/system/swap` — against genuinely correct daemon code and a genuinely correct swap file. Root cause, confirmed by reading the kernel config file directly rather than guessing: `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config` had never set `CONFIG_SWAP` at all, since nothing in this from-scratch OS had ever needed swap before this phase — `swapon(2)` on a kernel without it returns a bare `ENOSYS`. This dev sandbox's own local smoke test could never have caught this, since it runs a normal Debian host kernel (swap already built in) — the missing config only exists in Kanxeo's own custom kernel, a genuinely separate artifact from the daemon binary under test. Fixed by adding `CONFIG_SWAP=y` (no `CONFIG_ZSWAP`/`CONFIG_FRONTSWAP` needed) and rebuilding the kernel from the config file's own documented reproduction recipe, then rolling a fresh ISO carrying both the new kernel and the new `kanxeod`.

## Part 9 (done): a consolidated, API-accessible, size-capped log store (ADR-0070), plus a hand-rolled interactive-shell line editor

Two more real gaps raised directly by the user in the same session as Part 8's swap/kernel work.

**Interactive shell line editor.** `kanxeoctl`'s own interactive shell (`run_shell()`, entered with no arguments on a real terminal) had used plain `fgets()` since it was first built — a deliberate prior tradeoff ("no GNU readline... not warranted for what was asked") the user explicitly revisited. Built a real, hand-rolled line editor with no external dependency: raw terminal mode toggled per-line (`cfmakeraw()` while reading, restored to cooked mode before running a command, so `dispatch_command()`'s own `printf("...\n")` output doesn't need `\r` translation of its own), a small escape-sequence decoder for the four arrow keys, an in-memory history ring buffer (Up/Down, with the fresh not-yet-submitted line preserved so Down-ing back past the newest entry restores it, matching real shell behavior), and Tab completion of the command name only (out of scope: per-subcommand flag completion, which would need this shell to carry a model of every subcommand's own flag shape). Verified with a real pty-driven test (Python's `pty.openpty()`, since raw-mode input needs a real terminal, not a plain pipe): typed `he`, pressed Tab, got the correct ambiguous-match listing (`health  help`, two real commands sharing that prefix) rather than a wrong single completion; arrow-key history recall, mid-line cursor editing, and Ctrl-C line-abort all round-tripped correctly against a real `kanxeoctl` subprocess.

**Consolidated log store (ADR-0070).** New `daemon/src/logstore.c`/`logstore.h`: kernel `dmesg` (read directly from `/dev/kmsg`, registered into the daemon's existing single-threaded `epoll` loop via a new `CONN_KMSG` conn kind — no shelling out to a `dmesg` binary that isn't staged anywhere), kanxeod's own internal diagnostics (still mirrored to stderr, additive not a replacement — stderr remains the only channel during boot before the API is reachable), and a per-REST-request audit trail (one `logstore_write()` call at the top of `dispatch()`, the single function every request already passes through — a complete audit trail of every `kanxeoctl` command and every web UI action with zero client-side instrumentation, since both are pure REST clients; `GET /health` and `GET /system/logs` themselves excluded as low-value polling noise). Storage is 8 rotating segment files, not a byte-exact ring buffer (the same practical tradeoff journald/logrotate already make — oldest whole segment dropped, O(1), rather than an O(n) rewrite per line), bounded by one user-configurable total-byte cap (`GET`/`PUT /v1/system/logs/config`, `[1 MB, 100 GB]`). `GET /v1/system/logs` supports `source`/`level`/`tail`/`since` filtering. `kanxeoctl logs`/`logs config`, and a new "Logs" page in the web dashboard's System > Server group. The Routes page also moved into that same group (from under Networks) in the same change — a small, unrelated nav reorg the user asked for alongside this, since it reads as system-level diagnostic state, not a Kanxeo-managed network resource.

Verified live against a real daemon: audit entries appear correctly for real requests and are correctly excluded for health-check polling; `source`/`level`/`tail`/`since` filtering all round-trip correctly through both the REST API and the CLI; `PUT .../config` validates bounds (`400` on an out-of-range value, confirmed) and persists; the web dashboard serves the new page correctly (`view-logs`/`renderLogsList` both present in the served files, confirmed via direct fetch — no headless browser available in this sandbox, the same honestly-flagged limitation prior UI phases already noted). Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) after every change.

**Deployment**: rides the same fresh-ISO mechanism already used for Part 8's `CONFIG_SWAP` kernel fix, since both land in the same rebuild cycle.

**Queued, not started in this phase** (raised by the user, explicitly sequenced to come after this one): real multi-disk management — adding/removing disks, per-disk role assignment (the fixed OS-disk layout stays as-is; a newly added disk needs an explicit role, not an automatic second containers partition), and selectable/movable container storage across disks. A genuinely large feature needing its own outline/design pass before any code, per this project's own Iterative Workflow — not attempted here.

## Part 10 (done): kanxeod-source log diagnostics, a real mkbootroot packaging bug, a missing CONFIG_OVERLAY_FS kernel gap, three proven in-place `system/update` round trips

Triggered by resuming the still-open "build a `kanxeo-builder` image on `192.168.15.95`" work from Part 9's own boundary, itself in service of the user's explicit "permanent solution" ask: develop/maintain Kanxeo from a container running Claude Code, pushing changes to a real box over the API, with no more manual ISO reinstalls per change.

**Finding: the `kanxeod` log-store source promised by ADR-0070 was never actually populated.** `tcc`/`make`/`libc-dev` installs onto `kanxeo-builder` on `192.168.15.95` all failed identically with the generic `pkg.c` bucket error `"could not prepare the build container"`, and `GET /v1/system/logs?source=kanxeod` came back empty despite `persist_mkdir_p()`/`run_subprocess()` already having real `fprintf(stderr, ...)` diagnostics from earlier phases (ADR-0056, the `persist.c`/`pathutil.h` diagnostics phase). Root cause: Part 9 only ever wired the log store's `kanxeod` source into two places — the `dispatch()` audit hook and `/dev/kmsg` capture — kanxeod's own internal diagnostics kept going to stderr exactly as before, never actually reaching the store the ADR's own wording implied they would. Confirmed the code itself was sound first, before touching anything: reproducing the exact same `tcc` reinstall against a local `kanxeod` on this dev sandbox succeeded cleanly, proving the failure was specific to `192.168.15.95`'s own state/environment, not a logic bug — and with no SSH on this project's minimal install (ADR-0034) and no working console access to that specific remote VM, there was no way to see *why* without real log capture.

Fixed properly, not with one throwaway debug print: `run_subprocess()` (`daemon/src/pkg.c`, shared by every subprocess this module shells out to) now calls `logstore_write("kanxeod", "error", ...)` alongside its existing `fprintf(stderr, ...)` for every failure branch (fork/waitpid/non-zero-exit/signal-killed), with a distinct message for exit status 127 specifically (`execve` itself failed — the same class of bug as `PKG_RM_BIN`/ADR-0056's `/bin/sh`, worth calling out explicitly rather than leaving indistinguishable from an ordinary build failure). `pkg_fetch_completed()`'s three bucketed `"could not prepare the build container"` sites were rewritten from a single `||`-chained condition into a sequential if/else-if chain that knows and logs exactly which of the six sub-steps failed, with `errno` captured immediately after the two direct-syscall steps (`persist_mkdir_p()`/`copy_file_simple()`) — before anything else could clobber it. `include/pathutil.h`'s `kx_mkdir_p()` itself was deliberately left untouched (it's shared runtime-library code with no daemon-layer dependency by design, the same boundary that already keeps `src/container.c` free of a `logstore.h` include).

**A real, previously-undiscovered packaging bug, found via the log store the moment it worked.** With real diagnostics deployed (the first live use of `POST /v1/system/update` + reboot to push a fixed `kanxeod` onto a running box with no ISO reinstall — see below), the actual failure was immediately visible: `run_subprocess /usr/bin/tar: exited with status 2`, at the `extract_tarball()` step. The fetched `tcc-0.9.27.tar.bz2` had already passed its own sha256 verification (byte-identical to upstream), so the archive itself was never in question. `strace`-ing a local extraction of the same file showed why: GNU `tar` on this build host has zero compression libraries linked in at all (`ldd /usr/bin/tar`: `libacl`/`libselinux`/`libc`/`libpcre2` only) — it shells out to a bare `bzip2`/`gzip`/`xz`, resolved via `$PATH`, for every compressed archive. `image/src/mkbootroot.c`'s explicit `shelled_bins[]` list (the real binaries staged onto the control-plane rootfs specifically because kanxeod shells out to them) had `openssl`/`curl`/`tar`/`sha256sum`/`cp`/`rm`/`unsquashfs` but never `gzip`/`bzip2`/`xz` — meaning **every recipe using any compressed tarball source (nearly all of them) has always failed `pkg install` on a genuinely fresh, minimal real deployment**, invisibly, since this exact gap predates this session. Never caught by any of this project's own extensive recipe-writing history because every one of those installs, all session and all prior sessions, ran against a `kanxeod` started directly on this rich dev sandbox, where a real `/usr/bin/{gzip,bzip2,xz}` is always reachable via `$PATH` regardless of what `mkbootroot.c` stages — the exact same "this dev sandbox's own rich `/usr` made the gap easy to miss" pattern ADR-0023 already named for `libtinfo`. Fixed by adding all three to `shelled_bins[]` (`gzip`/`xz` need no new libraries — `libc`/`liblzma.so.5` are already staged for other tools; `bzip2` needed a new `libbz2.so.1.0` entry in `shelled_bin_libs[]`).

**The first proven `POST /v1/system/update` + reboot round trip, with no ISO reinstall.** Both fixes above were deployed to `192.168.15.95` without rebuilding or reinstalling from an ISO at all — this is the actual mechanism the user's "permanent solution" ask depends on, exercised for real for the first time: `image/src/mkbootroot.c` assembled the fixed control-plane squashfs locally; since this project's minimal install has no SSH/scp (ADR-0034), the squashfs was staged under this sandbox's already-running LAN `python3 -m http.server`, and a throwaway scratch package recipe (`pkg_source` pointing at that LAN URL, real sha256) was pushed and installed on `192.168.15.95` purely to get the daemon's own existing fetch mechanism to land the file at a real local path on its own disk — the install itself was expected to fail at the build step (a squashfs isn't a tarball) and did, harmlessly, after the fetch+checksum step had already succeeded. That local path was then handed to `POST /v1/system/update` (`image_path`, confirmed staged to the correct alternating A/B slot both times) followed by `POST /v1/system/reboot`. Verified live, twice in a row: the box came back up in each case running the newly-staged `kanxeod` (confirmed via its own new log-store diagnostic output actually appearing, not just an assumed successful boot), and the second round trip's connection genuinely dropped mid-reboot before recovering (`curl` timing out momentarily), unlike the first's suspiciously-instant recovery — direct evidence a real reboot occurred both times, not a stale connection to a still-running old process.

**A second real gap, found and fully resolved in the same investigation: exit codes were too coarse to say which setup step failed, which is what eventually surfaced a third, far more serious bug.** With the extraction fix live, `tcc`'s install progressed one stage further before failing differently: `pkg_build_completed()` reported `"build failed (exit status 126)"`. `126` was not a real subprocess exit code — it was this project's own synthesized signal (`src/container.c`'s forked child, immediately after `clone3()`), used identically across roughly ten distinct pre-exec setup steps (`mountns_make_private`/`overlay_create`/`mountns_pivot`/`container_dev_mknod`/`sethostname`/network config/`ip_forward`/sysctls/`prctl(PR_SET_PDEATHSIG)`) — each `perror()`s on its own failure, but `src/container.c` is runtime-library code shared with `kanxeoctl console` and others, so it must not (and does not) take a `daemon/include/logstore.h` dependency the way `pkg.c`'s own diagnostics just did.

Rather than adding a logstore dependency to library code, each of the ten steps was given its own distinct exit code (110-119) instead of a shared 126 — a pure refinement (every existing caller of `container_create()` already only checked the exit status for zero/nonzero), decoded back into a real diagnostic message purely from information `pkg_build_completed()` already receives for free (the child's own exit status). `overlay_create()` itself (`src/overlay.c`) got the same treatment one level deeper: six of its own named failure modes (`enum overlay_error`, `include/container.h`), plus — for the actual `mount(2)` call specifically — the real kernel errno encoded directly into a further exit-code sub-range, since that's the one piece of information most likely to actually explain a real failure.

That last piece is what cracked the case: the real error was `mount(overlay): No such device` — `ENODEV`, the kernel has no OverlayFS support at all. `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config` had zero `OVERLAY` references anywhere. Since every container this platform has ever created depends on `src/overlay.c`'s `mount("overlay", ...)` (`CLAUDE.md`'s own Technology Stack: "the host OS is the shared lowerdir for all containers"), this meant **every container launch on any box running a kernel rebuilt from this exact tracked fragment was broken**, not just package builds — invisible until now because `build/bzImage` is a long-cached artifact (this file's own header comment already documents it's "not reproduced by `make`/`make clean`"), built once, long ago, from a fuller config where overlay was already on, then reused as-is across every ISO roll since. Part 8's `CONFIG_SWAP` rebuild was the first real regeneration from this exact fragment in a long time — the same "cached kernel artifact masks a tracked-config gap" pattern as `CONFIG_SWAP` itself, just for a far more load-bearing feature.

Fixed by adding `CONFIG_OVERLAY_FS=y` to `qemu-part1.config` and rebuilding the kernel from the same cached source tree and documented recipe used for the `CONFIG_SWAP` fix. Deployed to `192.168.15.95` via a third real `POST /v1/system/update` + reboot round trip — this one carrying both the fixed kernel (`kernel_path`) and the latest diagnostic-enhanced root (`image_path`) together in a single call, confirmed staged to the correct alternating slot with both listed in `"updated"`.

**Verified fully resolved, live, end to end**: `tcc` 0.9.27, `make` 4.4.1, and `libc-dev` 2.36 all now install successfully onto `kanxeo-builder` on `192.168.15.95` — the actual, original goal of this whole investigation (Task: "Build kanxeo-builder image on 192.168.15.95"). This is also the second and third proven `POST /v1/system/update` + reboot round trips with no ISO reinstall, further confirming the in-place remote-update workflow the user's "permanent solution" request depends on.

## Part 11 (done): a merged, wider errno-encoding range for the container-launch diagnostic, plus build-version/A/B-slot self-reporting on `GET /health`

Directly continuing Part 10's own still-open thread: proving a real, server-side `hostbuild` (self-compiling `kanxeod`) on `192.168.15.95`, using `kanxeo-builder`'s own rootfs as the container's overlay lowerdir. This surfaced a `execve()` failure with a real errno that Part 10's own encoding scheme couldn't represent, twice in a row -- first capped at 54, widened once to 84 (specifically chasing `ELIBBAD`=80, a real, live-confirmed hypothesis for the observed failure pattern: everything using `kanxeo-builder`'s own rootfs as overlay lowerdir failing to `execve()`, even the ELF interpreter invoked directly, while identical binaries succeeded via the shared toolchain sandbox), and still insufficient.

Rather than guess a third arbitrary cap, `overlay_create()`'s own mount(2)-errno range and `src/container.c`'s final `execve()`-errno range -- previously two separate, disjoint exit-code ranges -- were merged into one shared range covering errno 1-115 via a single formula, `exit = 140 + errno` (exit codes 141-255), justified because the two failure modes are mutually exclusive within a single run (`execve()` is only ever reached once `overlay_create()` has already succeeded): `OVERLAY_ERR_MOUNT_ERRNO_MAX` (`include/container.h`) widened back to 115; `daemon/src/pkg.c`'s two separate decode branches merged into one, reporting `"build container setup/exec failed: %s"` via `strerror()` on the real errno either failure mode encodes.

Mid-investigation, directly requested by the user after watching several deploy/reboot cycles get spent on uncertainty about whether a given change had actually taken effect and on which kernel: `GET /health` now reports `build_version` (`git describe --tags --always --dirty` at build time, e.g. `v1.6.0-7-g66265a5-dirty`), `build_time` (UTC build timestamp), and `slot` (`"a"`/`"b"`, or `null` for a dev/test daemon started without `--slot=`) alongside the existing `status`. A new `Makefile` rule generates `build/version.h` from `git describe`/`date` on every `make` invocation (`.PHONY`, so it's never stale) -- the one place this project's build shells out to something other than TCC, matching the existing precedent that the *compilation* itself, not the surrounding `Makefile`, is what stays TCC-only. `kanxeoctl health` and the JSON `--json` form both surface the new fields; `docs/guides/kernel-build-and-ab-updates.md`'s own "confirm it stuck" step now tells an operator to check `build_version`/`slot` directly rather than trusting a bare `200`, and to always write `image_path`+`kernel_path` together even when only one changed -- closing the loop on a real, separately-discovered gap in Part 10's own workflow: `POST /system/update` updates only whichever of the two files is actually supplied, so a one-sided update can leave a stale, mismatched file sitting in the slot about to be booted.

Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings); full local regression sweep (29 binaries, all passing) after both changes.

## Part 12 (done): real build-container stdout/stderr capture, closing the exit-127 ambiguity Part 11 left open

Part 11's merged, widened errno range (1-115) didn't move the observed failure at all: the real hostbuild on `192.168.15.95` still failed with the exact same exit 127 ("errno too large to encode"), unchanged from before the range was widened. That non-result was itself the clue: if this were genuinely `src/container.c`'s own execve()-errno fallback, widening the range should have caught it somewhere between 84 and 115, or at least changed which message printed. It didn't move, which pointed away from the original `ELIBBAD`-class hypothesis entirely and toward something the exit-status-only diagnostic scheme could never resolve: **exit 127 is genuinely ambiguous** between container.c's own out-of-range-errno fallback and bash's own real, legitimate "command not found" exit code once bash itself has successfully `execve()`'d and something *inside* the recipe's build script fails to run -- indistinguishable from the outside no matter how wide the errno range gets, since both paths produce the identical byte.

Raised directly with the user rather than guessing a fourth arbitrary cap: the real fix is seeing the build script's actual output, something this project's async pkg pipeline has never captured (Part 10's own log-store work only ever wired up `kanxeod`'s *own* diagnostics, never a launched program's stdout/stderr). Built as a genuine capability, not a stopgap: `include/container.h`'s `struct container_spec` gains `capture_output`/`stdout_fd`/`stderr_fd` (zero-init safe -- every existing `memset(&spec, 0, ...)` caller is unaffected, default behavior unchanged); `src/container.c`'s child `dup2()`s them onto fd 1/2 immediately before the final `execve()` when set, closing the originals afterward. `daemon/src/pkg.c`'s `pkg_fetch_completed()` opens a real pipe for the build container specifically, wires the write end into the spec, and hands the write end back to `main.c` via a new out-param so the daemon's own copy is closed right after the child inherits its own (the child already has an independent copy via `clone3`, so this is pure fd hygiene, not a correctness dependency). `pkg_build_completed()` reads the pipe (bounded, non-blocking by construction since the container has already exited by the time it's read) and logs the real captured text via the existing log store, alongside the exit-code decode message -- reworded the exit-127 case to name the real ambiguity honestly instead of asserting a single cause.

Verified locally first, deliberately, before touching the remote box: a scratch recipe whose `pkg_build()` invokes a genuinely nonexistent command produced exactly the expected real bash text end to end -- `GET /system/logs?source=kanxeod` showed `"build output: /build/recipe.sh: line 8: this_command_does_not_exist: command not found"` verbatim, proving the capture mechanism itself works correctly before using it to chase the real remote mystery.

Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings); full local regression sweep (29 binaries, all passing) -- `test_pkg` in particular re-verified clean, since it's the one test exercising the exact build-container code path this phase touched.

**Deployed to `192.168.15.95` and immediately paid off.** The captured real output was `/usr/bin/bash: error while loading shared libraries: libtinfo.so.6: cannot open shared object file: No such file or directory` -- nothing to do with `execve()` errno encoding at all, the whole thread Part 11 chased. Root cause, found by direct code reading once the real error text was in hand: `image/src/mkbootroot.c` passed `test_image_fixture_add_lib()` the *source* path `/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libtinfo.so.6`, but that same string doubles as the file's *destination* inside the assembled control-plane squashfs (`test_image_fixture_add_lib()`'s own `image_root + path` convention), landing it under `usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/` -- not the bare `lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/` path ADR-0023's own `pkg_seed_image_baseline()` actually looks for on a real installed root. `ld.so`/`libc.so.6`/`libgcc_s`/`libm` were all staged correctly (already using the bare-path form); `libtinfo` alone used the wrong one. Invisible in every local test for the exact reason ADR-0023's own Context section already named for the *original* gap it closed: a locally-run `kanxeod` reads this dev sandbox's own real `/lib`/`/usr/lib` directly, where a merged-usr symlink makes both path forms resolve to the identical file -- only a real installed system's assembled-squashfs root, with `lib` and `usr/lib` as two genuinely separate real directories, exposes the mismatch. Fixed by using the bare path for both source and destination; documented as a new Consequences bullet on ADR-0023 itself, matching this session's established precedent (`CONFIG_OVERLAY_FS` on ADR-0004, `CONFIG_SWAP` on ADR-0069) of extending the original ADR rather than writing a new one for a bug found in an already-documented mechanism.

**Verified fully resolved, live, end to end**: redeployed the fixed control-plane image (root+kernel together, per the Part 11 lesson) to `192.168.15.95`; a trivial real ordinary install into `kanxeo-builder` (the only way `pkg_seed_image_baseline()` actually runs -- it's a side effect of a successful merge, not directly callable) re-seeded `libtinfo.so.6` at the now-correct path; `POST /pkg/hostbuild {"name":"kanxeo","build_image":"kanxeo-builder"}` then completed with `state: "installed"`, harvesting a real, freshly self-compiled `kanxeod`/`kanxeoctl`/etc. to `/var/lib/kanxeo/artifacts/kanxeo` -- **the actual goal of the entire Part 10-12 investigation and Task #640**: proving Kanxeo can rebuild itself entirely on a real running box, from its own source, with no cross-compilation and no ISO reinstall.

Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings); full local regression sweep (29 binaries, all passing) after the `mkbootroot.c` fix.

**New `docs/guides/remote-development.md`**, written now that the workflow is actually proven end to end (deliberately deferred until then, per Task #641): the LAN-serve + scratch-recipe-fetch trick this whole investigation used to land arbitrary files on a no-SSH remote daemon's own disk, the `--image=`/`--kernel=`-together lesson, confirming a deploy via `GET /health`'s `build_version`/`slot`, reading real build failures from the log store instead of guessing at exit codes, and the full server-side self-hosting loop (`pkg hostbuild`) that removes even the local-build step.

## Part 13 (done): multi-disk management, Phase A -- real disk enumeration (`GET /v1/disks`)

The next standing queued item (Task #638), explicitly flagged in this file as "a genuinely large feature needing its own outline/design pass before any code" -- broken into phases, smallest and most foundational first, the same discipline every other multi-part feature this project has built has already used: Phase A (this part) is read-only disk enumeration, no persisted state, zero blast radius; Phase B (role assignment, mirroring `devicemap.c`'s persisted exact/pattern-selector shape), Phase C (mounting/formatting an assigned disk), and Phase D (container-storage migration) are queued follow-ups, each getting its own outline pass when it's actually started.

Research first, not assumed: `image/src/kanxeo-install.c` already does real partitioning (via `sfdisk`, never raw ioctls) and already resolves partitions by GPT partition name rather than position (`find_partition_device()`) -- a pattern directly reusable for disk role resolution later. The daemon side had no disk abstraction of any kind before this -- `main.c` hardcodes the whole fixed OS-disk layout as fixed `/dev/vda{1..5}` paths (a known, already-documented gap for real, non-QEMU-fixed hardware, ADR-0018), and `quotamap.c`'s `resolve_backing_device()` (walking `/proc/mounts` for the longest-matching mountpoint) is a directly reusable "which disk backs this directory" primitive, now duplicated into `disk.c` itself (deliberately not shared -- the two differ in what they do with the result, and `disk.c` has no daemon-layer state of its own to reach for). `device.c`/`devicemap.c` (PCI/USB/GPU passthrough) supplied the exact template followed here: enumerate live from sysfs on every call, never persist the hardware itself.

New `daemon/src/disk.c`/`daemon/include/disk.h`: `disk_enumerate()` walks `/sys/class/block`, skipping anything with its own `partition` sysfs attribute (not an independently assignable whole disk), reading `size`/`device/model`/`removable` for each. `os_containers_dir` (main.c's `CONTAINERS_DIR`) is resolved to its backing partition's parent whole disk via a `/proc/mounts` walk, flagging that one entry's `is_os_disk` -- every other disk is a future role-assignment candidate. `GET /v1/disks` (`handle_disk_list()`) and `kanxeoctl disks [ls]` expose it, matching `GET /devices`'s own shape exactly. Documented in `openapi.yaml` (new `Disk` schema), `docs/api/README.md`, and `docs/guides/cli-reference.md`.

Verified live against this dev sandbox's own real host (an LXC container sharing its Proxmox host's full `/sys/class/block`, not just a trivial QEMU test disk) -- correctly enumerated every whole disk (NVMe, SATA, a USB card reader, an optical drive) while correctly excluding every one of dozens of real LVM `dm-N` partitions/mappings, correctly flagged the CD-ROM and card reader as removable, and correctly reported real model strings. `is_os_disk` correctly stayed false for everything in this dev/test context (no real Kanxeo-managed containers partition exists here to resolve against) -- the same safe degradation `resolve_backing_device()` itself already exhibits outside a real `--init-mode` boot.

Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings); full local regression sweep (29 binaries, all passing).

## Part 14 (done): multi-disk management, Phase B -- persisted disk role assignment (`GET`/`POST`/`DELETE /v1/diskroles`)

Direct follow-up to Part 13's read-only enumeration, mirroring `devicemap.c`'s own persistence shape (ADR-0012 atomic-JSON) as planned -- but with the binding direction inverted: `devicemap` binds an operator-chosen *name* to a hardware *selector*; a disk already has a real, stable-enough kernel name of its own (`disk.h`'s `struct discovered_disk.name`), so this binds that real name directly to a *role* instead of inventing a second alias layer with nothing to alias.

The role vocabulary is a small, fixed, closed set (`container-storage`, `backup`) rather than an arbitrary operator-chosen string the way a `devicemap` name is -- a role is only meaningful insofar as a later phase (C: mount/format, D: container-storage migration) actually understands and acts on it, so an open vocabulary here would just be state nothing could ever interpret (No Stop-Gaps). `"swap"` is deliberately excluded: this project already has a real, working, dedicated on-demand host swap *file* mechanism (`daemon/src/swap.c`, ADR-0069) with its own REST surface; a disk-level "swap" role would either duplicate that mechanism or need reconciling with it, a real design question with no answer yet, so it's left out rather than added as a role nothing can act on.

New `daemon/src/diskrole.c`/`daemon/include/diskrole.h`: `diskrole_create()`/`diskrole_delete()`/`diskrole_lookup()`/`diskrole_write_json_list()`, keyed by disk name, persisted via the same `persist_atomic_write()`/`persist_read_file()` pair every other simple state module in this codebase already uses. Always rejects assigning a role to the one disk `disk.c`'s own `is_os_disk` flags (re-checked live via `disk_enumerate()`, not cached) -- the fixed OS-disk layout is never a role-assignment candidate, the one hard constraint this whole feature was scoped around from the start. Tolerant of a `disk_name` that isn't currently present (mirrors `devicemap`'s own tolerance for temporarily-absent hardware) -- `GET /v1/diskroles` reports `present` per entry, resolved fresh every call, never cached.

`GET`/`POST /v1/diskroles` + `DELETE /v1/diskroles/{disk_name}`, `kanxeoctl diskrole create/ls/rm`, documented in `openapi.yaml` (new `DiskRole`/`DiskRoleCreateRequest` schemas), `docs/api/README.md`, and `docs/guides/cli-reference.md`.

**A real, deliberate scope boundary confirmed, not silently left out**: `POST /system/backup`'s bundle does not include `devicemaps.json` today (a genuine, pre-existing gap, not introduced here), so `diskroles.json` following the exact same "not backed up" boundary is consistency with existing precedent, not a new regression -- fixing that gap for both modules together is a real, separate future decision, not bundled into this phase.

Verified live end to end: create/duplicate(`409`)/invalid-role(`400`)/list/delete/not-found(`404`) round-tripped correctly against a real disk name from Part A's own `GET /disks` output; a second real disk assigned a different role simultaneously; persisted state confirmed to survive a real daemon restart (`diskroles.json` loaded back correctly). The `is_os_disk` rejection path itself could not be live-tested in this dev sandbox (no disk here resolves as the OS disk, the same safe-degradation case Part A already documented) -- verified by code review and by the identical, already-proven `resolve_backing_device()`-style resolution logic Part A's own `is_os_disk` flag already uses successfully.

Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings); full local regression sweep (29 binaries, all passing).

## Part 15 (done): multi-disk management, Phase C -- destructive format + mount (`GET`/`POST /v1/disks/{disk_name}/format`)

Direct follow-up to Part 14's role assignment. Before writing any code, the user was asked (via a direct design question, given the real, hard-to-reverse risk) how formatting should be triggered -- automatically as part of role assignment, as a separate explicit action, or deferred entirely -- and chose the recommended, safest option: a **separate, explicit** format action, requiring the operator to confirm the target disk's name again in the request itself. Full reasoning in ADR-0071.

Two genuinely new design problems, neither present in Parts A/B or in any prior async job this daemon has built:

- **A process-tree shape with no exact precedent.** Every existing async host job (`pkg install`'s build container, ISO assembly, `pkg bootstrap --toolchain-url=`'s curl fetch) forks a child that immediately `execve()`s exactly one external binary. Format+mount needs two *sequential* steps -- an external `mkfs.ext4` invocation, then a raw `mount(2)` syscall -- and `execve()` irreversibly replaces its caller's process image, so there's no way to `execve()` for step one and still reach step two. `daemon/src/diskformat.c`'s forked child does NOT `execve()` itself; it internally `fork()`s a grandchild that does `execve()` `mkfs.ext4`, waits for it, and only on success calls `mount(2)` directly before exiting -- an extension of `pkg.c`'s own `start_fetch_for()` pattern (which already forks a grandchild per curl invocation for its sequential multi-source fetches), not a new pattern. A plain `fork()` (no `CLONE_NEWNS`) shares the parent's mount namespace, so the `mount(2)` becomes visible daemon-wide the instant it succeeds.
- **A real toolchain-staging gap**, found by tracing the dependency chain rather than assumed: a real installed Kanxeo box's control-plane squashfs (`image/src/mkbootroot.c`) never staged `mkfs.ext4`. Confirmed on this build host that `/usr/sbin/mkfs.ext4` is a symlink to the real binary `mke2fs`, with a real shared-library closure (`libext2fs.so.2`, `libblkid.so.1`, `libuuid.so.1`, `libe2p.so.2`, plus `libcom_err.so.2` already staged for curl's own krb5 dependency) -- `test_image_fixture_copy_file()`'s plain `open()`/`read()`/`write()` transparently follows the symlink, so staging it as `usr/sbin/mkfs.ext4` lands the real `mke2fs` content directly, no separate `mke2fs` copy needed.

New `daemon/src/diskformat.c`/`daemon/include/diskformat.h`: `diskformat_start()` validates the disk currently exists (`disk_enumerate()`), is not `is_os_disk` (re-checked live, same as Phase B), and already has a role assigned (`diskrole_lookup()` -- a disk with no expressed intent can never be formatted); creates the mount point (`<data-dir>/disks/<disk_name>`, deliberately never `CONTAINERS_DIR` itself -- moving container storage onto it is Phase D, still unbuilt); forks the two-step job above; hands `pid`/`pidfd` back to the caller. `daemon/src/main.c` owns the actual epoll registration/reap (`register_disk_format_pidfd()`/`handle_disk_format_event()`, new `CONN_DISK_FORMAT` conn kind), mirroring `register_iso_assemble_pidfd()`/`handle_iso_assemble_event()` exactly -- `diskformat.c` itself has no epoll/conn knowledge, the same split `pkg.c`'s own `start_fetch_for()` already establishes. Only one format job may run daemon-wide at a time (`409` otherwise), the same v1 single-job constraint every other async job here already has.

`POST /v1/disks/{disk_name}/format` requires a `confirm_disk_name` field in the body matching the URL's own disk name exactly (`400` otherwise) -- the deliberate double-confirmation the user's design choice calls for. Returns `202` immediately with the job's initial status; `GET` on the same path polls for completion (`state`: `none`/`running`/`ready`/`failed`, with `error` distinguishing an `mkfs.ext4` failure from a post-`mkfs` `mount(2)` failure). `kanxeoctl disks format NAME` / `disks format-status NAME`, matching the API 1:1 -- no separate interactive CLI confirmation prompt, matching this CLI's own existing `pki reset` precedent for destructive operations (the request-body confirmation is the safety gate). Documented in `openapi.yaml` (new `DiskFormatRequest`/`DiskFormatStatus` schemas, `/disks/{disk_name}/format` GET+POST paths), `docs/api/README.md`, and `docs/guides/cli-reference.md`.

**Honestly flagged, not glossed over**: this phase's actual `mkfs`/`mount` execution path could not be locally, empirically verified the way every other feature this project has built has been. This dev/build sandbox has no loop devices at all (`CLAUDE.md`'s own long-documented constraint) and every real block device visible here belongs to the shared underlying Proxmox host -- none are safe or authorized targets. The code was written and reviewed carefully against ADR-0071's own reasoning about the process tree and mount-namespace behavior, but real verification needs a genuinely spare disk on a real target box and has not yet happened as of this part landing.

Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings, `daemon/src/diskformat.c` now in `DAEMON_SRCS`); full local regression sweep (all daemon-linked tests run with `sudo`/unsandboxed per `CLAUDE.md`'s own documented requirement for container-creating tests, all passing -- `test_installer`/`test_boot_ab` failed only because `build/bzImage`, the separately-built kernel never reproduced by `make clean`, had been wiped by this same session's clean rebuild, an unrelated, expected gap, not a regression from this work).

## Part 16 (done): log message cap raised, build-output tail-capture, settable minimum severity (ADR-0072)

Raised directly by the user while diagnosing a real, live `lldap` build failure on 192.168.15.95: the consolidated log store's own `LOGSTORE_MSG_MAX` (512 bytes) silently truncated every entry, and `pkg.c`'s build-output capture (`PKG_BUILD_OUTPUT_CAPTURE_MAX`, 300 bytes) only ever read the *head* of a failed build's output via one bounded `read()` -- for any real build (confirmed: a Rust build printed 10+ `Compiling x` lines before the 512-byte wall), that combination guaranteed the actual error line was never captured at all, only early, unhelpful progress spam. Both bugs found and fixed in the same real deployment session that hit them, not deferred.

`LOGSTORE_MSG_MAX` raised 512 -> 4096 (a plain stack buffer, `vsnprintf()`-truncated regardless, no on-disk migration needed). `PKG_BUILD_OUTPUT_CAPTURE_MAX` raised 300 -> 3800, and the capture itself rewritten from "one bounded read" to "drain the whole pipe (the child has already exited, so this can never block) and keep only the TAIL via a fixed-size sliding window" -- the actual error is almost always the last thing printed, so this is the correct default, not an approximation.

New, persisted, write-time minimum-severity floor (`logstore_set_min_level()`/`logstore_min_level()`, `daemon/src/logstore.c`) directly answering the user's own "settable so we do not over-log": covers the real syslog severity scale (`emerg`..`debug`) kernel `dmesg` entries already carry, checked in `logstore_write()` itself before any segment-file I/O (not merely `GET`'s pre-existing `level` query filter, which only ever filters what's already stored). `PUT /v1/system/logs/config` gains an optional `min_level` field, independent of the existing `max_bytes` (either alone is a valid partial update, matching `daemon-config`'s own established convention); default `"debug"` (log everything) is a deliberate no-op for anyone who never touches the setting. `kanxeoctl logs config [--max-bytes=N] [--min-level=LEVEL]`.

Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings); full local regression sweep (23 daemon-linked/container test binaries, all passing, `sudo`/unsandboxed).
