`daemon/include/registry.h`: new `struct registry_entry` fields (`output_fd`, `capture_requested`, `captured_output[4096]`, `captured_output_len`) -- a per-container destination, distinct from `pkg.c`'s single global build-output buffer, since multiple ordinary containers can have capture enabled concurrently. `daemon/src/main.c`: `create_container_from_body()` parses the new `capture_output` boolean, sets up the pipe (`pipe2(O_CLOEXEC)` + `O_NONBLOCK` read end, `pkg_build_start()`'s exact pattern), closes the parent's own copy of the write end once `registry_create()` returns (clone3() already gave the child an independent fd-table copy), and on success wires `entry->output_fd`/`capture_requested` and calls new `register_container_output()`/`handle_container_output_event()` -- incremental epoll-driven drain mirroring `register_pkg_build_output()`/`handle_pkg_build_output_event()` (ADR-0087's discipline), appending into the bounded per-entry buffer instead of a shared global. `GET /v1/containers/{name}` gains a `captured_output` field: `null` if never requested, the captured text (possibly `""`) otherwise -- the two deliberately distinguishable.

**A real bug caught by a live test, not inspection**: the epoll dispatch loop's own kind-dispatch chain had no branch for the new `CONN_CONTAINER_OUTPUT` conn kind -- registered correctly with epoll, but its readable events were silently never handled, so `captured_output` simply never updated past empty. `test/test_container_lifecycle.c`'s new step 10 (a fresh `test/output_child.c` helper writing a known line to each of stdout/stderr, staged into the existing `lifecycletest` image) caught this immediately: the buffer stayed empty no matter how long the test polled. Fixed by adding the missing dispatch branch.

`docs/api/openapi.yaml`/`docs/api/README.md`: documented `capture_output` on `ContainerCreateRequest`, `captured_output` on the `Container` response schema, and a new "Diagnosing a container that starts but exits on its own" narrative section with a worked `jumpbox1`-style example, positioned right after the interactive-console section it complements (console needs the process still running; this needs it to have already produced output before exiting).

Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`) zero warnings across every binary. Full local regression sweep clean, including the new lifecycle test scenario. Deployed to 192.168.15.95 and used immediately -- see Part 71.

## Part 71 (done, verified live end-to-end): glauth's "listener mystery" resolved for real -- two unrelated causes, neither a Kanxeo or glauth defect (closes task #762, corrects Part 69's own note on task #747)

See [ADR-0113](../adr/0113-glauth-listener-mystery-resolved.md) for the full arc. Deployed Part 70's `capture_output` to 192.168.15.95 and immediately used it against a fresh `glauth-diag2` diagnostic container -- and it worked exactly as designed, but revealed something Part 69's own investigation never suspected: glauth's real log showed `LDAP server listening address=0.0.0.0:3893`, no error at all. A live TCP connect from off-box still got an immediate `Connection refused`.

**Root cause 1, found first**: `glauth-diag2` was created without an explicit IP, and `POST /v1/containers`' auto-allocation landed on `192.168.15.1` -- outside this project's own standing reserved range for `management`-network scratch containers (`192.168.15.101`-`109`), and, as the user immediately suspected on being shown the IP, a real device already on the physical LAN. Every "refused" connection was that device's own kernel correctly RSTing a SYN to a port nothing was listening on -- never reaching glauth at all. Recreating the identical container pinned to `192.168.15.103` connected on the first attempt. Confirmed via a real `ldapsearch` bind (`Invalid credentials` -- a genuine LDAP protocol response, not a network failure) that glauth had been completely healthy the entire time.

**Root cause 2, found immediately after via the same tool**: standing up the real, persistent `ldap-1`/`ldap-2` pair (mirroring `dns-1`/`dns-2`, registered via `POST /v1/ldap/servers`) and creating a real Kanxeo-managed user showed a second, genuine defect: `captured_output` read `could not start config-watcher error="function not implemented"` -- `ENOSYS` from `inotify_init()`. `CONFIG_INOTIFY_USER` had never been enabled in `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config`'s entire history (confirmed via `git log -p`), since nothing built before glauth ever needed live in-container filesystem-change notification. glauth's own `watchconfig = true` fsnotify watcher (ADR-0109) silently never started, so every live `POST`/`PUT /v1/ldap/users`|`/groups` write reached disk correctly but never reached an already-running glauth process -- only a fresh container start (reading the file once at its own boot) ever showed current data, a subtle, creation-order-dependent symptom.

Fixed by adding `CONFIG_INOTIFY_USER=y` to the kernel fragment and rebuilding from the project's own documented, reproducible recipe (cached source tree already on hand from a prior session's kernel work). Deployed via the established LAN-serve `system/update --kernel_path=` + reboot round-trip -- no ISO reinstall.

**Live-verified end to end, with no restart needed for the second half**: recreated `ldap-1` post-reboot (its own autostart didn't survive the reboot for an unrelated, not-yet-investigated reason -- recreated manually, `ldap-2` came back fine on its own), confirmed `captured_output` now shows a clean startup with no config-watcher error, then issued a real `POST /v1/ldap/users` with the container already running -- `captured_output` showed glauth's own `watcher got event`/`Config was reloaded` firing live, and a genuine `ldapsearch` bind with the new user's real password authenticated successfully (`Insufficient access` is glauth's own real authorization response for a user granted no search capability -- proof the bind itself succeeded).

No code changes to glauth or Kanxeo's own LDAP module -- both were already correct. The fix was entirely operational (diagnostic IP discipline) and kernel-configuration (a genuinely missing, previously-undiscovered Kconfig option).

## Part 72 (done, verified live end-to-end): real chrony NTP server, ntp-1/ntp-2 standing up permanently on 192.168.15.95 (closes task #763)

`chrony.recipe` (new) -- chrony 4.8, chrony's own canonical release tarball, built clean under `tcc` with a real local `./configure` + build verification first (this project's own standing discipline before publishing any recipe). Every optional crypto/privilege-drop backend explicitly disabled (`--without-nss/--without-nettle/--without-gnutls/--without-tomcrypt/--without-libcap/--without-seccomp --disable-nts`) -- none of NTS, libcap-based privilege dropping, or seccomp filtering are things this project's own per-container netns/mountns/pidns isolation needs a second layer of. `--with-pidfile=/run/chronyd.pid` matches this project's own established "no `/var/run`, only `/run`" convention (the same class of gap `dnsmasq.recipe` already hit). Only two binaries shipped (`chronyd`/`chronyc`); a real `ldd` confirms zero extra runtime dependencies beyond `libc.so.6`/`libm.so.6`, already staged by `pkg_seed_image_baseline()`.

**A second real, previously-undiscovered gap found via `capture_output` (not by inspection)**: `chronyd` exited immediately with `Fatal error : Could not get user/group ID of root`, despite being configured `--with-user=root --without-libcap` (i.e. `-PRIVDROP` in its own feature summary -- it never actually calls `setuid()`). `getpwnam("root")` still gets called unconditionally to resolve its own configured user, and this project's minimal images ship no `/etc/passwd` at all by default -- `pkg_seed_image_baseline()`'s general `libnss_files.so.2`/`nsswitch.conf` staging (ADR-0111) makes the lookup mechanism reachable but doesn't manufacture the record it needs to find. Fixed the same way any container-instance concern is handled here: staged a minimal `/etc/passwd`/`/etc/group` as ordinary `files[]` content alongside `chrony.conf`, not a `pkg_seed_image_baseline()` change (see the new CLAUDE.md environment-notes bullet for the general form of this gap).

Stood up `ntp-1`/`ntp-2` permanently (`restart: always`, pinned to `192.168.15.105`/`.106` -- the reserved range, learned the hard way in Part 71 above), each running `local stratum 10` (an orphan reference, appropriate for a LAN-internal time source with no real upstream internet dependency), registered via `POST /v1/ntp/servers`. **Live-verified end to end**: `POST /v1/system/ntp/sync` against each in turn both returned `state: "ok"` with the correct `synced_from` IP -- a genuine NTP wire-protocol round trip using Kanxeo's own already-tested SNTP client (task #751-753), not a bare TCP/process-alive check.

`docs/api/README.md`'s NTP section gained a full worked example (chrony container creation + the `/etc/passwd` gotcha); `CLAUDE.md`'s environment notes gained the generalized finding (any binary that looks up its own configured user by name, even one that will never change UID, needs a real `/etc/passwd`, not just a reachable NSS backend).

## Part 73 (done, verified live end-to-end): jump box SSH login working for real -- CONFIG_SECCOMP kernel fix, plus a real DELETE disk-cleanup gap found and fixed along the way (closes task #759)

`capture_output` (Part 70) found the real, final cause of every SSH login attempt against the jump box failing with `Connection reset by peer` mid-handshake, while `sshd`'s own parent process stayed `running` and healthy throughout: `ssh_sandbox_child: prctl(PR_SET_SECCOMP): Invalid argument [preauth]`. OpenSSH's own privilege-separation preauth child unconditionally tries to install a seccomp-BPF filter as a hardening measure and treats `EINVAL` as fatal -- this kernel had never enabled `CONFIG_SECCOMP` (`CONFIG_HAVE_ARCH_SECCOMP`/`_FILTER` were both already `y`, x86_64 always supports the feature, but the symbol itself was never listed). The fourth confirmed instance of this project's `allnoconfig`-starting kernel build silently disabling a `def_bool y` Kconfig symbol, after `CONFIG_VETH` and `CONFIG_INOTIFY_USER`/Part 71. Fixed by adding `CONFIG_SECCOMP=y`/`CONFIG_SECCOMP_FILTER=y` to `image/kernel/qemu-part1.config`, rebuilt and deployed via the same LAN-serve `system/update --kernel_path=` + reboot round-trip as every prior kernel-only fix. See [ADR-0114](../adr/0114-kernel-config-gap-config-seccomp.md).

**A second, unrelated real bug found immediately after redeploying**: a `restart:"always"` jump box container recreated under the same name (`jumpbox1`) across this session kept crash-looping forever with `sshd`'s own `Unable to load host key: bad permissions`, even after a fresh `DELETE` + recreate with the correct `mode: "0600"`. Traced to `handle_delete()`'s task #738/ADR-0106 disk cleanup being silently skipped whenever the container was caught mid-crash (no live registry entry at the moment of `DELETE` -- exactly the state a crash-looping container spends most of its time in), leaving a stale, wrong-permission upperdir file that `files[]` staging's own `open(O_CREAT|O_TRUNC)` can never fix on an already-existing file. Fixed by making `handle_delete()`'s disk cleanup and DNS/LDAP/PKI/NTP/SSH-target ownership-forgetting run unconditionally, resolving the disk-placement `disk_name` from the persisted definition's own stored body when no live registry entry exists. See [ADR-0115](../adr/0115-delete-crashed-container-disk-cleanup.md).

**Live-verified end to end** against 192.168.15.95, post-reboot: created a fresh jump box container, registered it via `POST /v1/ldap/ssh-targets` (ADR-0111, Part 68), and a real `ssh -i <client key> osakka@<ip> "id"` succeeded cleanly -- `uid=1000(osakka) gid=1000(users) groups=1000(users)`, no `Connection reset by peer` -- the LDAP-to-filesystem account sync and the kernel fix working together end to end for the first time. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`) + full regression sweep (`test_container_restart`, the test that specifically exercises the original task #738 scenario this change must not regress, plus 19 other daemon-linked tests) all pass.

## Part 74 (done, verified live end-to-end): full feature regression sweep on 192.168.15.95 -- found and fixed a real json.c write/parse asymmetry (closes task #760)

`kanxeoctl ps` (and `--json ps`) silently returned nothing partway through the sweep, against a real, healthy box (`ldap-1`/`ldap-2` both running), while `curl`'s raw fetch of the identical `GET /v1/containers` endpoint returned a complete, valid response (confirmed independently parseable by Python's own `json` module). Root cause: `daemon/src/json.c`'s writer (`jw_escaped_string()`) has always `\u00XX`-escaped control characters below `0x20` to stay valid JSON, but its own parser (`parse_string_raw()`) rejected every `\u` escape outright -- silently failing the *entire* surrounding document parse (`json_parse()` has no partial-success mode) the instant it hit one. `json.h`'s own header comment had explicitly scoped `\uXXXX` as unsupported "since no field in this project's API needs one" -- true when written, false since `capture_output` (Part 70) started relaying real stdout/stderr, and `ldap-1`/`ldap-2` run `glauth`, whose `zerolog`-based logging colorizes its own terminal output with real ANSI escape bytes. See [ADR-0116](../adr/0116-json-parser-u-escape-support.md).

Fixed by teaching `parse_string_raw()` to decode `\uXXXX` as a single byte (`0x00`-`0xFF`) -- deliberately still narrower than full RFC 8259 (no UTF-16 surrogate pairs), matching exactly what the writer side has ever produced rather than adding unneeded general Unicode support. `test/output_child.c` (the exec target `test_container_lifecycle.c`'s own `capture_output` test uses) now writes a real ANSI color escape into its stdout line, and the test asserts the raw byte survives the full write-then-parse round trip -- a genuine regression test that would have failed before this fix, closing the gap that let it ship unnoticed since `capture_output` first landed.

**Live-verified**: reproduced directly by extracting the real response body from 192.168.15.95 and feeding it to a throwaway harness linking `json.c` -- `NULL` before the fix, `PARSE OK` after; `kanxeoctl ps` run locally against the live box now correctly lists all 7 running containers (`dns-1`/`dns-2`/`ldap-1`/`ldap-2`/`ntp-1`/`ntp-2`/`jumpbox1`, all `restart:"always"` and stable). Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (23 tests) all pass, including the newly-extended `test_container_lifecycle`.

## Part 75 (done, verified live end-to-end): DNS record GET/PUT/DELETE now qualify a bare name, matching POST (closes task #760's own sweep, second fix)

A second real bug found immediately after: `kanxeoctl dns record rm sweeptest` 404'd right after `kanxeoctl dns record create --name=sweeptest ...` had just succeeded and `dns record ls` showed it present as `sweeptest.uk.home.arpa`. `handle_dns_record_create()` has always qualified a bare label via `siteconfig_qualify()` (ADR-0052) before storing it; `handle_dns_record_get_one()`/`handle_dns_record_update()` (PUT, task #749)/`handle_dns_record_delete()` never did the same on the read side, so only the exact FQDN a caller might not even know to reconstruct -- not the bare name just used to create the record -- could ever look it back up. See [ADR-0117](../adr/0117-dns-record-qualify-on-read.md).

Fixed by adding the same `siteconfig_qualify()` call to all three read-side handlers -- safe and idempotent for an already-qualified name too, since it only ever qualifies a label with no `.` in it. `test/test_dns.c`'s existing site-qualification scenario gained three new bare-name `GET`/`PUT`/`DELETE` assertions that would 404 before this fix and pass after. Checked for and confirmed absent elsewhere: neither LDAP user/group names nor PKI certificate names are ever site-qualified, so no equivalent fix applies to those CRUD surfaces.

**Live-verified**: `kanxeoctl dns record create --name=sweeptest ...` followed by `kanxeoctl dns record rm sweeptest` now succeeds against 192.168.15.95. Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (23 tests) all pass.

## Part 76 (done): console/exec 500 on 192.168.15.95 root-caused and fixed -- boot_init() never mounted /dev/pts (closes task #764)

Task #764, a same-day follow-up from task #760's sweep finding `kanxeoctl console` returning a bare 500 against every container on 192.168.15.95 while the identical `exec_into_container()` code path passed cleanly in the local `test_console_exec` test. Diagnosed in two steps: first, surfaced `exec_into_container()`'s own failure reason -- previously only visible in `kanxeod`'s own stderr, invisible on a real installed box with no host shell access -- into the HTTP response body and the console client's own error output; second, once the real reason ("No such file or directory") was visible, traced it to `boot_init()` (the real PID-1 boot path an already-installed box actually runs) never mounting `/dev/pts`. `posix_openpt()` always succeeds (`/dev/ptmx` is `devtmpfs`-populated unconditionally), but the PTY slave path it hands back only resolves to a real device once devpts is mounted -- and only `image/src/kanxeo-install.c`'s own installer-only `early_mounts()` (ADR-0042/task #406) ever did that. See [ADR-0118](../adr/0118-boot-init-devpts-mount.md).

Fixed by adding the identical devpts mount to `boot_init()` (same options as `kanxeo-install.c`'s own copy, fatal like every other essential boot mount) and preserving `exec_into_container()`'s real errno through its own ns-fd-open failure path (previously overwritten with a blanket `ESRCH` regardless of which of the four opens actually failed). Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (24 tests) all pass, including `test_boot` and `test_installer` run explicitly -- both exercise a real QEMU PID-1 boot through `boot_init()` itself and confirm the new mount doesn't regress boot.

**Live-verified**: deployed as `v1.8.6` via the established hostbuild+deploy+upgrade+reboot round-trip. `kanxeoctl console jumpbox1 --cmd=/usr/bin/id` against 192.168.15.95 now returns `uid=0(root) gid=0(root) groups=0(root)` cleanly instead of a bare 500, confirming both the root cause and the fix. `ntp-1`/`ntp-2` (the known, separate reboot-autostart gap) were restored afterward per the established recreate procedure.

## Part 77 (done): ntp-1/ntp-2 never surviving a reboot root-caused and fixed -- /run was overlay-persisted, not tmpfs

Direct follow-up to Part 76's restore step: the user asked why `ntp-1`/`ntp-2` never reliably survived a real reboot at all. Investigating found the crash-loop was live and reproducible on demand, not reboot-specific -- any respawn of a container under those exact names failed within milliseconds, every time, while an identical container under a fresh name stayed up cleanly. `GET /v1/containers/{name}/files --path=/run/chronyd.pid` confirmed the mechanism: the file contained the literal byte `1`. `chronyd -d` writes its own pidfile to `/run/chronyd.pid` and checks it on startup; since every container gets a fresh PID namespace, chronyd is always PID 1 inside it, so on any respawn it found its own stale pidfile naming a PID that trivially, always exists, and refused to start. `/run` had simply never been given the same "fresh on every start" treatment `src/mountns.c`'s `mountns_pivot()` already gives `/proc`/`/sys` -- it was just ordinary overlay-persisted storage, deliberately reused as-is across a crash/restart by `overlay.c`'s own `EEXIST`-tolerant upperdir design (meant for genuine in-progress workload state, not transient pidfiles). See [ADR-0119](../adr/0119-container-run-tmpfs.md).

Fixed generally, not with a `chrony.recipe` patch: `mountns_pivot()` now mounts a fresh, empty tmpfs at `/run` on every container start, unconditionally, closing this whole class of "assumes /run is cleared on start" bug for any current or future recipe, not just chronyd. Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (24 tests) all pass, including every test that exercises `mountns_pivot()` via a real `clone3()`+`pivot_root()`.

**Live-verified**: explicitly `DELETE`d the pre-existing, poisoned `ntp-1`/`ntp-2` disk state (one-time cleanup for state this session's own prior crash-loops had already left behind -- the fix prevents new poisoning, it doesn't retroactively clean up the old), deployed as `v1.8.7`, recreated both, and confirmed stable. Then rebooted 192.168.15.95 **twice** in succession, deliberately, to prove this wasn't a one-off: both times `ntp-1`/`ntp-2` autostarted cleanly via `containerdef_autostart_all()` with zero manual intervention -- the exact failure mode this task set out to fix, now closed.

## Part 78 (done): pkg/ redesign Part 1 -- recipe.sh -> build.sh rename, plus a real backup/restore regression fixed along the way

Kicks off a five-part `pkg/` redesign (task #739), reached through a long, deliberately thorough discussion covering package/image recipe sync from a configurable git repo, precompiled-artifact fetch-and-cache, a new image-recipe concept mirroring package recipes, and per-container rolling-update policy with restart jitter -- each landing as its own sequenced part, smallest and most foundational first, per this project's own convention. Part 1 itself: the user's own observation that `pkg/recipes/<name>/<version>/recipe.sh` says "recipe" twice for a tree holding nothing else. Grounding the redesign in the current code before touching anything surfaced a real, unrelated bug along the way: `do_system_backup()`/`do_system_restore()` still assumed the pre-ADR-0107 flat `<name>.recipe` layout, silently matching zero recipes since that migration's version-keyed directories landed. See [ADR-0120](../adr/0120-pkg-redesign-part1-naming-and-backup-fix.md).

Fixed: every recipe file (67, via `git mv`) renamed `recipe.sh` -> `build.sh`, with `pkg.c`/`pkg.h` and every living doc updated to match (the unrelated in-container `/build/recipe.sh` staging convention is untouched, on purpose -- a different thing entirely). `do_system_backup()` now walks the real two-level `<name>/<version>/build.sh` structure, keyed `"<name>/<version>"`; `do_system_restore()` reconstructs the same nested path. `test_system_backup.c` extended with a real recipe added before the backup call, asserting the exact key/content round-trips through both backup and restore -- this would have failed on the old code. Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (24 tests) all pass.

**Live-verified**: deployed to 192.168.15.95 as `v1.9.0`. Every real package recipe re-published under the new `build.sh` layout (the daemon's own live recipe store still had the old `recipe.sh` filename on disk; the ADR-0107 duplicate check keys off `build.sh` specifically, so this was a clean re-add, not a rejected duplicate) -- all 7 running containers (`dns-1`/`dns-2`/`ldap-1`/`ldap-2`/`ntp-1`/`ntp-2`/`jumpbox1`) survived the reboot untouched. `GET /v1/system/backup`'s `pkg_recipes` field now returns 67 real entries with real content, confirmed directly, closing the exact gap this fix targets.

## Part 79 (done): pkg/ redesign Part 2 -- configurable recipe repo + merge/additive `pkg sync`

Second of the five-part `pkg/` redesign (task #767). Design (repo config shape, gitea/github/gitlab as three explicit branches rather than one generic abstraction, merge/additive semantics, manual-first with optional periodic auto-sync) was worked through with the user across several rounds before implementation, then confirmed with "yes, this looks fine" -- see [ADR-0121](../adr/0121-pkg-redesign-part2-configurable-repo-and-sync.md) for the full reasoning.

Added: `GET`/`PUT /v1/pkg/repo-config` (partial-update semantics; the configured `auth_token` is write-only, never echoed back) and `POST`/`GET /v1/pkg/sync` (async fetch-and-merge of the configured repo's recipe tree, `daemon/src/pkg.c`'s `pkg_sync_start()`/`pkg_sync_completed()` -- a direct structural clone of `CONN_BOOTSTRAP_FETCH`'s own fork+curl+pidfd async-fetch pattern, ADR-0065). Merge/additive is not new merge logic -- it's the existing `pkg_recipe_add()`'s own duplicate-(name,version) rejection (ADR-0107), so a sync can never overwrite or discard a recipe a host already has, only add ones it's missing. A periodic re-arming timerfd (`arm_pkg_sync_periodic_timer()`) mirrors NTP's own pattern (task #751) exactly -- `sync_interval_seconds=0`, the default, means periodic sync stays off and only the manual `POST` runs. `kanxeoctl pkg repo-config show|set`, `pkg sync [--wait]`, `pkg sync-status`.

Verified: new `test/test_pkg_sync.c` -- a real, unmocked round trip against a `python3 -m http.server` standing in for a gitea instance (serving a hand-built tarball at the exact path the daemon's own gitea-branch URL construction computes; the real `curl` subprocess and real `tar` extraction genuinely run, only the forge itself is a stand-in). Covers unconfigured-400, `PUT` partial-update semantics (a later PUT touching only `sync_interval_seconds` leaves `repo_url`/token untouched), explicit token clear, a first sync (`added=1, skipped=0`), a second sync of the identical repo proving merge/additive semantics (`added=0, skipped=1` -- not rejected, not duplicated), and a real fetch failure against a nonexistent path (`state=failed` with a real, non-empty error). Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (25 tests) all pass. github/gitlab fetch branches are real code following each forge's own documented API, but are explicitly flagged as unverified against a live github.com/gitlab.com account -- only gitea (`git.home.arpa`) is reachable from this project's own dev/test environment.

## Part 80 (done): pkg/ redesign Part 3 -- local build-artifact cache + plain-HTTP precompiled-artifact server

Third of the five-part `pkg/` redesign (task #768). A real mid-implementation course-correction, caught directly by the user: the first draft reused Part 2's own git-forge repo-sync mechanism for precompiled artifacts too (walking a `pkg/artifacts/` tree inside the same repo tarball recipes come from) -- which would mean checking compiled binaries into the same git tree as recipes to make them fetchable, exactly the "source contaminated with compiled output" problem this whole redesign exists to avoid. Fixed before any of that code shipped, and the user's own follow-up framing ("a three-way validation") became the actual design: two genuinely separate, independently-configured things -- recipes (git, Part 2) and precompiled artifacts (plain HTTP, never git) -- with the recipe's own git-tracked checksum as the sole reason a fetched artifact is ever trusted. See [ADR-0122](../adr/0122-pkg-redesign-part3-artifact-cache-and-server.md).

Added: a local build-artifact cache (`daemon/src/pkg.c`'s `pkg_cache_*`, `<data-dir>/pkg/cache/<name>-<version>.tar.gz`) populated automatically by every real build, with real LRU eviction (oldest-by-mtime first) under a single, always-enforced size cap (`GET`/`PUT /v1/pkg/cache-config`, default 2 GiB, no "unlimited" mode -- `GET`/`DELETE /v1/pkg/cache` for occupancy/clear). A cache hit for a second install of the identical `(name, version)` -- into a different image, or after a delete -- skips both the network fetch and the compile. The key implementation decision: rather than threading a new short-circuit through the existing async fetch→build→merge state machine (real risk to a heavily-relied-on pipeline, rejected under this project's own "No Regressions" maxim), a cache hit still spawns a real, unmodified build container -- `pkg_fetch_completed()` just pre-populates its own upperdir from the cache and gives it a `:` no-op command instead of the recipe's real build/install, so every downstream step (image merge, dependency-queue chaining, upgrade tracking) runs completely unchanged for both cases. Also added: an optional new recipe field `pkg_artifact_sha256=` (a recipe opts in explicitly; every existing recipe is unaffected) and a second, deliberately non-git-forge-aware server config (`GET`/`PUT /v1/pkg/artifact-config`, plain `base_url` + optional bearer token) -- when both are set, an install tries `<base_url>/<name>-<version>.tar.gz` first, verifies it against the recipe's own declared checksum (the identical verify-before-trust primitive `pkg_source`/`pkg_sha256` already use) before ever trusting it, and silently falls back to a real source build on any miss. `kanxeoctl pkg cache-config show|set`, `pkg cache-status`, `pkg cache-clear`, `pkg artifact-config show|set`. Also added, closing a gap that had been open since Part 2: web dashboard UI for both Part 2's repo-config/sync (a new "Repo & Sync" Packages sub-page) and this part's cache-config/status/clear + artifact-config (a new "Cache & Artifacts" Packages sub-page) -- both had been REST+CLI-only, per the user's own explicit instruction that every daemon-config surface needs all three of REST, CLI, and web.

Verified: new `test/test_pkg_cache.c` -- real gcc compiles, real curl fetches, a real `python3 http.server` standing in for the artifact server. Proves a genuine cache hit (by deleting the real source tarball before the second install, so a real re-fetch is physically impossible), LRU eviction under an artificially tiny cap (confirmed the evicted entry's own cache file is actually gone from disk afterward), cache-config/cache-clear, and the full artifact-tier round trip (a recipe with a real declared checksum and a deliberately-broken source URL still installs successfully, then shows up promoted into the local cache). Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (27 tests) all pass, including the pre-existing `test_pkg.c` (multi-source recipes, dependency chains, hostbuild, upgrades) completely unaffected when neither tier applies. Web dashboard verified by fetching the served `app.js`/`index.html` from a live scratch daemon and confirming they match the on-disk source byte-for-byte, plus exercising every new REST call directly.

## Part 81 (done): pkg/ redesign Part 4 -- declarative image recipes + whole-rootfs artifact fast path

Fourth of the five-part `pkg/` redesign (task #769). Two real design questions were resolved with the user before writing code, both surfaced by reading the existing image machinery directly rather than assumed: (1) editing an image's manifest today never triggers a build (`PUT /v1/images/{name}/manifest` only declares intent -- realizing it still needs real `pkg install` calls), so "apply a recipe" would either need genuinely new multi-package install sequencing or stay a pure bulk-declare; confirmed v1 scope is bulk-declare only, since a `rolling` entry has no single deterministic content an artifact checksum could ever validly describe anyway. (2) where the async whole-rootfs artifact fetch job should live -- `pkg.c` already has three separate instances of the same fork+curl+pidfd idiom (hostbuild fetch, package-install fetch, Part 2's repo-sync fetch), so a fourth small instance (not threaded through package-install's own container-spawning state machine, which has nothing in common with "curl a file, verify it") was the confirmed choice, still gated by the same `g_current_job_name` single-job guard so it can never race a real package install over the same shared state. See [ADR-0123](../adr/0123-pkg-redesign-part4-image-recipes-and-artifact.md).

Added: a name-keyed image recipe (`daemon/src/pkg.c`'s `image_recipe_*`, `<data-dir>/pkg/image-recipes/<name>.recipe`, recipe name == image name) declaring `image_packages="name:mode:version ..."` and an optional `image_artifact_sha256=`. `GET`/`POST /v1/images/recipes`, `GET`/`DELETE /v1/images/recipes/{name}`. `POST /v1/images/{name}/apply-recipe`: the common case bulk-declares the manifest synchronously (204, `image_manifest_set()` per entry, no rootfs touched); a fully-pinned recipe with a declared checksum and a configured plain-HTTP artifact server (Part 3's `pkg_artifact_*` config, reused as-is under a new `images/` URL prefix so package and image artifacts never collide in one server namespace) instead fetches one whole-rootfs tarball asynchronously (202) at a URL computed from the recipe's own entries via the identical `image_hash_manifest_string()` a real build would use, verifies it against the recipe's own checksum, extracts it directly as the new version's entire rootfs (no copy-forward staging needed -- this content fully replaces rather than builds on a prior version), records it, declares the manifest, and mirrors `g_packages[]` so `GET /v1/pkg` matches the rootfs that was actually written (per-package file lists left empty, the same accepted gap hostbuild packages already have). Any miss leaves the image completely untouched. `GET /v1/images/recipe-apply-status`. `kanxeoctl image recipe add|show|rm|ls`, `image apply-recipe NAME`, `image recipe-apply-status`.

Verified: new `test/test_image_recipe.c` -- a fresh recipe list is empty; a bulk-declare recipe applies synchronously and `current_version` is confirmed unchanged (proving no rootfs was touched); applying with no stored recipe is 404; a malformed entry is rejected (400); a fully-pinned recipe with a real declared checksum and a real `python3 http.server` standing in for the artifact server applies asynchronously, reaches `state=success`, and `current_version` matches an independently-precomputed hash the test derived from the recipe text alone (proving the daemon requested exactly the predicted URL); the manifest, `g_packages[]`, and the real extracted file on disk are all confirmed. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (28 tests) all pass, including the pre-existing `test_images.c` and `test_pkg.c` (manifest editing, dependency chains, hostbuild, upgrades -- the real-install path is completely unmodified).

## Part 82 (done): pkg/ redesign Part 5 -- rolling containers + restart jitter

Fifth and final of the five-part `pkg/` redesign (task #770), closing out task #739. Two design questions resolved with the user via direct discussion before writing code: (1) which running containers should auto-restart onto a newer rolling-image version -- an unconditional auto-restart was rejected outright given a rolling image's own accepted non-determinism (ADR-0107), confirmed instead as opt-in per container (`follow_rolling`, set only by an explicit flag on the create request, mirroring `restart_policy`/`depends_on`'s own "parsed once, cached in `struct container_def`" shape); (2) how to spread out simultaneous restarts when many containers follow the same rolling image -- confirmed as a new configurable jitter window (`GET`/`PUT /v1/system/rolling-config`, `jitter_window_seconds`, default 60, 0-3600, mirroring Part 3's own cache/artifact config resource shape). See [ADR-0124](../adr/0124-pkg-redesign-part5-rolling-containers-and-restart-jitter.md).

Added: `apply_rolling_container_restarts()` (`daemon/src/main.c`), run from the same "after every completed pkg job" trigger point ADR-0107's own rolling-rebuild reconciliation already uses -- for every `follow_rolling` container, compares its persisted `image_version` pin against its image's real `current_version`; on drift, `containerdef_patch_image_version()` (raw string surgery on the already-spliced body, mirroring `handle_create()`'s own splice posture) patches the pin before a jittered restart is armed, so the eventual replay via `create_container_from_body()` automatically picks up the new version. A new, deliberately separate timer idiom (`CONN_ROLLING_RESTART_TIMER`) rather than reusing `CONN_RESTART_TIMER`, since this case must actively tear down a still-live container first (not just respawn an already-exited one). `rolling_jitter_seconds()` mirrors `ldap_generate_secret()`'s own `/dev/urandom` idiom -- the only other randomness precedent in this codebase. `--follow-rolling` on `kanxeoctl container run`, `rolling-config show|set`, and web dashboard support (create-modal checkbox, container-detail fields, a Daemon-page jitter-window form).

Found and fixed along the way, by the new test: `timerfd_settime(2)`'s documented semantics mean an all-zero `it_value` disarms a timer rather than firing it immediately -- `jitter_window_seconds=0` (an intentional "no jitter, restart now" setting, unlike `restart_delay_seconds` which is always validated 1-300) hit this exactly, silently leaving the restart timer disarmed. Fixed in `arm_rolling_restart_timer()` with a 1-nanosecond floor when the computed delay is `<= 0`.

Verified: new `test/test_rolling_restart.c` -- stages a real dynamically-linked test binary as a synthetic package's install payload (sidesteps needing a working compiler inside the test's own build sandbox for something that isn't about compilation), bootstraps the toolchain sandbox, marks the package rolling, creates one `follow_rolling` container and one plain container pinned to the same image version, publishes a new package version purely through the real recipe API (never pre-written to disk, since the daemon correctly 409s an already-published version), and confirms the `follow_rolling` container's PID and pin both update while the plain container stays completely untouched; also exercises the rolling-config GET/PUT including range rejection. This is the test that caught the timerfd bug above. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (all daemon-linked and standalone tests) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 83 (done): pkg_delete() failed-state removal + per-container follow_rolling jitter override

Two further Part 5 follow-ups (task #739), both user-requested after the prior session's own live verification confirmed Part 5's core mechanism working but surfaced these two real gaps. See [ADR-0125](../adr/0125-pkg-delete-failed-state-and-jitter-override.md).

`pkg_delete()`'s own state guard only ever accepted `PKG_STATE_INSTALLED`, leaving a permanently-`PKG_STATE_FAILED` package entry (a bad checksum, a broken recipe, a network hiccup with no retry) stuck forever -- indistinguishable from an unknown name from the outside, both 404. Confirmed by tracing every `PKG_STATE_FAILED` transition in `daemon/src/pkg.c`: a failed entry's own `e->files` is either empty (the common case -- failure before the merge step ever runs) or, in the one path that calls `image_produce_new_version()` before deciding FAILED, only ever describes content in a scratch staging directory that function has already discarded on its own failure path (confirmed reading its own implementation -- every failure branch calls `persist_remove_tree(staging)` before returning, never committing it). Either way, nothing was ever really merged into a real, persisted image version. Fixed: a FAILED entry is now cleared directly (`pkg_entry_free_files()` then the slot memset), never through `image_produce_new_version()`/`delete_mutate()` (the INSTALLED path's own unlink-and-produce-a-new-version mechanism, unchanged) -- there's nothing to unlink and no new version to produce.

Added: `follow_rolling_jitter_seconds`, an optional per-container override (0-3600, same range `PUT /system/rolling-config` enforces) of the daemon-wide jitter window, set on `POST /v1/containers`. `struct container_def` gains `has_follow_rolling_jitter`/`follow_rolling_jitter_seconds` (mirrors `has_readiness`'s own "has-flag plus value" shape); `apply_rolling_container_restarts()` resolves `def->has_follow_rolling_jitter ? def->follow_rolling_jitter_seconds : containerdef_jitter_window_get()` per container rather than always reading the daemon-wide default. Persisted and migrated the same way `follow_rolling` itself was (absent/null both mean "no override," covering both a pre-existing `container_defs.json` and this daemon's own `save_state()` writing `null` for an unset override) and surfaced (nullable) in every container response. `--follow-rolling-jitter-seconds=N` on `kanxeoctl container run`, a `jitter=` column in `ps`/`inspect`; web dashboard gets a matching override field on both the create modal and the container-detail Options tab.

Verified: `test/test_pkg.c` deletes a checksum-mismatch install (`badsum`) after it reaches `failed`, confirms `204`/subsequent-`404`, and confirms the base image's own `current_version` is completely unchanged by the removal. `test/test_rolling_restart.c` proves the override round-trips (create response and `GET` both echo `follow_rolling_jitter_seconds:0`, not null) and, more importantly, actually takes precedence: the daemon-wide default is cranked to its own 3600s maximum, a new container gets an explicit `0` override, a second rolling rebuild is triggered, and the container is confirmed to restart within a ~10s poll window -- a daemon-default-only container has under a 0.3% chance of coincidentally restarting that fast, so this is real, not just plausible, proof the per-container value was actually consulted. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (all daemon-linked and standalone tests) confirm zero regressions; `test_rolling_restart` re-run 3x consecutively given its one timing-sensitive assertion.

## Part 84 (done): logging/web-UI epic Part 1 -- transparent container log capture into the consolidated log store

First of a multi-part logging/web-UI epic (user-requested: transparent container log aggregation with regex/level/container filters feeding a common backend; optional redundant `syslog-1`/`syslog-2` forward containers; several other web dashboard changes tracked as later parts of the same epic). Two design questions -- where container logs should live, and how a non-API syslog receiver fits this project's "100% API driven" mandate -- were worked through with the user before any code was written, both resolved in favor of extending the existing consolidated log store (`logstore.c`, ADR-0070) as the one source of truth, with syslog forwarding deferred to a later part as a purely optional, non-authoritative external-tooling target. See [ADR-0126](../adr/0126-transparent-container-log-capture.md).

Every container's stdout/stderr is now captured unconditionally (previously only when a client opted in via `capture_output:true`, which fed a separate, narrow 4KB-tail mechanism, ADR-0112) and forwarded line-by-line into the log store, tagged with the container's own name. The pre-existing `capture_output` opt-in still exists, narrowed to controlling only that separate `captured_output` tail -- fully backward compatible, confirmed unchanged behavior for existing callers. `GET /v1/system/logs` gained `container=` and `regex=` (POSIX extended, case-insensitive) filters, both validated (a malformed regex is a clean `400`, not a crash or silent empty result).

Two real, unrelated things were found and fixed along the way: a live console-spam bug (`client_conn_advance_handshake()` dumping the full OpenSSL error queue to the physical/serial console on every untrusted-cert HTTPS handshake attempt, now a single logged line instead) and a TCC/glibc `<regex.h>` parsing incompatibility (a C99 VLA-in-prototype construct TCC's parser rejects; fixed cleanly via the standard `__STDC_NO_VLA__` feature-test macro the header already branches on, no hand-redeclaration needed).

Verified: `test/test_container_lifecycle.c` extended to prove capture is genuinely unconditional (a container created *without* `capture_output` still has its real stdout line discoverable via the new filters), plus filter-miss and malformed-regex-400 checks. Manual live round-trip against a scratch daemon confirmed both stdout/stderr capture and full regression parity with the pre-existing `capture_output` mechanism. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (all daemon-linked and standalone tests) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 85 (done): logging/web-UI epic Part 2 -- optional syslog-1/syslog-2 forward targets

Second of the logging/web-UI epic. The consolidated log store stays the one source of truth the REST API and web UI ever read from -- `syslog-1`/`syslog-2` (or any operator-chosen name) are an optional, redundant forward target for real external syslog tooling, never a second store the platform itself depends on, resolving directly from the API-First Mandate (a syslog receiver has no REST API of its own to be authoritative through). See [ADR-0127](../adr/0127-syslog-forward-targets.md).

`POST`/`GET`/`DELETE /v1/syslog/targets` registers a running container as a forward target, mirroring the existing NTP server-registration mechanism (pure bookkeeping, live IP resolved fresh at every send, forgotten automatically on container delete). Every container-sourced log line captured by Part 1's own transparent mechanism is now also sent, fire-and-forget, as a real RFC 3164 UDP datagram to every registered target -- container-sourced entries only, matching the user's own "all the logs for the containers" scoping; kernel/kanxeod/audit entries are not forwarded. A new `sysklogd` recipe (a real, unmodified upstream syslogd) is the reference receiver, proving the design against real external tooling rather than only this daemon's own sender code.

Verified: `test/test_syslogfwd.c` covers the registration bookkeeping (mirroring the existing NTP test's own coverage) plus a genuine wire-level proof -- a real receiver container (a small new fixture binding real UDP `:514` inside its own network namespace) confirmed, via its own transparently-captured stdout, to have received a well-formed RFC 3164 datagram from a real sender container, naming the sender correctly and carrying its message verbatim. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (37 test binaries) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 86 (done): logging/web-UI epic Part 5 -- __host owner sentinel for PKI cert / DNS record

Small, user-requested clarity fix: the daemon's own auto-issued `"host"` PKI leaf and auto-maintained instance DNS record now carry a reserved `"__host"` owner sentinel instead of `null`, distinguishing "owned by the daemon itself" from "no owner for any other reason." See [ADR-0128](../adr/0128-host-owner-sentinel.md). Rendered as "host (this daemon)" in both the CLI and web dashboard; the REST API itself still returns the raw sentinel string.

Verified: `test_pki.c`'s existing site-config-triggered reissue assertion updated to expect the new sentinel. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (37 test binaries) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 87 (done): logging/web-UI epic Part 3 -- merged bottom log panel + toast notifications

User-requested web dashboard reorganization: browsing the consolidated log moved out of its tree page and into the dashboard's own always-visible bottom panel, merged with the panel's pre-existing client-side "web UI activity" stream into one filtered view; the status bar became a fixed-position, auto-dismissing toast that also lands in that same merged panel. See [ADR-0129](../adr/0129-merged-log-panel-and-toast.md).

The bottom log panel already existed (logging every mutating REST request the dashboard itself made, client-side only) -- extended rather than duplicated, per this project's own "No Parallel Implementations" maxim, to also poll in the server's own kernel/kanxeod/audit/container log entries, with a new source-filter dropdown in the panel's own header. The Logs page under the tree now configures only (the size cap); browsing lives in the panel. Two stale documentation claims left over from before Part 1 shipped (ADR-0126) -- "no log retrieval endpoint"/"no log viewer" -- were also found and corrected while touching these docs.

Verified: `node --check` (this project's closest thing to a compile check for its own build-step-free vanilla JS, ADR-0010). Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (37 test binaries, no daemon-side code changed) confirm zero regressions. Served `app.js`/`index.html` confirmed byte-for-byte identical to source from a live scratch daemon.

## Part 88 (done): logging/web-UI epic Part 4 -- host stats graphs page

`GET /v1/system/stats` (ADR-0073) has existed in the API since that phase but had zero web dashboard presence until now -- closed with a new System > Server > Host Stats page, four live graphs (CPU/memory/disk/network) reusing the container Stats tab's own hand-rolled canvas chart renderer, plus a load-average text line. See [ADR-0130](../adr/0130-host-stats-graphs-page.md).

Host-side CPU% needed a genuinely different computation from the container case (raw `/proc/stat` jiffies and the classic idle-delta formula, vs. a cgroup's own cumulative usage counter) -- not a copy-paste. Network is every real interface summed together, honestly labeled "all interfaces combined" since it necessarily includes loopback and every container's own veth traffic alongside real external I/O.

Verified: `node --check`, response field names cross-checked directly against `daemon/src/main.c`'s own JSON output (not just the OpenAPI doc). Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (37 test binaries, no daemon-side code changed) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 89 (done): logging/web-UI epic Part 6 (final) -- host process list + kill

Closes the logging/web-UI epic (Parts 1-6, ADR-0126 through ADR-0131). `GET`/`DELETE /v1/system/processes` -- a real, direct `/proc` scan of every process on the box, each correlated to a container (if any) by walking its own real host ppid chain against the registry's own known container root pids, rather than a pid-namespace comparison (every container's own init is a direct `clone3()` child of `kanxeod`, so the ppid walk gives the same answer more cheaply). Kill is a real, immediate SIGKILL, refusing only pid 1 and this daemon's own real pid -- a pid belonging to a running container is allowed, since killing it is exactly equivalent to that container crashing on its own and the existing exit handling already covers it. See [ADR-0131](../adr/0131-host-process-list-and-kill.md).

New `kanxeoctl process ls`/`process kill PID` CLI surface, and a new System > Server > Processes web dashboard page (fetch-on-demand, container values linking to that container's own detail page, Kill guarded by a confirm() dialog matching this dashboard's own destructive-action convention).

Verified: new `test/test_hostproc.c` confirms the daemon's own real pid is listed correctly, a real running container's own process is correlated to it by name, all kill-validation edge cases (pid 1, this daemon's own pid, nonexistent, non-numeric) are exercised over real HTTP, and a real disposable process is confirmed to actually die when killed via the API. Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (38 test binaries) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 90 (done): real-world fixes after the logging epic -- log panel fixed size/perf, dynamic shell prompt, CLI polish

Direct user feedback after using the logging/web-UI epic (Parts 1-6, ADR-0126-0131) for real. See [ADR-0132](../adr/0132-log-panel-fixes-and-cli-polish.md).

The log panel growing instead of staying fixed-size and the dashboard feeling slower turned out to share one root cause chain: a missing `min-height: 0` (a classic flexbox gotcha) let accumulated log entries spill past the panel's fixed height instead of scrolling inside it, and every rendered entry forced its own synchronous browser reflow -- costly once the daemon's own per-request audit log started including the dashboard's own routine ~30-request-per-2s polling cycle (`GET /system/logs` itself became one of those 30 once the merged log panel, ADR-0129, started polling it). Fixed on both sides: the audit log's `GET /health`-only exclusion generalized to every `GET` (a query is never an action; mutations are unaffected), and the client now batches a whole poll's worth of new entries into one DOM update instead of one reflow per entry.

A second, separately-reported issue (a `pkg sync` DNS failure even after adding the needed record) turned out to be a real, confirmed daemon bug: `PUT /v1/system/resolv` writes to a file bind-mounted onto the real `/etc/resolv.conf` at boot, but the write went through a rename-based helper -- and a bind mount binds to the inode, not the path, so the rename silently detached the mount from every write after the first post-boot one. `GET` kept reporting the new config correctly; the box's real outbound `curl` silently kept using the stale one until the next reboot. Fixed with an in-place write (same inode throughout), matching the module's own already-documented "live, no reboot needed" contract.

Also: the interactive shell prompt now reflects the connected daemon's own `instance_name` instead of a fixed literal; `process ls` restyled to match `ps`'s own established output convention; a new `container ls` command added as a noun-based synonym for `ps`, matching every other resource command's own `<noun> <verb>` shape, without touching the already-established Docker-familiar container verbs.

Verified: full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (38 test binaries, no test asserted on GET requests in the audit log) confirm zero regressions. A real PTY-driven shell session confirmed the dynamic prompt live.

## Part 91 (done): lenient pkg repo_url parsing -- accepts a real forge browse URL

Continuing the same real-world feedback round as Part 90: once the resolv fix (ADR-0132) closed the DNS layer of a `pkg sync` failure, retrying surfaced a second, genuinely different bug underneath -- `parse_repo_url()` split `owner`/`repo` at the *last* slash in the configured `repo_url`, so a real gitea browse URL (exactly what a browser address bar shows while looking at a repo) silently mis-parsed into a garbage owner and an opaque 404, with no hint the URL itself was the problem. See [ADR-0133](../adr/0133-lenient-repo-url-parsing.md).

Fixed by taking only the first two path segments as owner/repo and discarding anything after -- a bare `owner/repo` and a full browse-URL suffix now resolve identically and correctly, closing the single most likely real-world paste mistake for this field.

Verified: a new test scenario re-points at the same stand-in repo already proven reachable via a bare URL, this time through one with a browse-style suffix appended, and confirms `pkg sync` resolves it to the same repo rather than failing or mis-adding. Full clean rebuild + full regression sweep (38 test binaries) confirm zero regressions. Live-verified on 192.168.15.95: the user's own originally-configured browse-URL-style `repo_url` now syncs successfully.

## Part 92 (done): web dashboard surface for syslog forward targets (closing an ADR-0127 gap)

User-reported gap: Part 2 of the logging epic (ADR-0127) shipped the REST API and CLI for registering syslog forward targets, but never got a web dashboard surface -- a real miss against this project's own "every capability needs REST + CLI + web" convention.

New System > Server > Syslog Targets page (a registered-target list, mirroring the existing NTP Servers page exactly) plus a "Syslog Target" entry in the header's `+ Create` dropdown, alongside every other "register a container as X" form. Folded into the existing 2s poll cycle.

Verified: `node --check`, served files confirmed byte-identical to source, `GET /v1/syslog/targets` response shape confirmed to match what the new UI reads. Full regression sweep (38 test binaries, no daemon-side code changed) confirms zero regressions.

## Part 93 (done): full documentation audit -- taxonomy compliance, accuracy fixes, three new guides, architecture.svg redrawn for real

A second full, user-requested documentation audit (the first was Phase 30 parts 5-6) -- "fine tooth pick" accuracy across every document, explicit priority on the API docs, a formal task list tracked through `TaskCreate`/`TaskUpdate` one item per phase. The existing taxonomy (`docs/README.md`'s own "Taxonomy and naming" section, `CLAUDE.md`'s Documentation Map) was already sound and confirmed compliant (nothing but `README.md` in `docs/`'s own root, one subdirectory per document kind) -- this was a compliance and drift-correction pass against it, not a redesign, plus closing real content gaps.

**CHANGELOG.md**: 6 real newest-first ordering violations found (blame-timestamp-verified, not just guessed) and fixed -- e.g. Part 85 sitting above Part 90, Part 79 above Part 80. A 7th flagged candidate was a false positive from naive integer parsing of "Part 0.5"; blame confirmed it was already correctly placed. The intro paragraph's hand-listed tag range (only `v1.0.0`-`v1.5.0` out of 36 real tags) was replaced with a statement of the rule ("`git tag` is the ground truth") instead of a copy that will drift again by construction.

**Root README.md**: removed a "40 phases shipped" line that directly violated `CLAUDE.md`'s own Documentation Map rule (root README never carries phase-status detail) -- and had already drifted, same as the rule's own reasoning predicted.

**ROADMAP.md**: retired the 12-row Phase 0-11 summary table (never extended past the original charter, while 48 Phase + 95 Part headings existed below it) in favor of a pointer to the section headings themselves as the living source of truth. Separately, this same audit's own link-resolution sweep found the Part 83-92 tail was itself jumbled in exactly the pattern CHANGELOG's Part 1 fix had just corrected there (evidently carried over from CHANGELOG at the time) -- reordered to continue the file's own dominant ascending convention.

**docs/api/README.md**: a background parity audit confirmed `openapi.yaml`<->`dispatch()` already has 100% parity (139/139) -- no schema gaps. But the file's own "Endpoints at a glance" quick-reference table was a separate, real gap: 9 already-prose-documented endpoints missing from it entirely (`POST`/`DELETE /system/routes`, `GET`/`POST`/`DELETE /system/swap`, `GET /system/logs`, `GET`/`PUT /system/logs/config`, `GET /pkg/build/log`) -- fixed, now 141/141 verified by script. Documented that `/containers/{name}/console` and `/pkg/build/log` are WebSocket-upgrade endpoints served outside `dispatch()` entirely, so a future `dispatch()`-only parity audit doesn't misreport them as missing.

**docs/guides/cli-reference.md**: a second background audit found real, concrete gaps -- five entire commands undocumented (`rolling-config`, `iso`, `image recipe` subgroup, `image apply-recipe`, `image recipe-apply-status`), four missing `run` flags (`--follow-rolling`, `--follow-rolling-jitter-seconds=`, `--file=`, `--sysctl=`), and a false blanket claim ("`--json`... every subcommand supports it" -- untrue for `console`/`files get`, both confirmed in code to silently ignore it). `web-dashboard.md` spot-check found the Image detail view actually has five tabs, not four (an undocumented Recipe tab) and an undocumented "Rolling restart jitter" section on the Daemon page. `installing.md` gained a cross-link to the self-hosted, REST-driven ISO build path it never mentioned.

**Three new guides** (a real content gap, not previously scoped): `administration.md` (monitoring, backup/restore, disk management), `networking.md` (networks, VLAN/physical-NIC attachment, routing), `security.md` (PKI, HTTPS, LDAP-backed accounts) -- sourced strictly from already-shipped, already-documented capability in `docs/api/README.md`, cross-checked command-by-command against the CLI's own flag parsing. Caught and fixed one wrong ADR citation while drafting (`run --interface=` is ADR-0022, not ADR-0028 as first written). Wired into `docs/guides/README.md`'s index (now grouped Getting Started / Operating / Extending) and root `README.md`'s own "Getting started" list.

**architecture.svg**: found to be far more stale than originally scoped -- headed "as of Phase 40" with none of the LDAP, NTP, pkg/ redesign, disk-quota/role, or logging-epic work drawn at all. Given a direct choice between a minimal 4-box patch and a full redraw, chose the full redraw (user's own call): kanxeod's capability row grows from 8 cards in one row to 10 in two (adding LDAP, NTP, and consolidating Disks into Device, Processes into Stats, Console into Logs to fit), Persistent State gains a log-store entry, footer notes rewritten to summarize every era of work since Phase 40. Verified via `rsvg-convert` render + visual inspection at full and cropped resolution, per this project's own established discipline for this file.

**Final sweep**: a repo-wide script check (155 markdown files) for broken links and unresolvable anchors found and fixed 20 broken ADR links in ROADMAP.md itself (a repo-root-relative path used from a file two directories deep, resolving nowhere) and one stale self-referencing anchor in `docs/api/README.md` (left over from an intermediate-CA-tier heading rename). 0 broken links/anchors remain. `pkg/recipes/README.md`'s package count corrected (67 -> 68, `sysklogd` had been added without updating it).

Verified throughout: every factual claim checked against real, current code (grep/read), never assumed; `openapi.yaml` re-validated as parseable YAML after edits; every markdown link and anchor resolution-checked by script, not sampled by eye. No functional code changed -- pure documentation, tracked one commit per phase (10 commits total).

## Part 94 (done): per-source-IP throttling for repeated failed HTTPS handshakes (ADR-0134)

Direct follow-up to a live finding during the Part 93 documentation audit above: while investigating a real console error the user reported (`https handshake failed: sslv3 alert certificate unknown`), a script-verified check against 192.168.15.95 found this warning firing 500+ times a minute, sustained -- an untrusting client repeatedly hitting the HTTPS listener. Diagnosed as a real, ongoing flood (not a one-off), with no way to identify its source (`log_tls_error()`, ADR-0126, never logged a peer IP) and no way to stop it costing a real `accept4()`+`SSL_new()`+`SSL_accept()` attempt every single time, since this daemon deliberately never does HTTP keep-alive. The user asked directly for the peer IP to be logged and for throttling, explicitly requiring both be API-driven and configurable.

`daemon/src/connthrottle.c`/`connthrottle.h` (new module): tracks per-source-IP handshake failures in a 256-entry in-memory table (not persisted, matching this daemon's own established posture for transient in-flight state), with a persisted config (`enabled`/`threshold`/`window_seconds`/`block_seconds`). `accept_loop()`'s own `accept4()` now captures the real peer address (previously discarded) and, before any allocation or TLS work, refuses a currently-blocked source outright on **both** the plain HTTP and HTTPS listeners -- one shared table, checked uniformly, since a source flooding one is a reasonable thing to also stop bothering with on the other. `GET`/`PUT /v1/system/tls-throttle` (partial update, mirroring `daemon-config`/`pkg/repo-config`'s own convention) and `GET /v1/system/tls-throttle/status` close the API-First loop; `kanxeoctl tls-throttle show|set|status` and a System > Server > TLS Throttle web dashboard page (config form + live status table) follow.

**The single most important correctness decision, found the hard way while writing this feature's own test**: loopback (`127.0.0.1`) is never throttled or tracked. `kanxeoctl`'s own default `--host=` is `127.0.0.1`, and since a block applies uniformly across both listeners, tripping it from loopback would lock out this daemon's own local admin access entirely -- the same class of hazard as a firewall rule that can shut out its own operator. Testing this also surfaced a second real gap: a clean request needed to reset a source's failure count on *either* listener, not just a successful TLS handshake, otherwise a source behaving well over plain HTTP stayed permanently one stale HTTPS failure away from a block its own good behavior never got credit for -- fixed by resetting on any complete, well-formed HTTP request (`handle_client_event()`, right after parsing, regardless of what it dispatches to), not just a TLS handshake success.

Verified: new `test/test_tls_throttle.c` proves the whole mechanism against a real daemon, using a genuinely distinct source (`127.0.0.2`, daemon bound to `0.0.0.0` -- `127.0.0.0/8` routes locally on Linux, no second host needed) so the loopback exemption doesn't interfere with exercising real enforcement: config round-trip and validation, a real block tripped by real malformed-handshake bytes, the peer IP appearing in `GET /system/logs`, the block covering both listeners while loopback stays completely unaffected, the block expiring on its own, a clean request resetting the count, and `enabled=false` genuinely disabling enforcement under a sustained flood. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (39 test binaries) confirm zero regressions. Web dashboard verified with a real headless-browser session (Chromium + Puppeteer): the page renders real API-fetched config values, and a real click on Save round-trips a changed threshold through the daemon.

## Part 95 (done): ADR-0134 follow-up -- rate-limited handshake-failure logging, CA download + trust instructions (ADR-0135)

Same-day follow-up to Part 94 above: with the new peer-IP logging live on 192.168.15.95, the user checked the real flood and found it was their own desktop, not a hostile source -- its browser had the web dashboard open over HTTPS and didn't trust the install's self-signed root CA, so every 2-second poll cycle failed TLS (no HTTP keep-alive, so every poll is a fresh handshake), interspersed with just enough successes to keep resetting below the block threshold via Part 94's own "clean request forgives past failures" design. The user asked two things: make the log noise itself friendlier (rate-limit, not the block logic, since blocking their own desktop was never the goal), and add a way to actually fix the root cause -- downloading and trusting the CA.

`daemon/src/connthrottle.c` gains a second, independent per-source cooldown (`log_interval_seconds`, default 5): at most one log line per source per interval, regardless of real attempt volume, while the failure is still always counted toward the real block threshold -- only the logging is suppressed, accounting never is, and the OpenSSL error queue is still always drained even when a line is suppressed. Shares the same per-IP tracking table Part 94 already built rather than a second structure. The web dashboard's PKI > Root CA and PKI > Intermediate CA pages gain a "Download certificate (.pem)" button once bootstrapped -- a pure client-side save-as over `cert_pem`, a field the API already returns, so no new REST endpoint was needed. `docs/guides/security.md` gained a full "Trusting the CA on your own device" section covering Windows/macOS/Linux/Firefox, since each has a genuinely different trust-store story.

Verified: `test/test_tls_throttle.c` extended -- a burst of 3 deliberately-triggered failures (well under a second, real time) with `log_interval_seconds=10` now produces exactly 1 log line, not 3, while the real failure count used for blocking is confirmed unaffected (still exactly 3), proving logging and accounting are genuinely decoupled. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (40 test binaries) confirm zero regressions. The PKI download buttons (root and intermediate) were both verified with a real headless-browser click producing a real downloaded file, confirmed byte-identical to the API's own `cert_pem` in each case.

## Part 96 (done): HTTPS listener now sends the full chain -- the intermediate CA was never actually served (ADR-0136)

Direct continuation of Part 95: after downloading and trusting the root CA per the new workflow, the user reported HTTPS still failing ("I added it, and updated, but it's still failing?"). `openssl s_client -showcerts` against the live handshake showed only 1 certificate sent, despite an intermediate CA being bootstrapped on that box -- confirmed via Authority Key Identifier/Subject Key Identifier matching that the served leaf genuinely is intermediate-signed, it just was never being sent alongside it. Root cause: `create_tls_ctx()` only ever called `SSL_CTX_use_certificate_file()`, which loads exactly one cert from a PEM file and silently ignores any others, against a `host.crt` on disk that holds only the leaf. A real, previously-silent regression -- the intermediate CA and HTTPS have coexisted on 192.168.15.95 for multiple prior phases with no test ever exercising a live handshake to catch it.

Fixed via a new `pki_intermediate_cert_pem()` accessor (`daemon/src/pki.c`/`.h`) that reads the intermediate's cert PEM off disk if bootstrapped, and `SSL_CTX_add_extra_chain_cert()` in `create_tls_ctx()` to add it to the listener's `SSL_CTX` as an extra chain certificate alongside the already-loaded leaf -- correct ownership-transfer handling throughout (never freed on success, always freed on every failure path). Deliberately not written into `host.crt` itself: that file also feeds `pki_cert_deliver()`'s own already-correct chain-building for containers receiving an issued cert, which would double the intermediate there. Same round: the CA download button's file extension/MIME changed from `.pem`/`application/x-pem-file` to `.crt`/`application/x-x509-ca-cert` per a direct user suggestion -- content unchanged, but `.crt` is what Windows' "Install Certificate" flow recognizes without a manual rename. `docs/guides/security.md` updated to state plainly that trusting the root alone is now sufficient with an intermediate present, and to document the separate, still-open hostname/SAN-mismatch caveat when connecting by bare IP (the auto-issued "host" leaf's SAN carries only the install's DNS FQDN, not raw IPs -- no IP-SAN issuance exists in this project).

Verified: new `test/test_https_chain.c` bootstraps a root, then an intermediate, then enables HTTPS -- the exact ordering that exposed the bug -- captures the live handshake via `openssl s_client -showcerts`, asserts exactly 2 certificates are sent, and verifies the captured chain with `openssl verify -CAfile <root> -untrusted <chain> <chain>`, real cryptographic verification mirroring `test_pki.c`'s own `run_openssl_argv()` pattern. Ran 3x consecutively, all pass. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (41 test binaries) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 97 (done): TLS throttle blocks now only apply to the HTTPS listener (ADR-0137)

Direct continuation of Part 96: right after that deploy and reboot, the user's desktop briefly seemed unreachable ("I cannot connect to https? It's down?"). Live logs showed it was still failing HTTPS handshakes -- a genuinely separate, still-unresolved local cert-trust issue on the desktop, unrelated to the just-shipped chain fix -- and had tripped a real ADR-0134 block. Once the user confirmed the box itself was fine, they asked directly: does an HTTPS-triggered block also throttle plain HTTP for that source, and does that make sense? Reading `accept_loop()` confirmed it did, by original ADR-0134 design ("uniformly on both listeners, since a source flooding one is a reasonable thing to also stop bothering with on the other"). Since only a failed HTTPS handshake can ever cause a block in the first place (`connthrottle_record_failure()` is only ever called from the HTTPS handshake-failure path), enforcing that block against plain HTTP too achieves nothing the block is actually for, and -- found live -- can strand an otherwise-working admin fallback path behind an unrelated TLS trust problem.

Fixed by gating `accept_loop()`'s `connthrottle_should_block()` check on `is_tls` -- a plain HTTP connection from a currently-blocked source is now accepted and processed normally, regardless of how many HTTPS handshakes that source has failed. No change to the tracking table, thresholds, or block/expiry mechanics themselves, only to which listener enforcement applies to; `connthrottle_record_success()` still credits a clean request from either listener toward resetting a source's failure count, since a clean plain-HTTP request is just as real evidence of legitimate behavior as a clean HTTPS one. `docs/adr/0134-tls-handshake-throttling.md` keeps its original Decision text untouched (append-only, this project's own ADR discipline) with a short pointer annotation added noting this one claim changed, linking to the new ADR-0137.

Verified: `test/test_tls_throttle.c` rewritten around a real `openssl s_client -bind <ip>:0 -connect <https-port>` probe (the same fork/execve `openssl` subprocess pattern `test_pki.c`/`test_https_chain.c` already established) -- found necessary the hard way: the test's original raw-byte-count check (does any response data come back) couldn't distinguish "blocked before `SSL_accept()`" from "reached real handshake processing but failed on garbage input," since a malformed `ClientHello` gets an immediate connection reset with zero application bytes back either way. The s_client probe instead checks for a real negotiated cipher (`Cipher is (NONE)` = blocked at accept(), a real cipher name = reached the TLS layer) -- unambiguous regardless of certificate trust. The rewritten test proves a blocked attacker's HTTPS connection is refused at `accept()` while its plain HTTP stays simultaneously, fully reachable (the exact behavior this fix changes), and confirms block expiry, `enabled=false`, and cross-listener failure-count reset all still work exactly as ADR-0134 originally established. Full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings) + full regression sweep (39 test binaries) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 98 (done): web dashboard information-architecture rework (ADR-0138)

Asked directly, separate from any bug, to step back and audit the dashboard's own tree/page consistency -- "a lack of consistency on what's on a leaf, the number of items on a page." A full audit of the tree definition and every static leaf's render function found the taxonomy sound but its application inconsistent: System > Server held 10 leaves against every sibling subgroup's 2-5; "Root CA" routed to a page that actually held three sections (Root CA, Intermediate CA, a destructive Reset action) with no leaf of its own for Intermediate CA; "Logs" was a one-field size-cap form, not the log viewer (which has lived in the bottom panel since ADR-0129); NTP's Status and Time were two thin pages answering one real question; Route was the only creatable resource not going through the shared `+ Create` modal; Container detail defaulted to an interactive terminal (Console) rather than showing status first, split Summary and Stats across two tabs a user had to click between, and gave a whole tab (Backup) to one sentence explaining backup isn't per-container. A follow-up "raise the bar" pass, with two concrete examples from the user (missing per-network IP visibility, and whether Container Summary/Stats should be one page), surfaced a real, previously-unfilled data gap alongside the organizational ones: `GET /networks/{name}` has no per-container view, even though a container's own `networks[]` field already carries the reverse mapping.

Regrouped: System > Server split into **Host** (Daemon, Site -- moved from DNS, general instance identity not DNS-specific --, Devices, Routes, Host Swap, Rolling Restart -- the latter two split out of the old bundled Daemon page), **Monitoring** (Host Stats, Processes, Log Store -- renamed from Logs --, Syslog Targets), **Maintenance** (TLS Throttle, Update, Backup) -- each now sized like PKI/DNS/LDAP/NTP's own range instead of standing out as a dumping ground. Software gained the same two-tier shape: Catalog (Images, Packages, Recipes), Build Pipeline (Repo & Sync, Cache & Artifacts). PKI's "Root CA" leaf split into separate Root CA and Intermediate CA leaves (2 -> 3). NTP's Status and Time merged into one Time & Sync leaf (4 -> 3). Route creation moved into the shared modal, added to the `+ Create` dropdown; the Routes page is now a plain list like every other CRUD leaf.

Container detail: Summary absorbed the four live stat graphs, with polling now keyed off the Summary tab (`cd-tab-summary`) instead of a separate Stats tab -- opening a container immediately shows identity and live health together, auto-polling from the moment the page opens. The content-free Backup tab was removed; its one real sentence (platform config is backed up as a whole, never a container's own filesystem) became a short note at the bottom of Summary linking to Maintenance > Backup. Summary became the default tab in place of Console (6 tabs -> 4: Summary/Hardware/Options/Console).

Network detail gained a "Containers on this network" table (name/IP/status) -- the reverse of what a container's own Hardware tab already shows about itself, built entirely client-side from the already-cached container list (`container.networks[].ip`, confirmed against `openapi.yaml`'s `ContainerNetworkAttachment` schema) with no new endpoint, the same "presentation over already-available data" reasoning ADR-0135's CA-download button established.

Verified: a full `CATEGORY_VIEWS`/`DETAIL_VIEWS`-vs-`index.html` cross-check (script-based, both directions -- every route has a view, every view has a route) confirmed zero dangling routes or unreachable views after the rework. Real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`, no `puppeteer`'s bundled download available in this sandbox so pointed at the system's own `/usr/bin/chromium`) against a scratch daemon: extracted the live tree DOM (not a screenshot -- text content survives collapsed `hidden` state) and confirmed it matches the planned structure exactly, subgroup names/leaf order/leaf counts included; screenshotted Root CA (bootstrap status + download button + Reset CA chain, Intermediate CA no longer bundled in), Intermediate CA (its own clean page), Time & Sync (sync status + Sync now + host clock + manual override together), Daemon (now just listener/network config), Host Swap and Rolling Restart (each its own page), the Route creation modal (opened via `+ Create`, real fields, real submit), Network detail's new Containers table (correct empty state and column headers), and Container detail's merged Summary tab (4 tabs confirmed via DOM query, Summary active by default, fields + 4 live charts + backup note with working link all present) -- zero console/page errors across every page visited. `docs/guides/web-dashboard.md` rewritten to match the new tree and every affected page description; `docs/guides/administration.md`'s two "System > Server > ..." cross-references and `docs/guides/installing.md`'s one "System > Daemon" reference corrected to the new paths. `CHANGELOG.md`'s own historical entries (accurate descriptions of the dashboard *at the time* they were written) left untouched, per this project's established precedent. Zero API/daemon changes -- entirely a `web/` client-side reorganization, nothing for `kanxeoctl` or a direct API caller to adjust to.

## Part 99 (done): web dashboard theme toggle + last-viewed-page memory (ADR-0139)

Asked directly to make the tree remember its open/closed state and current page "always," and to add light/dark modes.
