Investigated the existing tree-collapse persistence against the real code and a live browser test first, rather than assuming a gap existed: `kanxeo-tree-collapsed` in `localStorage` already worked correctly for any category unrelated to the page currently being viewed (confirmed live -- collapsing an unrelated category, reloading, it stayed collapsed), and `ensureActiveCategoryExpanded()`'s force-expand of whichever category contains the *active* page turned out to be deliberate, correct behavior, not a bug -- a page whose own containing category is hidden would be actively confusing to land on. The one real, confirmed gap: a bare load with no hash at all (a fresh tab, a bookmark to the plain origin) always started at Containers, with zero memory of the last page.

Added a header theme toggle cycling Auto/Light/Dark (`kanxeo-theme` in `localStorage`). `style.css` already had a complete `prefers-color-scheme: dark` block driving CSS custom properties -- what it couldn't do was let an operator override the OS setting either direction. Two new blocks, `:root[data-theme="light"]`/`:root[data-theme="dark"]` (exact copies of the existing base `:root` and dark-media-query blocks respectively), outrank the media query via ordinary CSS specificity whenever a manual choice is active, and fall straight back to following the OS the moment it's set back to Auto (no `data-theme` attribute at all). A small inline `<script>` in `index.html`, running before the stylesheet finishes and before `app.js` loads at all, applies a saved choice synchronously so switching themes and reloading never flashes the previous one for a frame first.

Added last-viewed-page memory (`kanxeo-last-view` in `localStorage`): `renderCurrentView()` saves `location.hash` every time it resolves to a real, valid view; `restoreLastViewIfNoHash()` runs once at startup, before the first `poll()`, and only acts when the hash is already empty -- an ordinary reload with a hash present in the URL bar needs nothing from this, since the browser already keeps that on its own. Per-instance hashes (a specific container's own detail page) are remembered exactly the same as category hashes.

Verified: real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`) against a scratch daemon -- theme cycling confirmed via computed background color and the `data-theme` attribute at each of the three states; a saved `dark` choice survived a reload with no observable light-then-dark transition; visiting a page (including a container-detail hash) then opening a fresh tab at the bare origin landed back on that exact page, confirmed via both the restored hash and the actually-rendered page heading, not just the URL. Zero console/page errors throughout. `docs/guides/web-dashboard.md` gained a new Theme section and updated Layout bullets describing both behaviors precisely, including the one real nuance (a page's own containing tree category always shows expanded). Zero API/daemon changes -- both features are `web/`-only client-side preferences.

## Part 100 (done): web dashboard disk management page (ADR-0140)

Asked directly: "we need to add the disk management stuff onto the web ui right?" Checked before assuming rather than just agreeing -- grepped every "disk" reference across `web/app.js`/`web/index.html` and confirmed all 22 hits were the disk-usage *stat* graphs (Host Stats, container Stats), never disk *management*. Cross-checked `docs/api/openapi.yaml` and `cli/src/main.c`: `GET /disks` (real host block-device enumeration), `GET`/`POST`/`DELETE /diskroles` (persisted role assignment), and `GET`/`POST /disks/{name}/format` (destructive mkfs+mount) have all existed since multi-disk management Phases A-C shipped (ADR-0071/ADR-0102/ADR-0104), with `kanxeoctl disks`/`diskrole` already exposing every one of them -- but zero web dashboard surface at all. A real, confirmed gap, not a hypothetical one.

Added one new leaf, System > Host > Disks, alongside Devices rather than a new top-level subgroup -- disk management doesn't have PKI/DNS/LDAP/NTP's multi-page depth, one table-based page covers it. The table (`GET /disks`, polled like every other list) cross-references `GET /diskroles` client-side for a Role column (mirroring how Network detail's own "Containers on this network" table, ADR-0138, already cross-references two already-fetched lists rather than needing a combined endpoint), and shows live format-job state fetched only for role-assigned, non-OS disks while the page is actually open -- the same "only while this page is showing" guard `refreshImageRecipeApplyStatus()` already established, so the extra per-disk requests stay bounded no matter how many disks exist.

Role assignment is reachable two ways: the header's shared `+ Create > Disk Role` modal, or a per-row "Assign role…" shortcut that pre-fills the same modal -- the exact pattern the Devices page's own "Name…" button (device mapping) already established. Role removal is a plain button with no confirmation dialog, matching the dashboard's existing convention for reversible, non-data-destroying removals. Format is a per-row action instead of a modal, since the disk is already unambiguous from its own row -- a filesystem select plus a Format button, gated by a real `confirm()` dialog. `confirm_disk_name` (the API's own deliberate double-confirmation field) is filled in automatically from that same already-known disk name rather than asked for a second time, mirroring `kanxeoctl disks format NAME`'s own established reasoning (`cmd_disks_format()`'s own comment: "matching what the operator already typed once") rather than inventing a stricter web-only convention. A disk with a format job already `running` shows that state instead of the controls, since the API itself refuses a second concurrent job with a 409.

Verified carefully given the real risk involved: this sandbox's own `/sys/class/block` enumerates real host block devices (LVM `dm-*` volumes, real disk models like "EDILOCA EN600 Pro 2TB"), not isolated ones, so live verification was deliberately read-only-only -- confirmed via a real headless-browser session that the table renders all 64 real rows correctly with correct badge states, and that the "Assign role…" shortcut opens the shared modal with the correct disk pre-selected and the select populated from only currently-unassigned, non-OS disks. No role-assignment or format action was ever submitted against this sandbox's own disks. The "has a role assigned" rendering path (Remove role / Format controls) was verified by code review against the already-working `deviceRow()`/TLS-Throttle patterns it mirrors, plus a planned read-only check against 192.168.15.95's own already-role-assigned `sda` (assigned during the original Phase B/C verification, left untouched) once deployed. Zero API/daemon changes -- entirely `web/`.

Found along the way, noted but explicitly out of scope for this change: 192.168.15.95's `sda` currently shows `mounted: false` despite having been formatted and mounted successfully during the original Phase C work -- consistent with the API's own documented caveat that a real mount can outlive the in-memory format-job state, but silent on whether the mount itself survives a reboot; this box has rebooted several times since (this session alone deployed v1.27.0 through v1.30.0). Worth a real investigation of its own later, not folded into this UI change.

## Part 101 (done): storage layout grouping -- STATE_DIR/REBUILDABLE_DIR, automatic upgrade migration (ADR-0141 Phase 0)

First of six phases for ADR-0141's multi-disk storage placement work (itself a direct follow-up to Part 100: asked where packages/images/config/logs actually live and whether another disk role was needed, which surfaced the real answer -- one shared partition for almost everything, no way to move any of it except container-storage). This phase is the prerequisite refactor the rest of the work depends on, with no new user-facing capability of its own: checked directly rather than assumed, and confirmed every path under `g_base_dir` was completely flat (`networks.json`, `pki/`, `images/`, `pkg/`, and a dozen more, all direct children) -- nothing grouped a "state" or "rebuildable" set that could later migrate as one unit.

`main.c`'s `init_base_dir_paths()` gained two new grouping directories, `STATE_DIR` (`g_base_dir/state`) and `REBUILDABLE_DIR` (`g_base_dir/rebuildable`). Every JSON state file plus `PKI_DIR`/`SIGNING_KEYS_DIR` now nest under `STATE_DIR`; `IMAGES_DIR`/`PKG_DIR`/`ARTIFACTS_DIR`/`ISO_DIR` now nest under `REBUILDABLE_DIR`. `CONTAINERS_DIR`/`SWAP_DIR`/`LOG_DIR`/`DISKS_MOUNT_DIR` deliberately stay put -- each already has its own relocation story (or, for `LOG_DIR`, nothing else to group with) established either already or in a later phase of this same ADR. Found and fixed along the way: `PKGBUILD_TOOLCHAIN_FETCH_PATH` reconstructed its own `"pkg/..."` prefix directly from `g_base_dir` instead of deriving from the already-existing `PKG_DIR` the way every sibling path correctly does -- harmless before this change since the two happened to agree, but would have silently broken the moment `PKG_DIR` moved.

**The real risk in this phase**: every already-installed box, including 192.168.15.95 with its real live state, needed to survive this layout change automatically, with zero data loss and zero manual intervention. A new one-time, idempotent startup step, `migrate_flat_layout_to_grouped()`, detects any old-flat-layout entry still sitting directly under `g_base_dir` and `rename(2)`s it into its new grouped home -- running after `boot_init()` has mounted the real containers partition (calling it any earlier would see an empty pre-mount directory and migrate nothing) but before `ensure_dir()`'s own directory-creation pass or any subsystem's `_init()` call ever reads or writes any of these paths. A genuine bug was caught and fixed during this same work: `ensure_dir()` is a plain single-level `mkdir()`, not `mkdir -p`, so a fresh install with nothing to migrate crashed on startup (`ensure_dir(IMAGES_DIR)` failing `ENOENT` since its new parent, `REBUILDABLE_DIR`, was never created first) until `ensure_dir(STATE_DIR)`/`ensure_dir(REBUILDABLE_DIR)` were added ahead of every path nested under either.

New `test/test_layout_upgrade.c` proves the upgrade path for real rather than by inspection: bootstraps a real PKI CA and sets a real site config through the real API (so the test never risks drifting from the actual persisted schema), manually relocates both back to the old flat paths (simulating exactly what an already-installed, not-yet-upgraded box looks like), restarts the daemon against the same data directory, and confirms not just that the migration ran (old paths gone, new grouped paths present) but that the daemon is genuinely *reading* the migrated content afterward -- `cert_pem` and `instance_name` compared byte-for-byte against what was set before the round trip. Ran 3x consecutively, all pass.

The full regression sweep surfaced real, expected fallout: 66 hardcoded old-flat-path references across 26 existing test files (direct filesystem staging/inspection that bypasses the API for speed, a long-established pattern in this test suite) needed updating to the new grouped paths. Fixed via a precision script rather than a blind find-and-replace -- it only rewrote occurrences confirmed to format the real `g_data_dir` variable, correctly leaving alone same-shaped-but-unrelated paths in the same files (a mock upstream git-archive fixture's own internal layout in `test_pkg_sync.c`, a mock artifact-server scratch directory in `test_image_recipe.c`) that happened to start with the same string prefixes. Every current-tense reference in `docs/api/openapi.yaml`, `docs/api/README.md`, `docs/guides/administration.md`, and `docs/guides/remote-development.md` was updated to the new paths; historical references inside ADRs and this file's own earlier phase narratives were deliberately left as accurate-at-the-time records, per this project's established precedent.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings), full regression sweep (40 test binaries) confirm zero regressions.

## Part 102 (done): multi-disk storage placement Phase 1 -- shared primitives + mid-stream disk-health/passthrough/stats requirements (ADR-0141/ADR-0142)

Second of six phases for ADR-0141's multi-disk storage placement work, this one building the shared primitives every later per-resource phase (state-storage, log-storage, rebuildable-storage, container-storage migration) needs, rather than shipping a new REST resource of its own. Mid-stream, four more requirements arrived directly from the operator in the same breath as approving the overall plan -- "all the way to the end" -- and were folded in rather than deferred: a checkdisk/unmount/remount cycle, support for role-less/unlabeled disks passed straight through to containers, per-disk/per-partition monitoring graphs with real disk I/O and I/O-delay stats, and the ability to move containers/configs/backups between disks after the fact. Written up as ADR-0142 alongside ADR-0141 rather than folded silently into it, since it's a genuinely separate (if related) set of decisions.

`diskrole.h`/`diskrole.c` gained the three new roles ADR-0141 defines -- `state-storage`, `rebuildable-storage`, `log-storage`, alongside the existing `container-storage`/`backup` -- each a daemon-wide singleton placement (several disks can be *eligible* candidates for the same role, but only one is ever the *active* placement, tracked separately in a later phase). `role_from_str()` was factored out of what used to be two separate inline `strcmp` checks (load and create), now shared by both.

Closed a real, previously-deferred gap along the way: a disk's `fs_type` (which real filesystem a successful `POST /disks/{name}/format` used) is now persisted in `diskroles.json` via new `diskrole_set_fs_type()`/`diskrole_lookup_fs_type()` calls, and a new `diskformat_remount_present_role_disks()` runs once at daemon startup -- after `diskrole_init()`, before the daemon serves any request -- remounting every present-but-unmounted, role-assigned disk with its remembered filesystem. This directly answers the gap flagged but deferred at the end of Part 100/101: 192.168.15.95's own `sda`, formatted and mounted successfully back in the original Phase C work, showing `mounted: false` after a reboot. Never a second automatic `mkfs` on remount failure -- logged and left alone, since silently re-formatting a disk that merely failed to mount would be a real data-loss risk for a filesystem that might just need a manual `fsck`.

A new shared primitive, `treecopy.h`/`treecopy.c` (`treecopy_recursive()`), was built specifically for the migrations later phases add -- deliberately *not* reusing `pkg.c`'s existing `merge_tree()`/`copy_file_simple()`, both of which hardcode destination file mode to `0755` regardless of the source. Harmless for `pkg.c`'s own callers (recipe/artifact staging, nothing sensitive); a real security regression for a state-storage migration, which would carry `pki.c`'s `0600` private keys through it. `test/test_treecopy.c` proves exact mode preservation across a 0600 key, a 0644 cert, and a 0640 nested file, plus verbatim (never-followed) symlink recreation.

The raw-disk-passthrough requirement landed in `device.c`: a new `enumerate_disk()` pass, alongside the existing usb/pci/net/gpu ones, exposes every non-OS, role-less whole disk under `GET /devices` as a `"disk:<name>"` entry -- grantable to a container's `devices` field exactly like any other bus id, for fresh/unlabeled media (a USB enclosure, a PCI-passthrough disk) with no filesystem concept of its own at all yet. A disk already carrying a role is correctly excluded, since it's already owned by this daemon's own storage-placement system, not independently grantable at the same time. `struct discovered_device.bus` widened from `char[4]` to `char[8]` to fit `"disk"` (5 bytes with the terminator). Threading the OS-disk-exclusion context through required widening `device_enumerate()`/`device_find()`/`device_find_group()`/`device_write_json_list()` and the `devicemap.c` equivalents with an explicit `os_containers_dir` parameter, mirroring `disk_enumerate()`'s own already-established "caller passes it in, module holds no daemon-layer global" convention, rather than reaching for `main.c`'s `CONTAINERS_DIR` global directly from a lower-level module.

Not yet done in this phase, explicitly deferred to where each belongs: per-disk I/O/capacity stats (`GET /disks`, `/sys/block/<dev>/stat`) and the monitoring-page graphs land in the final Part 6 phase alongside the full regression/deploy; the checkdisk/unmount/remount *operator-triggered* workflow (distinct from this phase's own automatic startup remount) and the move-things-between-disks mechanism land in the state/log/rebuildable-storage phases and the dedicated container-storage-migration phase respectively, where the actual migration machinery this phase's `treecopy_recursive()` exists to support gets wired up end to end.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings across all 41 build targets). Full regression sweep (33 daemon-linked tests plus `test_devices`) -- zero failures. `test_daemon_devices.c` extended with a real, live scenario proving the passthrough/role interaction end to end against this dev sandbox's own real `/sys/class/block` (64 real disk entries, including `nvme0n1`/`sdb` with real model and size): a non-OS disk is listed under `GET /devices`, disappears the instant `POST /diskroles` assigns it a role, and reappears the instant that role is removed. `docs/api/openapi.yaml`/`docs/api/README.md` updated together, in this same change, for the extended role enum, the new `fs_type` field, and the new `disk:` bus type -- per this project's own documentation-map rule that these two files never drift apart.

## Part 103 (done): fix a real boot-order race in Part 101's own layout migration -- /etc/resolv.conf silently empty after the first post-upgrade boot

Found live, mid-deploy of Part 102 to 192.168.15.95: the hostbuild's own source-fetch step failed outright, `curl: (6) Could not resolve host: git.home.arpa`. Investigated rather than routed around -- `GET /v1/system/resolv` correctly reported the real, previously-configured nameservers, and directly querying those exact resolvers from another machine on the same network confirmed they answered the query correctly; the operator then asked to point the box at its real external resolvers instead (192.168.15.200/192.168.15.100) and remove two now-redundant records from Kanxeo's own internal DNS (`git.home.arpa`/`git.uk.home.arpa`, both already correctly served by the real external resolvers too) -- done, but the fetch still failed identically. A throwaway scratch recipe pointed at a plain, universally-resolvable public hostname (`example.com`, unrelated to `.arpa` entirely) failed the exact same way, ruling out anything specific to the hostname or DNS server in use: outbound DNS resolution was broken *wholesale* for every hostname, regardless of which resolver was configured.

Traced to the real cause by reading `boot_init()`'s own statement order rather than guessing further: its `/etc/resolv.conf` bind-mount (`open(RESOLV_CONF_PATH, O_CREAT...)` then `mount(..., MS_BIND, ...)`) runs immediately after the containers partition is mounted -- but `migrate_flat_layout_to_grouped()` (Part 101) isn't called until much later, back in `main()`, after `boot_init()` has already returned. Both reference the identical, already-*grouped* `RESOLV_CONF_PATH`. On this box's first boot after Part 101's grouping shipped, nothing had migrated to that path yet, so the `O_CREAT` silently created a fresh, empty file and bind-mounted `/etc/resolv.conf` onto *that* inode. The later `rename(2)` that moved the box's real, historical `resolv.conf` into the same path only repoints the directory entry -- a bind mount is tied to the inode it captured at mount time, not the path -- so `/etc/resolv.conf` stayed permanently bound to the orphaned empty file, invisible to every subsequent `PUT /v1/system/resolv` (which correctly updated the *real*, now-differently-pointed-to path the whole time, explaining why the daemon's own state was never wrong). A pure one-time race, self-resolving on any later boot once nothing is left to migrate -- exactly why `test/test_layout_upgrade.c` never caught it: that test proves the migration itself works, but never calls `boot_init()` or exercises a real bind-mount at all.

Fixed by also calling `migrate_flat_layout_to_grouped()` from inside `boot_init()`, right after the containers partition mount and before the resolv.conf bind-mount step that follows it -- closing the race for every grouped-layout path `boot_init()` touches, not only this one. The function was already explicitly designed to be idempotent (a no-op once nothing remains at the old flat paths), so `main()`'s own existing call right after `boot_init()` returns needed no change -- it stays exactly as Part 101 left it, still the only path reached by a non-`--init-mode` test/dev daemon, and a harmless no-op the second time on a real boot. 192.168.15.95 itself needed no waiting for this fix to actually ship: since `STATE_DIR/resolv.conf` already held the real, correct nameservers by the time this was diagnosed, a plain reboot on the still-running, unpatched v1.32.0 binary was already sufficient to re-establish a correct bind mount against the now-real file -- confirmed by retrying the `example.com` scratch-recipe probe after the reboot and watching it succeed.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings). This is a boot-sequencing fix specific to a real `--init-mode` boot with genuine pre-existing flat-layout state to race against -- not independently reproducible in this dev sandbox, which has none of the real block devices (`CONTAINERS_DEVICE`/ESP/config) `boot_init()` needs, the same QEMU-only boundary `test/test_boot.c` already documents for this class of code. Verified instead by a complete manual trace of the exact statement order and each path's own construction, and by the live, real-world reboot test on 192.168.15.95 matching the theorized mechanism exactly: identical wholesale DNS failure before, full resolution restored immediately after, with no other change in between.

## Part 104 (done): multi-disk storage placement Phase 2 -- state-storage end to end, live migration (ADR-0141)

Third of six phases for ADR-0141's multi-disk storage placement work, and the first to ship a real, operator-facing resource: `GET/POST /v1/system/state-storage(/migrate)`, `kanxeoctl storage state`, and a new Disks-page section on the web dashboard. Asked directly at the start of the overall plan for state-storage migration to be genuinely live -- "no pause, no readonly window, ordinary operation continues" was the ADR's own stated design, not a nice-to-have -- and building it for real, rather than reaching for a simpler "copy now, apply on next reboot" compromise, surfaced two significant design gaps that needed solving first.

**The first**: `STATE_DIR` becoming relocatable creates a genuine bootstrap circularity. Knowing *which* disk state-storage lives on requires reading `diskroles.json` (the role table) and the new storage-placement pointer -- but Phase 0 had put `diskroles.json` *inside* `STATE_DIR` itself, meaning the file that says "state lives on disk X" would itself live inside the thing being relocated. Fixed by giving both `diskroles.json` and the new `storage_placement.json` a genuinely fixed, never-relocated home directly under `g_base_dir` -- the same "stays put, foundational" category Phase 0 already established for `CONTAINERS_DIR`/`SWAP_DIR`/`LOG_DIR`/`DISKS_MOUNT_DIR`. Since Phase 0 had already shipped and already migrated `diskroles.json` into `STATE_DIR` on the live box, this needed a second one-time migration (`migrate_diskroles_out_of_state_dir()`) moving it back out -- applying the exact boot-ordering discipline Part 103's own resolv.conf bug had just taught: run it early, before anything reads the path it's fixing.

**The second, larger gap**: a real hot repoint -- the daemon continuing to operate normally throughout, no restart needed for the new location to take effect -- turned out to require far more than flipping one shared `STATE_DIR` string in `main.c`. Every one of the roughly fifteen subsystems that persist state under `STATE_DIR` (`network.c`, `dns.c`, the four LDAP modules, `pki.c`, `containerdef.c` twice over, `siteconfig.c`, `daemon_config.c`, `devicemap.c`, `quotamap.c`, `resolv.c`, `ntp.c`, `syslogfwd.c`, `connthrottle.c`) copies its own state-file path into a private static buffer once, at its own `_init()` call, rather than reading a shared global on every write -- confirmed directly, the same pattern `resolv.c`'s and `daemon_config.c`'s own code already showed. Mutating `main.c`'s own `STATE_DIR` after boot would do nothing to any of them; they'd keep writing to the old location forever, silently. Simply re-calling each module's existing `_init()` a second time wasn't safe either -- for a daemon already running with real, populated in-memory state (live bridges, real DNS records, real PKI certs), re-loading from a path whose content had only just been copied there would either duplicate work harmlessly or, worse, momentarily present as if that state had reset. The right fix, and the one built: a real `X_repoint(new_path)` added to every one of those ~15 modules, each doing exactly one thing -- update the module's own private path buffer -- and nothing else. The already-copied file at the new location is correct from the moment `treecopy_recursive()`'s bulk pass finishes; repointing only needs to affect *future* writes, never trigger a reload.

The migration itself follows the ADR's own two-phase design exactly: `storagemigrate.c` (mirroring `diskformat.c`'s fork+pidfd+epoll shape, one job per storage kind rather than one daemon-wide slot, since a state-storage migration and a future rebuildable-storage migration are genuinely independent operations) forks a child to do the bulk permission-preserving copy while the daemon keeps reading and writing the *current* location completely normally; once that finishes, the single-threaded reactor thread (ADR-0009 -- no locking needed, nothing else can run concurrently and observe a half-repointed state) does one more synchronous, fast copy pass, then calls every module's new `_repoint()`, re-establishes the real `/etc/resolv.conf` bind mount against the new location (the exact Part 103 lesson applied proactively this time, not discovered the hard way again), persists the new active placement, and removes the old location's data. `DELETE /diskroles/{name}` and `POST /disks/{name}/format` both gained the 409 safety check ADR-0141 specified from the start -- refused against a disk that's the current active state-storage placement, since removing the role or destroying the disk's content out from under a live placement would silently strand the daemon's own state with no error at the time it happened.

Building this also surfaced two smaller, unrelated confirmed gaps, fixed along the way: `GET /disks`' own API description still claimed container-storage migration was "a queued follow-up (Phase D)" -- Phase D (choosing a container's storage disk at creation time) shipped long ago; the real still-open item is the separate, later ADR-0142 post-creation-migration phase, and the text was simply describing the wrong thing. And the disk-role assignment UI (both `kanxeoctl diskrole create --role=` help text and the web dashboard's own modal dropdown) had never been updated for the three roles Phase 1 added at the API layer -- `state-storage`/`rebuildable-storage`/`log-storage` existed as valid, working role values with no way to actually select them from either surface until now.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings across 66 build targets). Full regression sweep (35 test binaries) confirms zero regressions -- two flakes seen once (`test_pkg_build_log`, `test_container_lifecycle`, both real timing-sensitive races unrelated to this change, e.g. a cgroup-freeze-state poll losing to system load) both reproduced clean on immediate retry. New `test/test_storage_placement.c` proves every validation and rejection path that doesn't require formatting a real disk (unknown disk, the OS disk itself, wrong role, role-correct-but-unmounted, a missing `disk` field distinguished from an explicit `null`) against this dev sandbox's own real, live hardware via non-destructive role assignment only -- the genuinely successful migration path needs a real, disposable block device this sandbox's own real host disks can't safely provide (confirmed real models like an NVMe drive and a USB "Game Drive" showed up in earlier live enumeration), the same honest testability boundary this suite already documents for GPU/veth/QEMU-only cases elsewhere. Real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`) confirmed the new Disks-page "State storage placement" section renders the current placement, the target-disk select, and correctly surfaces a real 409 (attempting to migrate to the already-active default) through the dashboard's existing status mechanism with zero console/page errors; confirmed the disk-role modal's dropdown now lists all 5 roles.

## Part 105 (done): multi-disk storage placement Phase 3 -- log-storage end to end (ADR-0141)

Fourth of six phases for ADR-0141's multi-disk storage placement work, and noticeably lighter than Phase 2 -- the shared machinery Phase 2 built (`storagemigrate.c`'s async job runner, `storageplacement.c`'s persisted pointer, the boot-time `resolve_*_placement()` pattern) was already generic across all three storage kinds from the start, precisely so this moment would be mostly wiring rather than a second round of design work. `GET/POST /v1/system/log-storage(/migrate)`, `kanxeoctl storage logs`, and a second placement section on the web dashboard's Disks page now give operators the same live, no-downtime migration for the consolidated log store (kernel dmesg, this daemon's own diagnostics, the per-request audit trail, every container's stdout/stderr) that state-storage already had.

The one genuinely new piece, flagged back in the original ADR and confirmed still true when this phase actually built it: `LOG_DIR` has exactly one consumer (`logstore.c`), unlike `STATE_DIR`'s roughly fifteen, but that one consumer holds a persistently-open file handle across writes (`ensure_current_segment_open()` only reopens when `g_current_fp` is `NULL`, not on every call) -- a bare path-buffer update the way every other repoint works would leave already-open writes landing on the old, about-to-be-deleted disk. `logstore_repoint()` closes the current segment and nulls the handle before updating the path; the next write naturally reopens, in append mode, the already-migrated segment file at the new location -- resuming the exact same logical segment rather than either losing track of it or starting a fresh one. `resolve_log_storage_placement()` mirrors `resolve_state_storage_placement()`'s own boot-time shape exactly (same fail-loud-not-silent posture if the configured disk isn't available), and `finalize_log_storage_migration()` mirrors `finalize_state_storage_migration()` minus the one piece log-storage doesn't need -- there's no `/etc/resolv.conf`-style external bind mount to re-establish for a plain log directory.

Two smaller pieces of housekeeping came along with wiring a second kind through code that Phase 2 had, reasonably, only ever exercised with one: `is_active_storage_singleton_placement()` and its two 409 call sites (`DELETE /diskroles/{name}`, `POST /disks/{name}/format`) now check log-storage's own active placement alongside state-storage's, and `respond_storagemigrate_error()` gained a `storage_kind` parameter so a log-storage request gets a message that actually says "log-storage" (busy, wrong role, already active) instead of a hardcoded "state-storage" left over from being the only kind that existed when that function was first written. Both the CLI and the web dashboard took the same refactor in parallel -- `cmd_storage_kind()` and `STORAGE_KINDS`/`refreshStoragePlacement()` respectively -- turning what would otherwise have been a second copy-pasted block of near-identical code into one shared, kind-parameterized implementation.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings across 66 build targets). Full regression sweep (35 test binaries) -- zero failures. `test/test_storage_placement.c` extended with the identical class of validation-path coverage Phase 2 already established for state-storage (default `disk:null`, wrong role, role-correct-but-unmounted) run again for log-storage specifically -- proving the kind-awareness actually reaches every layer (`storagemigrate_start()`'s own `role_str_for_kind()`, the REST error messages, the daemon's stored job state) rather than assuming the refactor was correct from code review alone. Real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`) confirmed both placement sections render independently with correct, distinct current-placement text, and that a log-storage migration attempt against the already-active default surfaces the real, kind-specific 409 ("this is already the active log-storage placement") through the dashboard's shared status mechanism -- not a leftover "state-storage" string from the section it was cloned from.

## Part 106 (done): multi-disk storage placement Phase 4 -- rebuildable-storage end to end (ADR-0141)

Fifth of six phases for ADR-0141's multi-disk storage placement work, and the third and final storage kind to reach real REST/CLI/web surfaces. `GET/POST /v1/system/rebuildable-storage(/migrate)`, `kanxeoctl storage rebuildable`, and a third section on the web dashboard's Disks page now cover the last of Kanxeo's own daemon-wide storage concerns -- container images, installed packages, build artifacts, staged ISOs -- with the identical live, no-downtime migration state-storage and log-storage already had.

Where Phase 2 needed a genuine design correction (moving `diskroles.json` to a fixed path to break a circularity) and Phase 3 needed one real piece of extra care (`logstore.c`'s persistently-open segment handle), this phase's own scope note from the original ADR review -- "`rebuildable-storage` is expected to be the same [as state-storage's safe bare-string repoint]... but this needs confirming directly during implementation, not assumed" -- was checked directly rather than taken on faith, and turned out to hold: every one of `REBUILDABLE_DIR`'s real consumers (`image.c`'s own single cached path, and `pkg.c`'s five distinct concerns -- main package state, repo-sync config, cache config, artifact-fetch config, and `image_recipe_init()`'s own image-recipe-apply state) caches nothing but a plain path string, with no held-open file descriptor anywhere in the chain. `pkg_repoint()` and its four siblings (`pkg_repo_repoint()`, `pkg_cache_repoint()`, `pkg_artifact_repoint()`, `image_recipe_repoint()`) each do exactly what every `STATE_DIR`-backed module's own `*_repoint()` already established: update the cached path, touch nothing else -- no reload of the installed-package list, no disruption to any job already in flight, no reset of `image_recipe_init()`'s own last-apply-status fields the way a genuine re-init would.

The one small piece of real refactoring this phase needed in `main.c` itself: `init_base_dir_paths()`'s own `REBUILDABLE_DIR`-relative path computations (`IMAGES_DIR`, the whole `PKG_DIR` family, `ARTIFACTS_DIR`, `ISO_DIR`) were previously computed inline, once, at daemon startup only -- exactly the same shape `STATE_DIR`'s own paths had before Phase 2 needed them re-runnable. Split out into `compute_rebuildable_dir_relative_paths()` and paired with a new `resolve_rebuildable_storage_placement()` (identical boot-time shape, and the identical fail-loud-not-silent posture, as its `state`/`log` siblings), the same pattern is now proven across all three kinds this ADR ever defined.

`is_active_storage_singleton_placement()` and its two 409 call sites (`DELETE /diskroles/{name}`, `POST /disks/{name}/format`) were extended a third time, completing the safety net across every storage-placement kind this project has.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings across 66 build targets). Full regression sweep (35 test binaries) -- zero failures (one confirmed pre-existing timing flake, `test_container_lifecycle`, reproduced clean on immediate retry). `test/test_storage_placement.c` extended a third time with the same validation-path coverage already proven correct for state and log storage, now covering all three kinds from one shared test file. Real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`) confirmed all three placement sections render independently and correctly on the Disks page, and that a rebuildable-storage migration attempt against the already-active default surfaces the correct, kind-specific 409 through the dashboard's shared status mechanism.

## Part 141 (done): live, single-file container updates without a recreate (ADR-0153, task #861)

Investigating "where does cmd/files/network/options live in a recipe" (the same question behind Part 137's container recipes) surfaced a narrower, related gap task #861 asked for directly: updating a container's files without a full recreate. `PUT /v1/containers/{name}/files?path=...` closes the files-only piece of that -- same request shape as a `POST /containers` `files[]` entry, applied to a container that already exists. Path resolution mirrors the existing read side's own running/not-running split: running, through `/proc/<pid>/root` (the overlay's own copy-up lands the write in the real, host-visible upperdir); stopped or exited, written directly into the upperdir.

Deliberately live and ephemeral by design, not an oversight: the write never touches the container's own persisted `files[]` body, so a future restart or recreate replays the original definition unchanged. The durable "survive a recreate" path stays a container recipe (ADR-0151, Part 137) -- edit the recipe, re-apply it. Merging one ad-hoc write into an arbitrary already-persisted `files[]` array (dedup by path, handle removal, stay consistent across repeated writes) is real complexity this feature deliberately doesn't take on.

A real use-after-free was caught and fixed during this feature's own verification, before it shipped: the first implementation read the new content as a pointer straight out of the parsed JSON request body, then freed that JSON tree *before* using the same pointer in the subsequent `write()` -- a live end-to-end test wrote 17 bytes and read back 17 bytes of garbage instead of the real content. Fixed by taking an owned copy of the content before freeing the JSON tree, freed explicitly on every return path.

`kanxeoctl files put NAME --path=/some/path --file=LOCAL_PATH [--mode=0644]` mirrors `files get`'s own existing shape.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`), zero warnings. New coverage in `test/test_container_files.c` proves both code paths for real -- a running-container write/read round-trip checked directly against the real host-visible `upper/` file (content, mode, and owner/group via `stat()`, not just a 204), an overwrite-replaces-not-appends check, the same traversal/no-leading-slash/missing-content/unknown-container rejections the read side already has, and a write/read round-trip against an already-exited container. Full regression sweep (`test_daemon`, `test_cli`, `test_web`, `test_container_lifecycle`, `test_images`, `test_container_restart`, `test_container_recipe`, `test_hostauth`) clean afterward. `cmd`, network attachment, and other non-file option updates without a recreate remain unimplemented -- task #861 stays open for that remaining scope.

## Part 140 (done): audit every recipe for the ambient-gcc-contamination risk (task #845)

CLAUDE.md's own environment notes already documented a real, confirmed bug class: `openssh/10.4p1-7`/`-8` needed an explicit `CC=tcc` after the shared build sandbox's own bare `cc` started silently resolving to real GCC instead of TCC. Audited all 78 package recipes for the same exposure -- 68 had a real, confirmed `./configure` or plain `make` with no compiler pin, now fixed (`perl.recipe` uses Perl's own `-Dcc=tcc` `Configure` flag instead, since a bare environment `CC=` isn't reliably honored there).

A real mistake was caught mid-audit, not shipped: the first automated pass also added `CC=tcc` to `tcc.recipe` itself, which its own header explicitly documents as deliberately built with real gcc (bootstrapping) -- reverted before committing. `gcc.recipe`'s own `CC=tcc` (bootstrapping gcc from tcc) was correctly kept; `kernel`/`openssl`/`gitea`/`kanxeo` were confirmed, by their own existing header comments, to be deliberate real-gcc exceptions and left untouched.

**Not rebuild-verified** -- no bootstrapped local toolchain sandbox was available during this audit (the real box was unreachable at the time), so none of the 68 recipes were actually rebuilt after the change. Each edit is a minimal, additive compiler pin matching the exact pattern already proven correct for `openssh`/`sysklogd` -- low-risk, but genuinely unverified by a real build, flagged honestly rather than assumed safe.

## Part 139 (done): host-auth session listing + revoke (ADR-0152)

User-asked directly: where do tokens live, can they be listed, do they survive a restart, is there a CLI/web surface? `g_sessions[]` turned out to have zero introspection anywhere. Confirmed with the user this was worth closing.

`GET /v1/system/hostauth/sessions` (username + expires_in_seconds, never the raw token) and `DELETE /v1/system/hostauth/sessions/{username}` (revoke every active session for that user -- deliberately per-username, since the only real per-session identifier is the token itself, never surfaced past its one-time login response). New `kanxeoctl hostauth-sessions ls|revoke`, and a new `System > Host > Sessions` web page.

A real off-by-one bug in the route's own prefix-length arithmetic was caught by the new test before shipping (the revoke endpoint 404'd unconditionally). Verified: full clean rebuild, zero warnings, `test/test_hostauth.c` covers real expiry data, no token leakage, revoke invalidating the token immediately, and a no-op revoke for a user with no session. Web panel verified via the Chrome DevTools Protocol directly: real login, real session shown, real revoke confirmed via the dashboard's own audit log. See [ADR-0152](../adr/0152-hostauth-session-introspection.md).

## Part 138 (done): web dashboard Software Catalogue -- one page, three recipe tabs

User-requested direct follow-up to Part 137: the "Recipes" page was package-recipes-only; image recipes were only reachable buried inside each image's own detail page, and the new container recipes had zero UI. `#view-recipes` restructured into a real 3-tab catalogue (Packages/Images/Containers), each with its own client-side name search and Apply/Edit/Delete directly from the list.

New `container-recipe-form`/`container-recipe-apply-form` modals (the latter taking a `secrets` JSON object for `{{SECRET:KEY}}` substitution). `image-recipe-form`'s name field is no longer hardcoded readonly -- toggled per open path.

Verified: real headless-Chromium session via the Chrome DevTools Protocol directly (`puppeteer-core` unavailable here) -- all three tabs render with real data, the container-recipe Apply modal submits and a real container gets created (confirmed via a direct API check), the image-recipe Edit modal loads existing content correctly.

## Part 137 (done): container recipes -- a third recipe kind, alongside package/image (ADR-0151)

User-requested directly, motivated by this session's own repeated pain: `jumpbox1`/`dns-1`/`dns-2`/`ldap-1`/`ldap-2`/`syslog-1`/`syslog-2` have each been recreated multiple times, every time hand-retyping the full `cmd`/`files`/network/restart definition from memory, nothing git-tracked to source it from.

`recipes/container/<name>/<version>/container.json`: content is a real `POST /v1/containers` body verbatim. Daemon storage mirrors `image_recipe_*` (one current recipe per name, no daemon-side version-keying). New `{{SECRET:KEY}}` substitution mechanism (generalizing `kanxeo.recipe`'s own manual `REPLACE_WITH_REAL_TOKEN` convention into something mechanical) lets a recipe's staged file content reference a credential without ever committing the real value to git -- correctly JSON-escaped via two new small `json.c` primitives sharing the one existing escaping ruleset. `POST /v1/containers/recipes/{name}/apply` renders the recipe and calls the exact same `handle_create()` a direct `POST /containers` uses. `pkg sync` extended a third time. CLI: `container recipe add|show|rm|ls`, `container apply-recipe NAME [--secret=KEY=VALUE ...]`.

Verified: full clean rebuild, zero warnings. New `test/test_container_recipe.c` covers name/content mismatch rejection, add/list/get, apply with a secret (correctly escaped, including a value containing a literal `"`), apply with no secret (token left untouched), 404 on an unknown name, and rm. Full regression sweep clean. See [ADR-0151](../adr/0151-container-recipes.md).

## Part 136 (done): dns-1/dns-2 repointed at real LAN resolvers

User-requested: `dns-1`/`dns-2`'s own upstream forwarders (`dnsmasq --server=`) were still `1.1.1.1`/`8.8.8.8` from initial re-provisioning -- switched to the real LAN resolvers `192.168.15.200`/`192.168.15.100`, which know `home.arpa` addresses this public-DNS pair never could. Both containers recreated one at a time (never simultaneously, to avoid a full management-network DNS outage), verified reachable and forwarding correctly (`dig` against each) before moving to the next.

## Part 135 (done): all 4 image manifests switch to rolling, closing a dead follow_rolling flag

Direct follow-up to Part 132/ADR-0149: publishing the 4 captured image recipes surfaced that every one declared its packages `pinned`. Asked directly whether that was intentional; the user pointed to Kanxeo's own charter (`CLAUDE.md`'s opening line calls it "a custom, rolling-release... platform") -- pinned was never the actual policy, just what got captured from what happened to be installed. A deeper check found the consequence was real: all 4 images' manifests had zero entries, meaning jumpbox1's own `follow_rolling=true` (set in an earlier session) had nothing to follow and had been silently inert the whole time.

`recipes/image/{dns,ldap,syslog,jumpbox}/1.1.0/build.sh` switch every entry to `rolling`; published and applied on the real box via the synchronous bulk-declare path (ADR-0123). All 4 manifests confirmed populated and rolling via a live round-trip. `queue_rolling_rebuilds_for()` (ADR-0107) is now live for real: any future recipe-version bump for one of these packages auto-queues a rebuild, and jumpbox1's `follow_rolling` (re-confirmed `true` this time, Part 134) picks it up with a jittered restart (ADR-0124).

## Part 134 (done): jumpbox1 auto-creates LDAP home directories (task #864), gains real admin tooling

Second of two real interactive-SSH gaps reported this session (the other, PTY allocation, is Part 133/ADR-0150). `recipes/package/linux-pam/1.6.1-2/build.sh` adds `modules/pam_mkhomedir` to the existing module set -- glauth has no home-directory-provisioning concept, and this project's minimal images ship no `/home` content, so an LDAP account's first login had nowhere to land. jumpbox1's `/etc/pam.d/sshd` gained `session required pam_mkhomedir.so skel=/etc/skel umask=0077`.

Also closes most of task #842: `psmisc`/`screen`/`htop`/`inetutils`/`mtr`/`iproute2` (plus `ncurses`) installed onto the jumpbox image -- all six recipes already existed in git but had never been published to the real box.

jumpbox1 recreated with both changes plus `--follow-rolling` (a real `container ls --json` inspection found this had actually been `false` on the live box despite an earlier intent to set it -- corrected here).

Verified: real SSH login confirms `/home/osakka` auto-creates (`drwx------ osakka admins`) and a fresh session lands in it directly. `pkg ls --image=jumpbox` confirms all 6 tools plus `ncurses` installed.

## Part 133 (done): every container gets a real devpts mount + /dev/ptmx (ADR-0150, task #865)

User-reported, real: interactive SSH into jumpbox1 failed with "PTY allocation request failed on channel 0" -- every prior test this session only ever used non-interactive `ssh ... id`. Root cause: `pkg_seed_image_baseline()` never seeded a `/dev/ptmx` node, and `mountns_pivot()` never mounted `devpts` inside a container's own mount namespace -- the same bug class task #764 already fixed once for the daemon's own host-namespace console-exec feature, one layer deeper, never addressed for containers.

`mountns_pivot()` (`src/mountns.c`) now mounts a fresh `devpts` at `/dev/pts` on every container start, same treatment `/proc`/`/sys`/`/run` already get. `pkg_seed_image_baseline()` (`daemon/src/pkg.c`) now seeds a `/dev/ptmx` symlink alongside its five existing static device nodes -- self-heals into pre-existing images automatically since this function reruns on every install, not just an image's first version.

Verified: full clean rebuild, zero warnings. New `test/test_container_pty.c` + `test/pty_child.c` proves a real pty allocates and round-trips a byte inside a real container, the same sequence `sshd`'s own interactive session uses. A real regression the new test caught on its own first run (the `mountns_pivot()` change assumed `/dev` already existed, breaking `test_overlay.c`/`test_container_net.c`'s own minimal lowerdirs) was found and fixed before this ever left the session. Full regression sweep (15 tests) clean. See [ADR-0150](../adr/0150-container-devpts-mount.md).

## Part 132 (done): unified `recipes/package/` + `recipes/image/` git layout (ADR-0149)

Follow-up to task #867 (git-track the 4 image recipes captured re-provisioning the box's own live infrastructure, wire `pkg sync` to walk them -- the exact gap ADR-0123's own Consequences section flagged and deferred). Mid-implementation, the user objected that `pkg/recipes/` (versioned `<name>/<version>/build.sh`) and the newly-created `pkg/image-recipes/` (flat `<name>.recipe`) didn't read as one coherent system, and proposed a single top-level `recipes/<kind>/` split with an identical shape either side. That runs directly into ADR-0123's own deliberate "image recipes aren't versioned" reasoning (an image's build result is already content-addressed, ADR-0108) -- surfaced back to the user rather than silently resolved either way (`AskUserQuestion`); the user chose to version both, reconciled by treating an image recipe's version as tracking the *recipe's own declared-content history*, a distinct axis from the image's build-result hash, not a second copy of it.

`pkg/recipes/` -> `recipes/package/` (77 packages, format/content unchanged). `pkg/image-recipes/` -> `recipes/image/<name>/<version>/build.sh`; the 4 recipes captured this session start at `1.0.0`. `daemon/src/pkg.c`'s `pkg_sync_completed()` gained `sync_walk_image_recipes()`, walking `recipes/image` and publishing only the highest version per image name (`pkg_version_compare()`, the same rule an unpinned `pkg install NAME` already uses) via `image_recipe_add()` -- whose own daemon-side API is otherwise untouched, still one current, unversioned manifest per name.

Verified: full clean rebuild, zero warnings. `test/test_pkg_sync.c` extended with a two-version image-recipe fixture, proving both that the higher version wins and that image-recipe syncs land in "added" every time (always-overwrite) while package-recipe syncs correctly land in "skipped" once already present -- a real, documented asymmetry between the two kinds, not a bug. Full regression sweep clean.

## Part 131 (done): glauth's own baseDN becomes daemon-managed (ADR-0148)

Direct follow-up: the user asked whether the LDAP base DN should be genuinely dynamic rather than fixed by hand -- it lived in four independently-typed places, agreeing only by coincidence. Closed the one that's genuinely closable: a registered glauth server is a real, tracked Kanxeo resource, so `ldap_write_config_file()` now also rewrites a `baseDN = "..."` line in the preserved config prefix to match `hostauth_ldap_base_dn()` (a new getter), on every write it already does. The other two copies (client containers' own `nslcd.conf`/`ldap-authkeys.conf`) stay deliberately, permanently manual -- Kanxeo has no model of those containers to safely template into, documented explicitly rather than left an unstated gap. Found and fixed along the way: `docs/api/openapi.yaml`/`README.md` both claimed LDAP registration "never touches the container's filesystem," which was already false before this change.

Verified: full clean rebuild, zero warnings. New `test/test_ldap.c` coverage proves the rewrite (and its deliberate no-op cases) directly. 3 consecutive clean runs, full regression sweep.

## Part 130 (done): renaming an LDAP user or group in place (ADR-0147)

User-requested: `PUT /v1/ldap/users|groups/{name}` had always discarded the body's own `name` field entirely -- the only way to rename a record was delete-then-recreate. Now a real, different `name` in the body renames it (omitted or identical: unchanged, no-rename behavior). The group case carries one real, deliberate cascade the user case doesn't need: a new `hostauth_rename_admin_group()` (`daemon/src/hostauth.c`) keeps `hostauth-config`'s own `admin_groups` list pointed at a renamed group automatically, so a renamed admin group can never silently fall out of write-gating -- the same class of incident ADR-0146 closed once already, a different cause this time. CLI: `--new-name=NEWNAME` on both `ldap group update`/`ldap user update`, deliberately distinct from `--name=` (which keeps meaning "which record").

Verified: full clean rebuild, zero warnings. `test/test_ldap.c` proves the rename mechanics (both kinds, plus collision rejection); `test/test_hostauth.c` proves the critical safety property directly -- rename the currently-active admin group, confirm `admin_groups` follows, confirm a fresh login still works immediately after. 3 consecutive clean runs, full daemon-linked regression sweep.

## Part 129 (done): full live-infrastructure re-provisioning after the ADR-0146 reinstall, plus a real openssh privsep-chroot fix

The reinstalled real box (192.168.15.95) was a genuine blank slate. Stood back up: `dns-1`/`dns-2` (dnsmasq, 192.168.15.101/.102), `ldap-1`/`ldap-2` (glauth 2.4.0, rebuilt from source this session, 192.168.15.103/.104) with host-auth switched from Part 126/127's local backend to the live-LDAP backend and verified across a real reboot (the exact ADR-0146 scenario), `syslog-1`/`syslog-2` (sysklogd, 192.168.15.107/.108) confirmed receiving real forwarded container logs with the correct originating hostname, and `jumpbox1` (openssh+linux-pam+nss-pam-ldapd, 192.168.15.109) with real live-LDAP SSH login confirmed both ways -- password and pubkey, wrong password correctly rejected.

Real bugs found and fixed along the way, not worked around: glauth's `config` backend needs an explicit `sshkeyattr = "sshPublicKey"` (confirmed via its own source, no default assignment exists); `nslcd.conf` needs `pam_authc_ppolicy no` (glauth mishandles the LDAP password-policy control `nslcd` requests by default, returning a spurious `Invalid credentials`); and a real, standard OpenSSH privsep interaction -- with `UsePAM yes`, PAM's own conversation runs inside `sshd`'s privsep pre-auth child, which `chroot()`s to `--with-privsep-path` before that conversation happens, so `pam_ldap.so` can't reach `nslcd`'s local socket unless the chroot target is on the same filesystem. New `openssh/10.4p1-8/build.sh` moves `--with-privsep-path` from `/var/empty` to `/run/sshd-empty` (alongside `nslcd`'s own socket), and `jumpbox1`'s startup script hard-links the socket into the chroot once bound. Also: `pkg/recipes/kanxeo/v1.4.0/build.sh` re-pinned to `v1.49.0` (task #852, closing a 45-release-stale gap that predated `kanxeo-recover.c` entirely) and `glauth/2.4.0/build.sh`'s dead source URL re-served -- both real, separate gaps surfaced while standing this infrastructure back up.

Verified: full clean rebuild, zero warnings. Real, live, end-to-end SSH sessions against `jumpbox1` for both auth methods, confirmed via `sshd`/`nslcd`'s own captured logs and glauth's own server-side bind log, not just client exit codes.

## Part 128 (done): default HTTP/HTTPS ports become 80/443, plus a real `kanxeoctl login` bug found live

User-requested standard ports (`DEFAULT_PORT` 7620->80, `DEFAULT_HTTPS_PORT` 8443->443, in `daemon/src/main.c`/`daemon/src/daemon_config.c`/`cli/src/main.c`), applied live to 192.168.15.95 first via `PUT /system/daemon-config` then made the actual default. Found and fixed along the way: `kanxeoctl login`'s `cmd_login()` was the one call site in the whole CLI missing `w.buf[w.len] = '\0'` before using its JSON writer's buffer as a C string -- `jw_ensure()` reserves capacity for the trailing NUL but never writes it, so `strlen()` read past the real content into uninitialized heap memory, producing an intermittent, genuinely confusing `invalid JSON body` login failure. Also fixed a separate, pre-existing `docs/guides/remote-development.md` drift caught while editing the same lines: every example used a nonexistent `--server=` flag (real syntax is `--host=`/`--port=`) and a nonexistent bare `recipe add`/`recipe rm` subcommand (real: `pkg recipe add`/`pkg recipe rm`).

Verified: full clean rebuild, zero warnings; full daemon-linked regression sweep clean; live round-trip against 192.168.15.95 confirming both new ports answering and the old port gone, plus `kanxeoctl login` working against both the real box and a local scratch daemon.

## Part 127 (done): installer end-of-install UX fix -- no more silent freeze after MOK enrollment

Real feedback using the Part 126 ISO for a reinstall: after entering the MOK password twice, the installer looked frozen -- `kanxeo-install` (PID 1) just returned from `main()`, which the kernel answers with an unprompted panic. Fixed: a new `dual_console_wait_for_key()` helper (`image/src/dual_console.c`/`.h`, tested in `test/test_dual_console.c`), a clear "remove media, press Enter to reboot" banner, and a real `sync()`+`reboot(RB_AUTOBOOT)` instead of falling through to a panic. Also added a short note ahead of `mokutil --import` explaining its harmless `"Can't open /proc/keys"` warnings. Verified: 5 clean `test_dual_console` runs, full clean rebuild, and `test_installer`'s full QEMU install-and-boot re-run still PASS.

## Part 126 (done): LDAP startup resync + `kanxeo-recover` break-glass boot tool (ADR-0146)

Activating write-gating for real (task #843) caused a genuine total API lockout on the real box -- every login failing, no in-band recovery possible (no shell exists anywhere on this OS by design). Root cause: `ldap_init()` loads registered LDAP server bindings at startup, but nothing re-pushed live user/group content into those servers until the next explicit LDAP write; since every container is recreated on every host reboot, a restart silently left LDAP config empty, and write-gating's own deliberately-strict login logic treats that as an authoritative rejection rather than falling back locally. The identical gap ADR-0091 already fixed once for DNS, never mirrored to LDAP. Unblocked immediately via a from-scratch installer ISO build + full reinstall; this part fixes the actual bug and adds a real recovery path for next time.

Fixed: `daemon/src/main.c` now calls `ldap_record_sync_all()` at startup, mirroring `dns_server_sync_all()`'s own placement exactly. Added: `image/src/kanxeo-recover.c`, a deliberately tiny (~230 line) break-glass boot tool -- boots from a second GRUB entry on the same installer media, mounts the installed system's containers partition, resets only `admin_groups` back to empty (everything else preserved byte-for-byte) after a typed `RESET` confirmation, touches nothing else. `image/src/mkinstalleriso.c` grew a `kanxeo-recover-bin` argument (11->12) to stage it and write the second `menuentry`; `daemon/src/main.c`'s `iso_build_start()` (`POST /system/iso`) and `test/test_installer.c` updated to match.

See [ADR-0146](../adr/0146-ldap-startup-resync-and-break-glass-recovery.md) for the full reasoning, including why the recovery tool deliberately doesn't share `kanxeo-install.c`'s own partition-discovery code (a break-glass tool's real security property is how small and auditable it is), and a known, separately-tracked gap: `pkg/recipes/kanxeo/v1.4.0/build.sh` (the self-hosted ISO-build artifact source) is pinned to a tag that predates this tool, so it needs re-pinning before the server-side `POST /system/iso` path can produce a recovery-capable ISO too.

Verified: a new `test/test_ldap.c` regression proves the resync fix (register an LDAP server, create a group, real stop/start daemon cycle with zero LDAP writes in between, confirm the group still renders). Full clean rebuild, zero warnings. `mkinstalleriso` run for real against current kernel/signing-keys/isotools artifacts, producing a real bootable ISO with both GRUB entries and both binaries confirmed correctly staged. `test/test_installer`'s full QEMU boot-and-install verification re-run against the updated pipeline.

## Part 125 (done): `kanxeoctl hostauth-config` -- the last CLI gap before activating write-gating for real (ADR-0144, part 14 of N)

`GET`/`PUT /v1/system/hostauth-config` had no CLI surface -- raw `curl` was the only way to manage admin-group/LDAP-backend settings. Closed while preparing to actually activate write-gating on the real box (task #843): `hostauth-config show`/`set`, the latter a real client-side read-modify-write (the underlying `PUT` is full-replacement) so a single-flag change never silently wipes the rest of the config.

Verified via a local round-trip confirming the read-modify-write logic preserves untouched fields across two separate `set` calls. No dedicated CLI-level test added, matching this project's own precedent for comparably thin config wrappers (`tls-throttle`/`daemon-config`) -- the underlying API is already fully covered.

## Part 124 (done): retire the file-rendered SSH-target sync mechanism (ADR-0145)

ADR-0144's own Decision section already stated the intent: real live-LDAP SSH login "replaces the file-rendering SSH-target mechanism entirely." With Part 122 verified end to end, the user asked directly to complete that replacement: "let's remove the old mechanism." Removed outright -- `daemon/src/ldap.c`'s whole SSH-backed sync block and every call site, the `POST`/`GET`/`DELETE /v1/ldap/ssh-targets` API surface, the `kanxeoctl ldap ssh-target ...` CLI commands, and every current-state doc reference (rewritten, not just deleted, where an operator guide needed the real replacement instructions instead). `ssh_public_key` itself is untouched -- still rendered as glauth's own `sshkeys` attribute, its one real consumer now.

See [ADR-0145](../adr/0145-retire-file-rendered-ssh-target-sync.md) for the full reasoning. Verified: `GET /v1/ldap/ssh-targets` had zero live registrations on the real box before the code was deleted (the only container ever registered, `jumpbox1`, had already moved to the live-LDAP mechanism in Part 122); a full clean rebuild across every build target plus the full daemon-linked regression suite -- zero failures.

## Part 123 (done): real per-file owner/group on `POST /containers`' `files[]` (ADR-0144, part 13 of N)

A real gap Part 122's own `AuthorizedKeysCommand` credentials file surfaced: `files[]` had `mode` but no way to say *who* should own a staged file, so the only way to make it readable by the non-root `AuthorizedKeysCommandUser` was staging it world-readable (`0644`). Raised directly by the user once this compromise was disclosed -- "no hacky manual changes." Fixed as a real, general platform capability: `owner`/`group` (raw numeric uid/gid, `fchown()`'d immediately after the file is written, same pre-`clone3()` staging pass `mode` already uses) on every `files[]` entry, plus `kanxeoctl run --file-owner=CONTAINER_PATH:UID:GID` as a **separate** CLI flag from `--file=` -- a first attempt layering `OWNER:GROUP` directly onto `--file=`'s own `[:MODE]` grammar was caught live mis-parsing real values (`"75"` is simultaneously a plausible uid and a valid two-digit octal mode, so the existing trailing-colon heuristic can't disambiguate a second field the same way it safely handles MODE alone), so it was redesigned as a separate, unambiguous flag matched by `container_path` instead.

`jumpbox1`'s own `/etc/ldap-authkeys.conf` now stages `owner: 75, group: 75, mode: "0600"` -- the real `authkeys` uid, not world-readable.

Verified via a direct `stat()` against a container's own real upperdir in `test_container_files.c` (not just "it compiled"), plus a manual pass that caught the `--file=`-layered-grammar bug live before the `--file-owner=` redesign fixed it. Full regression sweep -- zero failures.

## Part 122 (done): real, live-LDAP SSH login -- `nslcd`/PAM/`AuthorizedKeysCommand` wired end to end, closes task #838 (ADR-0144, part 12 of N)

The closing landing of task #838 and of ADR-0144's container-side half: `sshd` on the real `jumpbox1` container now authenticates directly against live LDAP -- password via `pam_ldap.so`'s own real bind-as-user check, public key via a live `AuthorizedKeysCommand` `ldapsearch` -- rather than reading Kanxeo-rendered files. The original file-rendered SSH-target mechanism (task #731) stays as a real, still-supported alternative for images without PAM support, not replaced (`docs/api/README.md`'s new "Real, live-LDAP SSH login" section covers both).

A real, durable `svc-nslcd` service account (`can_search: true`) provides the bind identity both `nslcd.conf` and the `AuthorizedKeysCommand` script need -- confirmed live that glauth requires a real, capability-granted bind for any search at all, even with `IgnoreCapabilities = true` set (that only bypasses capability checks for an already-bound identity). `jumpbox1` was recreated with a real `nslcd.conf`/`nsswitch.conf`/`pam.d/sshd` stack, `UsePAM yes` plus `AuthorizedKeysCommand`/`AuthorizedKeysCommandUser` in `sshd_config`, and now runs `nslcd` and `sshd` together as two real foreground processes in one container -- this project's first multi-process container, both direct children of the container's own PID1 so neither becomes an orphan needing reparenting.

**Two more real, project-wide gaps found and fixed along the way**: (1) this project's own TCC-built `sshd` cannot `execve()` a shebang script directly -- a real `ENOEXEC`, root cause not further isolated once the fix (an explicit interpreter in the `AuthorizedKeysCommand` directive) was confirmed working; (2) every standard device node (`/dev/null` etc.) ended up mode `0644` instead of the `0666` already explicitly requested, because `mknod()`'s own permission bits are subject to this daemon's inherited umask and nothing ever called `umask(0)` -- never surfaced before because every prior daemon in this project runs as root; fixed with an explicit `chmod()` after `mknod()` in both `pkg_seed_image_baseline()` and `container_dev_mknod()`.

Verified end to end against a real, throwaway LDAP account (created, tested, deleted): real pubkey login, real password login, and a wrong password correctly rejected. Full regression sweep after the device-node fix -- zero failures, clean rebuild.

## Part 121 (done): `can_search` exposed as a real public LDAP user field (ADR-0144, part 11 of N)

Preparatory work for task #838's remaining parts (`nslcd`'s own `binddn`, a live `AuthorizedKeysCommand`'s own search bind) -- both need a real, durable, named LDAP service account with glauth's own `search` capability, and `can_search` (task #727) previously had no way to get set except through the internal container-auto-provisioning path; the public `POST`/`PUT /v1/ldap/users` always hardcoded it to `0`. Now a real, optional, full-field-replacement-on-PUT boolean like every other user field.

Also closed two unrelated, pre-existing real API-doc gaps found while touching this exact schema (`docs/api/openapi.yaml`/`docs/api/README.md`): `secondary_groups` was never documented at all, and the `password` field's description still said SHA-256/`passsha256`, stale since the bcrypt migration.

Verified: `test_ldap.c` extended to set `can_search` via PUT, confirm it's echoed and rendered as glauth's own `[[users.capabilities]]` stanza, then confirm a plain PUT omitting it clears it back to `false` (full-field-replacement, not just "can enable"). Regression sweep across `test_ldap`/`test_daemon`/`test_hostauth`/`test_dns`/`test_pki`/`test_web`/`test_cli` -- zero failures.

## Part 120 (done): `openssh` rebuilt with real PAM support, task #838 part B (ADR-0144, part 10 of N) -- plus a real build-sandbox toolchain gap found and fixed along the way

Second landing of task #838: `openssh` rebuilt with `--with-pam`, so `sshd` can authenticate through the real PAM stack Parts 116-118 already provide, instead of the previous no-PAM, shadow-file-only path. `UsePAM` stays at its real "no" default even in a `--with-pam` build -- turning it on plus a real `/etc/pam.d/sshd` stack is runtime container config, this task's next parts.

**A real, project-wide build-sandbox gap surfaced and got fixed along the way**: the `__pkgbuild` build sandbox's own default `cc` can no longer be trusted to mean TCC -- a `pkg_build()` with no explicit `CC=tcc` produced real GCC-family hardening flags on its link commands and silently dropped `-lpam`/`-lcrypt` from the built `sshd`'s own ELF `DT_NEEDED`, despite `configure` correctly detecting PAM and the link line genuinely listing both libraries. Root-caused via a live, intentionally-failing diagnostic build capturing `readelf -d` output, confirmed by re-running with `CC=tcc` forced. Almost certainly `gcc.recipe`'s own toolchain output having merged into the cumulative `g_pkgbuild_rootfs` sandbox at some point since most other recipes were last built there. Fixed in `openssh/10.4p1-7` by pinning `CC=tcc` explicitly (`CLAUDE.md` gets a new environment note); task #845 tracks auditing the other 82 `configure`-driven recipes still relying on the now-unsafe ambient default.

Verified: a local from-scratch `CC=tcc` build confirmed all five expected `NEEDED` entries (`libcrypt.so.1`, `libc.so.6`, `libpam.so.0`, `libcrypto.so.3`, `libz.so.1`); the formal recipe driven through the real `pkg` pipeline on 192.168.15.95's `jumpbox` image, a fresh throwaway container's own `/usr/sbin/sshd` confirmed byte-identical linkage, and `jumpbox1` itself restarted onto the new binary.

## Part 119 (done): LDAP-rendered `sshkeys` array -- real glauth SSH-key attribute, task #838 part A (ADR-0144, part 9 of N)

First landing of task #838 (a PAM-enabled `openssh` rebuild with a live `AuthorizedKeysCommand` LDAP query): `render_users_groups_toml()` now renders a user's `ssh_public_key` as glauth's own real `sshkeys = [...]` TOML field, the real LDAP Public Key (LPK) convention -- exposed by default as the `sshPublicKey` attribute. Previously this field only ever reached per-container `authorized_keys` files, a file-rendered copy invisible to a real `ldapsearch` against the directory; a live `AuthorizedKeysCommand` (this task's next part) needs exactly this to query.

Verified: `test_ldap.c`'s existing full rendered-config end-to-end test extended with a real `ssh_public_key` on user create, asserting the exact `sshkeys = [...]` line appears in the container's own rendered config, read back via the real `GET .../files` endpoint. Full regression sweep across `test_ldap`/`test_daemon`/`test_hostauth`/`test_dns`/`test_pki` -- zero failures.

## Part 118 (done): `nss-pam-ldapd` 0.9.13-2 -- real `/run` pidfile/socket paths, found while wiring `nslcd` for real (ADR-0144, part 8 of N)

A real, confirmed-needed follow-up surfaced while actually starting `nslcd` for the first time, during task #833's own PAM-enabled `openssh` work: `nslcd`'s upstream compiled-in defaults are `/var/run/nslcd/nslcd.pid` and `/var/run/nslcd/socket` -- the identical "`/var/run` doesn't exist in these images, only `/run` does" gap this project already hit and documented for `dnsmasq`'s and `chrony`'s own default pidfile paths (`CLAUDE.md`'s environment notes). Not a new TCC-vs-upstream gap of its own -- every one of Part 117's own source files still compiles unchanged.

Fixed with two real, first-class upstream configure knobs -- `--with-nslcd-pidfile=/run/nslcd/nslcd.pid --with-nslcd-socket=/run/nslcd/socket` (confirmed via `configure --help`), not a source patch. Since recipe versions are immutable once published (ADR-0107), this landed as a new `0.9.13-2` revision rather than an edit to `0.9.13`.

Verified: a local reconfigure confirmed `config.h` now defines both paths correctly; a full local `make` through `compat`/`common`/`nss`/`pam`/`nslcd` built clean under TCC with no new errors, and the resulting `nslcd` binary's own strings confirm both compiled-in paths. The formal `0.9.13-2` recipe was then driven through the real `pkg` pipeline end to end on both the local dev daemon and the real target box (192.168.15.95's `jumpbox` image), upgrading cleanly over the already-installed `0.9.13`.

## Part 117 (done): `nss-pam-ldapd` recipe -- real NSS/PAM LDAP lookups, closes task #832 (ADR-0144, part 7 of N)

The third and closing part of ADR-0144's container-side real-LDAP chain: `libnss_ldap.so.2`, `pam_ldap.so`, and the `nslcd` daemon that both talk to over a local unix socket, built from real source directly on top of Part 115's `openldap-client` and Part 116's `linux-pam`. With this landing, a container can resolve real Kanxeo LDAP accounts through ordinary `getpwnam()`/`getgrnam()`/PAM -- the mechanism a future PAM-enabled `openssh` rebuild will actually use for real SSH login, closing the loop this whole ADR started from: replacing file-rendered SSH-target accounts with a genuinely live LDAP-backed directory.

**Chosen deliberately over the classic PADL `nss_ldap`/`pam_ldap` modules** -- the literal names task #832 itself used -- because that original reference implementation has been unmaintained since roughly 2013 with no current, reachable source distribution. `nss-pam-ldapd` (Arthur de Jong's actively-maintained project) is a functional drop-in replacement with the same real integration shape (`nsswitch.conf`, `pam.d`), but a genuinely better security posture: one small privileged daemon (`nslcd`) is the only process that ever holds a real LDAP connection or credential, with the NSS and PAM modules themselves reduced to thin local-socket IPC clients rather than every process on the box linking `libldap` and touching LDAP credentials directly.

**Remarkably clean build -- zero new TCC compatibility gaps.** Every one of this package's roughly 35 source files across `compat`/`common`/`nss`/`pam`/`nslcd` compiled without a single error on the first real attempt (confirmed via a local build in this sandbox before the recipe was ever registered). The two real issues that did surface were both direct consequences of choices this same ADR-0144 chain had already made in its own earlier parts, not new problems of this package's own: (1) `configure`'s LDAP-library link probe (`AC_SEARCH_LIBS(ldap_search_ext, [ldap_r ldap])`) failed against `openldap-client`'s deliberately static-only `libldap.a` -- a static archive needs every one of its own transitive dependencies (`liblber`, and the full OpenSSL TLS symbol set from that recipe's `--with-tls=openssl`) spelled out explicitly by whoever links against it, unlike a shared `.so`'s own automatic `DT_NEEDED` resolution -- fixed by seeding `LIBS="-llber -lssl -lcrypto"` ahead of `configure`, so its own real link-test candidates succeed instead of failing opaquely; (2) the identical `-Wl,-h,...`/`--version-script` gap `openldap-client.recipe` already documents, here hardcoded directly into `nss/Makefile.am` rather than probed for -- fixed with a targeted `sed -i` on the *generated* Makefiles (no `configure.ac`/autoreconf-level patch needed, since these are plain Makefile-level LDFLAGS substitutions) swapping in the `-Wl,-soname,...` syntax TCC actually implements.

**Runtime module paths matched to what Part 116 already established and confirmed live**, via `--libdir=`/`--with-pam-seclib-dir=` -- both real, first-class upstream configure options, no relocation hack needed -- rather than trusting upstream's own `/usr/lib`/`/lib/security` defaults, which these images (no `ld.so.cache`/`ldconfig` step) would never actually resolve.

Verified at the same two real levels every part in this chain has established: a from-scratch local build using `openldap-client`'s and `linux-pam`'s own already-built headers/libraries directly, confirming both real fixes in isolation and a working `nslcd -V` banner; then the formal recipe driven through this project's own real `pkg` pipeline end to end, on *both* the local dev daemon and the real target box (192.168.15.95's `jumpbox` image) -- its own real dependency chain (`openldap-client linux-pam`) already satisfied from the two earlier parts, `nslcd -V` executed inside a real running container on both targets (exit `0`, correct version banner), and on `jumpbox` specifically, `libnss_ldap.so.2`/`pam_ldap.so` confirmed present at exactly the paths intended.

**Task #832 (container-side real LDAP packages: OpenLDAP client, Linux-PAM, nss/pam_ldap) is complete as of this part** -- all three pieces real, built from source under this project's own TCC toolchain across Parts 115-117, each verified through the real `pkg` pipeline, and all three deployed to the real target box, not left as local-only artifacts (a workflow correction made mid-chain, at the operator's own direct prompt -- see Part 116's own closing note). Next up, continuing the same ADR: task #833, a PAM-enabled `openssh` rebuild with a live `AuthorizedKeysCommand` LDAP query, to actually put these three recipes to work for real SSH login.

## Part 116 (done): `linux-pam` recipe -- real Linux-PAM framework from source under TCC (ADR-0144, part 6 of N)

The second part of ADR-0144's container-side real-LDAP work, continuing directly from Part 115's own OpenLDAP client foundation: a real, from-source Linux-PAM 1.6.1 build -- `libpam.so`/`libpam_misc.so` and a working baseline module set (`pam_unix`, `pam_permit`, `pam_deny`, `pam_env`, `pam_limits`, `pam_rootok`, `pam_warn`) -- the authentication framework the next part's `pam_ldap` module plugs into, and a future PAM-enabled `openssh` rebuild will link against.

**Pinned to the last autotools-based release, matching `iputils.recipe`'s own established precedent for the identical reason**: confirmed directly, via the GitHub API's own content listing across several tags, that v1.6.1 still ships a real `configure`/`configure.ac` while v1.7.0 switched to meson-only -- and meson's only supported Linux build backend is ninja, itself written in C++, which puts any meson-based release categorically out of reach of this project's C-only TCC toolchain no matter what else might be staged to support it.
