**One real, genuinely new TCC-vs-upstream portability gap, isolated and fixed via a real local build before this recipe was ever registered.** `pam_end.c` failed to compile with `error: invalid array size`, traced to `pam_cc_compat.h`'s own `PAM_IS_SAME_TYPE()` macro -- gated by `#if PAM_GNUC_PREREQ(3, 0)`, true only when `__GNUC__`/`__GNUC_MINOR__` are both defined (confirmed via `tcc -E -dM` that TCC never defines either). The resulting fallback path always evaluates to a hardcoded `0`, and `pam_end.c`'s own `pam_overwrite_string()` call expands to a compile-time array-vs-pointer safety check built on that exact macro -- the always-`0` fallback makes the check fail unconditionally, for *any* expression, not only the real mismatches it exists to catch. This is a genuine upstream portability gap (any compiler lacking `__builtin_types_compatible_p`, gated the same way, would hit it identically -- it has simply never surfaced because every real distro only ever builds Linux-PAM with GCC or Clang, both of which implement the builtin). Confirmed directly with a minimal standalone test program that TCC *does* correctly implement `__builtin_types_compatible_p` itself -- the bug is purely in how the macro gates access to it. Fixed with a one-line, narrowly-scoped `sed -i` source patch inside `pkg_build()` (this project's own established pattern for exactly this class of fix, per `tcc.recipe`'s own precedent), extending the one `#if` to also match `defined(__TINYC__)`. A broader `-D__GNUC__=N` compiler-flag approach was tried first and rejected after it broke glibc's own `<sys/syslog.h>` include chain a different way (unlocking `__GNUC__`-gated system-header branches TCC can't actually satisfy) -- the source patch is scoped to exactly the one macro that needed it, with zero blast radius anywhere else.

**A real design detail confirmed live, not just read from source and trusted.** `SECUREDIR` (where PAM modules live, and the literal string compiled into `libpam.so` as its own `DEFAULT_MODULE_PATH`) defaults to `$(libdir)/security` -- so this recipe's `--libdir=/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu` (this project's own real multiarch runtime-library convention, needed since these images have no `ld.so.cache`/`ldconfig` step to make PAM's own upstream `/lib64` default resolvable) correctly relocates the modules right along with the libraries, no separate `--enable-securedir=` needed. Verified by fetching the actually-deployed `libpam.so.0` back out of a real running container (`GET .../files`) and confirming its own compiled-in path string reads `/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/security/`, exactly matching where the module `.so` files themselves landed -- not assumed from a source-code reading alone.

Verified at the same two real levels Part 115 established: a from-scratch local build isolating the one real fix (`libpam_internal` → `libpam`, a genuine shared `.so` this time, unlike Part 115's static-only choice -- every module linked against it cleanly under TCC with none of that part's transitive-shared-library gap → `libpam_misc` → all seven modules, zero further errors); then the formal recipe driven through this project's own real `pkg` pipeline end to end, on *both* the local dev daemon and the real target box (192.168.15.95's `jumpbox` image -- the same image `openssh` already runs from), with the resulting container's own real files inspected directly to confirm both the library and module placement and, as above, the deployed library's own compiled-in search path.

**A real workflow correction mid-part, from the operator directly**: this recipe's own debugging/iteration initially happened entirely on a local dev daemon rather than the real target box, for faster and cheaper iteration while still actively hitting real compile failures -- but once a recipe is actually working, leaving it stranded on a local sandbox instead of also registering and building it on the real target defeats the point. Both `openldap-client` (Part 115) and this part's `linux-pam` were registered and built for real on 192.168.15.95's `jumpbox` image directly once each recipe was confirmed working, not left as local-only artifacts.

## Part 115 (done): `openldap-client` recipe -- real libldap/liblber from source under TCC (ADR-0144, part 5 of N)

The first part of ADR-0144's *container-side* real-LDAP work (task #832), shifting from the host-auth work Parts 111-114 covered to the other half of the original ask: real LDAP/PAM/NSS integration for container SSH login, replacing the file-rendering mechanism that started this whole ADR. This part is the foundation everything else in that chain (`nss_ldap`, `pam_ldap`, a PAM-enabled `openssh` rebuild) will link against: a genuine, from-source OpenLDAP client build under this project's own TCC toolchain -- `libldap.a`/`liblber.a` (static) plus the standard `ldapsearch`/`ldapmodify`/`ldapdelete`/`ldapmodrdn`/`ldappasswd`/`ldapwhoami`/`ldapvc`/`ldapcompare`/`ldapexop`/`ldapurl`/`ldapadd` tools.

**Three real, distinct TCC-vs-upstream-source gaps, each isolated and fixed via a real local build before the recipe was ever formalized or added to the package registry** -- matching this project's own established discipline (the same "confirmed directly via a local build in this sandbox" approach `gcc.recipe`/`sysklogd.recipe` already used): (1) the already-known `<regex.h>` VLA-in-prototype parse failure (`CPPFLAGS="-D__STDC_NO_VLA__=1"`, the identical fix `logstore.c` needed); (2) a genuinely new one -- TCC's linker accepts an undefined reference to a legacy LinuxThreads-only symbol (`pthread_kill_other_threads_np`) at link time instead of failing the way a real linker does, which misdirects `configure` down a legacy-detection path that then correctly fails because this really is modern NPTL glibc, not LinuxThreads (fixed via the standard `ac_cv_func_pthread_kill_other_threads_np=no` autoconf cache-variable override, no source patching); (3) TCC's linker has no support at all for GNU ld's symbol-versioning mechanism, despite `configure`'s own capability probe wrongly reporting that it does (fixed via OpenLDAP's own real, first-class `--enable-versioning=no` configure flag -- no workaround needed, the upstream build system already anticipated this exact situation for a different reason).

**A fourth, deliberate design choice rather than a bug fix**: static linking (`--enable-static=yes --enable-shared=no`) instead of this project's usual "build a real shared `.so.N`" default (`openssl.recipe`'s own precedent). Building `liblber.so`/`libldap.so` themselves links cleanly under TCC -- the real limit only appears one level further out: TCC's linker can't resolve a shared library's own *transitive* shared-library dependency purely at link time (confirmed directly: `libldap.so`'s embedded `DT_NEEDED` on `liblber.so.2` fails to resolve when linking `ldapsearch` against it, with `referenced dll 'liblber.so.2' not found`, even though the exact file is genuinely present on disk and `-rpath` would resolve it fine at runtime under glibc's own `ld.so`). Static linking sidesteps this cleanly, and happens to be the *better* shape for this task's own real end consumers regardless: a small, `dlopen()`'d NSS/PAM module wants its LDAP client code fully self-contained, not a separate runtime `.so` dependency every process loading that module would then also need to resolve.

Verified at two real levels, not one: first a from-scratch local build isolating each fix in turn (`configure` → `make -C libraries` → `make -C clients`, confirming every library, internal self-test binary, and client tool links cleanly, then a real bind+search with the freshly-built `ldapsearch` against a real local `glauth` instance -- the same one Part 113's own `ldapclient.c` verification used -- returning the correct entry); then, separately, the *formal recipe* driven through this project's own real, unmodified `pkg` pipeline end to end: `pkg recipe add`, `pkg install` (resolving and building its own real dependency chain, `openssl`/`libuuid`, first), and the resulting `base` image's own installed `ldapsearch` executed inside a real, freshly-created container via `POST /containers` with `capture_output` -- exit status 0, correct version banner captured. Not a scratch-directory approximation at any point; the actual artifact a real deployment would get.

## Part 114 (done): host authentication -- `kanxeoctl`/web dashboard login flow, API docs (ADR-0144, part 4 of N)

The fourth landing under ADR-0144, and the one that makes Parts 112-113's own login/write-gating machinery actually usable from both first-party clients, rather than only reachable via a raw `curl -H "Authorization: Bearer ..."` the way it was verified until now.

**`kanxeoctl login`/`logout`, persisted so a session survives across separate invocations.** Each `kanxeoctl` run is a fresh process with no memory of the last one, so the token has to live somewhere on disk between them -- `~/.kanxeoctl_token` (mode `0600`, since it holds a live bearer credential), written by `login` and removed by `logout`, the CLI's first-ever piece of local state (it has always been a purely stateless REST client until now, per its own top-of-file comment). The design choice that made this a small change rather than a sweeping one: `struct kx_client` (`client/include/httpclient.h`) itself gained an optional `token` field, and plain `kx_client_request()` now attaches it automatically when set -- every one of this file's several hundred existing call sites picked up authentication for free, with zero changes needed at any of them. `login` prompts for whichever of username/password isn't given as a flag, with real terminal echo disabled for the password (a `read_line_noecho()` helper built on the same `termios` raw-mode toggle the interactive shell's own line editor already uses).

**The web dashboard gets the same capability, browser-shaped.** A header badge/button (`Log in` when logged out, `logged in: <user>` + `Log out` once authenticated) opens a login modal built from the same shared modal component every other create-form already uses. The token lives in `localStorage` (this dashboard's own analog of the CLI's dotfile) and, mirroring `kx_client_request()`'s own design, is attached automatically by the two functions every one of this file's requests already funnel through (`apiRequest()`/`apiRequestRaw()`) -- no per-call-site changes anywhere else in a 6600-line file. A genuinely useful piece of UX beyond the bare minimum: any `401` from any request (a write attempted with no session, or one whose token just expired) automatically opens the login modal, so "why did my action just fail" has an immediate, actionable answer instead of only a status-bar error -- log in, then retry the same action.

**A real, previously-undocumented API contract gap, found and closed while writing this part's own docs.** `openapi.yaml` had zero mention of `POST /login`, `POST /logout`, or `GET`/`PUT /system/hostauth-config` -- Parts 112 and 113 shipped real, working, tested endpoints without ever adding them to the one file `CLAUDE.md`'s own Documentation Map calls "the authoritative REST API contract," a direct violation of that same file's "updated whenever the contract changes, before the daemon code that implements it" rule. Fixed: all four endpoints documented in full (request/response schemas, every status code, a new `HostauthConfig` schema), plus a new `components.securitySchemes.bearerAuth` entry carrying the write-gating rule's own prose once, referenced from the two operations most directly about authentication itself rather than mechanically repeated across the roughly 130 other mutating operations already in the file -- annotating each of those individually with a per-operation `security:` block is real, valuable, but larger mechanical work, deliberately left to the still-open documentation-audit effort's own API-confirmation phase rather than rushed here. `docs/api/README.md` gained a full "Host authentication" narrative section (the endpoints-at-a-glance table plus the actual mental model: one rule, bootstrap safety, sliding sessions, one directory with two backends) in the same change, per the Documentation Map's own "never after" rule for this pair of files. `docs/guides/cli-reference.md` and `docs/guides/web-dashboard.md` updated to match.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings). `test_cli`/`test_web` -- zero failures, unmodified, confirming nothing already covered regressed. `openapi.yaml` re-validated as real YAML after every edit (one real syntax bug caught this way: an unquoted `Authorization: Bearer <token>` inside a plain scalar description was parsed as a second mapping key -- fixed by quoting it). `kanxeoctl login`/`logout` verified end to end against a real scratch daemon: bootstrap-safety, a real `401` once gating activates, a real `0600`-permission token file, automatic reauthentication on the next separate invocation, correct `401` immediately after logout. The web dashboard's own login flow was verified against a **real running headless Chromium instance** (driven directly over the DevTools Protocol via a small script -- no test framework needed for a one-off verification like this) hitting a real daemon: confirmed the unauthenticated-write-then-401-then-auto-open-login-modal sequence, a real form submission logging in and persisting the token, an authenticated write succeeding afterward, and logout clearing every piece of client-side state back to logged-out -- with zero JavaScript console errors observed across the whole sequence.

## Part 113 (done): host authentication -- live LDAP backend, hand-rolled client (ADR-0144, part 3 of N)

The third landing under ADR-0144, completing the "one directory, two interchangeable backends" design Part 112's own doc comments already promised: `hostauth_login()` can now authenticate against a real, running `glauth` server over the wire, in addition to the local, in-process bcrypt check that has been the only path until now.

**A minimal, hand-rolled LDAPv3 client (`daemon/src/ldapclient.c`), matching this project's own established precedent** of speaking wire protocols directly rather than linking a client library -- the same posture already taken for rtnetlink, HTTP, WebSocket, and JSON. Implements real BER/DER encoding and decoding from first principles (definite-length short and long form; `SEQUENCE`/`INTEGER`/`OCTET STRING`/`ENUMERATED`/`BOOLEAN` primitives), a real RFC 4511 simple-bind exchange, and a search operation supporting exactly the one filter shape this project needs -- a top-level AND of one or more equality-match terms. Deliberately narrow in scope, by design: no SASL, no TLS, no referrals, no general filter grammar. A later ADR-0144 part doing real container-side LDAP integration (PAM/NSS) uses real OpenLDAP client libraries inside the isolated container runtime instead -- this hand-rolled client is specifically for the trusted daemon process's own login path, where the existing "no external client library" convention already applies.

`hostauth_login()`'s own extension: when `ldap_enabled`, every configured server (up to 3, a try-in-order list matching `resolv.c`'s own nameserver-list shape) is tried in order, using a bind DN built from this daemon's own already-loaded user/group records (`cn=<user>,ou=<primary-group-name>,<ldap_base_dn>` -- the confirmed-working short form; kanxeod has no other way to learn a running glauth server's own configured base DN, since that part of its config file is operator-authored and never rendered by this daemon). The first server to answer *authoritatively* -- a successful bind, or a real, well-formed rejection -- decides the outcome; an unreachable or erroring server is skipped in favor of the next one, never mistaken for a wrong password. Total unreachability (every configured server down, or LDAP disabled, or no local record to build a DN from at all) is the only case that falls back to the pre-existing local bcrypt check -- the same underlying credential data either way, so the fallback only ever changes *availability*, never *which password is actually correct*.

**A real, previously-undiscovered bug, found by this part's own live verification discipline, present since Part 111 and already deployed live.** `render_users_groups_toml()` wrote the `passbcrypt` TOML field as the literal bcrypt hash string. glauth's own real config-backend bind code, confirmed directly against its fetched v2.4.0 source, calls `hex.DecodeString()` on that field *before* ever calling `bcrypt.CompareHashAndPassword` -- meaning every single bcrypt-based bind against every glauth-backed account has silently failed with glauth's own `"invalid credentials, incorrect stored hash"` since Part 111 shipped, even though the daemon-side data, the TOML syntax, and the bcrypt hash computation were all completely correct the entire time. `test_ldap.c` never caught this because, deliberately (per its own header comment), it never spins up a real glauth process to attempt an actual bind -- only the rendered TOML's *shape* was ever checked, not whether a real server would actually accept it. This is exactly the class of gap "test it before moving on" exists to catch, and it was only found because this part's own client needed a real bind target to verify against: a real local `glauth` binary (already present in this sandbox from earlier session work) was configured by hand and driven both by the new `ldapclient.c` and, critically, by the standard `ldapsearch`/`ldapwhoami` reference client -- the same failure from both proved the bug was in the rendered data, not the new protocol code, before any fix was written. The fix hex-encodes the hash's own ASCII bytes (a plain byte-for-byte encoding, not a real transcoding -- glauth hex-decodes it straight back before ever touching bcrypt); `test_ldap.c`'s own assertions now decode the rendered hex and confirm a real `$2b$` hash underneath instead of checking a literal prefix.

`GET`/`PUT /v1/system/hostauth-config` gains `ldap_enabled`, `ldap_servers` (the try-in-order list), `ldap_port` (default 3893, glauth's own real default), and `ldap_base_dn` -- all optional on `PUT` so an older-shaped request body still works unchanged, but `ldap_enabled: true` is rejected outright without at least one server and a non-empty base DN.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings). `ldapclient.c` verified standalone against a real local glauth 2.4.0 process: successful bind, wrong-password rejection, nonexistent-user rejection, a closed-port connect failure, single-term and 2-term-AND-filter search (match and no-match cases both) -- every one of these cross-checked against `ldapsearch`/`ldapwhoami`, not just self-consistency against this project's own new code. `test_hostauth.c` extended with config-validation coverage for all four new fields, a full round-trip GET after a valid PUT, and a fully portable (no real glauth binary needed) functional proof that an unreachable configured server falls back to the local backend correctly while a wrong password still fails either way. Regression sweep across every plausibly-affected daemon-linked test (`test_daemon`, `test_cli`, `test_web`, `test_dns`, `test_pki`, `test_ldap`, `test_hostauth`) -- zero failures. **Live verification against the real deployed `glauth` servers (192.168.15.95's `ldap-1`/`ldap-2`), initially deferred and since confirmed.** The apparent unreachability turned out to be this session's own network path to that specific host, not the box itself (the operator confirmed direct access throughout). Once resolved, the fix was deployed there for real via this project's own self-hosted rebuild mechanism (`docs/guides/building-kanxeo.md`'s "From a running Kanxeo host" path) rather than any manual patching: pushed and tagged `v1.42.0` on the project's own git remote, ran `pkg hostbuild kanxeo --build-image=kanxeo-builder --upgrade --wait --deploy` on the box itself (compiles with its own already-staged `kanxeo-builder` toolchain image, assembles a fresh squashfs server-side, stages it to the inactive A/B slot), confirmed the assembly generation counter before trusting the fresh squashfs, then `POST /system/reboot` to cut over -- with the operator's explicit go-ahead first, given the real, visible interruption a host reboot causes to every container it runs (`dns-1/2`, `ntp-1/2`, `syslog-1/2`, `ldap-1/2`, `jumpbox1`). The box came back up on the new build (`GET /system/boot` reporting the fresh slot and build time), and every one of those containers restarted on its own (`restart: always`). A real throwaway user (`bindprobe`, created via `kanxeoctl ldap user add`, removed immediately after) then bound successfully -- `resultCode=0` -- against both `ldap-1` and `ldap-2` directly, using this same `ldapclient.c`, plus a real search confirming the entry. The bcrypt hex-encoding fix is confirmed working in the real, live deployment this whole ADR-0144 effort was ultimately for, not just in a local test harness.

## Part 112 (done): host authentication foundation -- login/logout/write-gating, local backend (ADR-0144, part 2 of N)

The second landing under ADR-0144, and the first one with real, visible behavior: `kanxeod`'s REST API goes from "no authentication at all, ever" to real, working sessions and write-gating -- local-backend only (checking this daemon's own already-persisted user records directly, no network call); a later part adds a live LDAP bind attempted first when enabled, without changing this part's own contract at all.

`POST /v1/login` verifies real bcrypt credentials (`ldap_user_check_password()`, ADR-0144's own first part) and issues a real, random session token; `POST /v1/logout` invalidates it, idempotently. `GET/PUT /v1/system/hostauth-config` holds the two things this whole model actually needs configured: which group(s) count as admin (a list, not a single hardcoded name -- "a configurable group," per the user's own explicit ask), and a sliding idle timeout in seconds, where 0 means something deliberate and real: no session reuse at all, every single write re-authenticates from scratch.

The one authorization rule lives at the very top of `dispatch()`, before any route is ever matched: every `POST`/`PUT`/`DELETE` needs a valid admin-group session, and so does the container console specifically -- a `GET`-verb WebSocket upgrade that is, in real effect, arbitrary command execution inside a container, gated here by what it actually *does* rather than what HTTP method it happens to use. Every other `GET` stays open, unconditionally, forever -- reads were never the concern. Bootstrap safety is structural, not a special-cased escape hatch: `hostauth_gating_active()` -- a real, direct scan via a new `ldap_user_for_each()` enumeration primitive, never a cached flag that could drift out of sync with a group deletion or membership change -- is false until the first admin-group user genuinely exists, and true the instant one does. A fresh install can never lock itself out of its own API; the alternative designs considered and rejected earlier in this same conversation (a Cisco-style always-present local enable password, a separate break-glass credential) turned out to be unnecessary once "gating only activates once someone is actually there to gate for" was the real rule -- simpler, and arguably more correct, than either.

**A real bug, caught by the test written for exactly this, not shipped.** `handle_logout()`'s own header-parsing buffer was sized to fit only the bare token, not the real `"Bearer <token>"` string `http_find_header()` actually has to match against -- the lookup silently reported "doesn't fit," `hostauth_logout()` was never called at all, and the handler still responded `204` (its own correct, documented "idempotent, no error either way" contract) as if it had worked. `test_hostauth.c`'s own step 11 caught this immediately (`write after logout expected 401, got 201`) before anything shipped -- exactly the value of writing the real, adversarial test alongside the feature rather than after it.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings). Full regression sweep (47 test binaries) -- zero failures (one confirmed pre-existing timing flake, `test_container_restart`, reproduced clean on immediate retry). Critically, every one of the 46 *pre-existing* tests continued to pass completely unmodified -- none of them ever creates an admin-group user, so gating correctly never activates for any of them, which is the bootstrap-safety design proving itself in practice rather than merely being asserted. New `test/test_hostauth.c` covers the full real lifecycle: bootstrap-safety, wrong-password rejection, gating activating the instant an admin exists, unauthenticated/garbage-token rejection, a real authenticated-but-non-admin user correctly failing authorization while still passing authentication (two different questions, confirmed to actually behave as two different questions), logout invalidation and its own idempotency, and the `idle_timeout_seconds=0` single-use-token contract end to end.

## Part 111 (done): host-auth data model foundation -- bcrypt + real secondary groups (ADR-0144, part 1 of N)

The first landing under ADR-0144 -- real host authentication for `kanxeod`'s own REST API (which has had none at all, ever, this entire project) and real LDAP for container login (replacing the existing file-rendering SSH-target mechanism). Raised directly by the user once the shape of the existing SSH-account rendering was understood for what it actually is: a copy, not a live query -- "we're engineering, not hacking," real LDAP throughout, for containers *and* the host. This part ships the data-model foundation everything else depends on; no write-gating or login flow exists yet, that's the next part.

**Real bcrypt, not hand-rolled, resolving rather than reversing a prior documented decision.** The LDAP user store's original password field (`passsha256`, unsalted SHA256) was itself a deliberate "No Hacks" call -- hand-rolling bcrypt from scratch would mean reimplementing a security-critical primitive with no audit trail, a real and correct objection at the time, when this was only ever a container-facing directory password. It stops being sufficient once these same credentials become the *host's own* authentication root of trust. The resolution vendors a real, audited, widely-deployed reference implementation instead of writing one from scratch (OpenBSD's own `bcrypt.c`/`blowfish.c`, ISC-licensed, `daemon/src/vendor/`) -- still zero hand-rolled crypto, just no longer avoiding bcrypt altogether. `glauth` already supports `passbcrypt` as a first-class field (confirmed directly in its own real source), so this is a strict strengthening with zero directory-compatibility loss -- nothing about the container-facing LDAP contract changes shape, only the credential strength underneath it.

**Real secondary/supplementary group membership**, not just `primarygroup` -- `struct ldap_user` gains `secondary_groups[]` (up to 16, matching real POSIX `id -Gn` semantics), a new `--secondary-groups=` CLI flag and `secondary_groups` REST field, rendered into `glauth.cfg` as `othergroups = [...]` (glauth's own real `User.OtherGroups` field, field-name-lowercased with no toml tag, the same convention every other field in `render_users_groups_toml()` already follows). `ldap_user_is_in_group()`/`ldap_user_check_password()` are new, shared primitives -- the one real answer to "is this user valid and a member of that group" every future host-auth backend (local in-process, and a later live-LDAP one) will consult, not reimplemented per caller.

A real mistake happened mid-work and is recorded here rather than quietly fixed: an `ldap user update` call meant to trigger a harmless re-sync instead clobbered a real user's `givenname`/`sn`/`mail`/`loginshell`/`homedirectory`/`ssh_public_key` fields, because this endpoint has always been full-field-replacement (an omitted field resets to empty) -- a real, pre-existing, already-documented behavior, not something this change introduced, but one that bit for real during this session and is worth a permanent note. Restored immediately from values captured moments before; no data was lost, but it's the kind of thing a "we're engineering, not hacking" session should own up to plainly rather than let slide by.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings). Full regression sweep (46 test binaries) -- zero failures (one confirmed pre-existing timing flake, `test_container_restart`, reproduced clean on immediate retry). A standalone functional test proved the vendored bcrypt implementation actually works correctly end to end (correct/wrong password, empty hash, and -- the one property that actually matters for a salted hash -- two hashes of the identical password differ and both still independently verify) before it was ever wired into `ldap.c` at all. `test_ldap.c`'s own old literal-SHA256-hash assertions (structurally incompatible with bcrypt's own deliberate non-determinism) were replaced with a real `$2b$` shape check plus a captured-value comparison proving the hash survives an update unchanged; new secondary-group scenarios cover the update echo, the `othergroups` rendering, and an unknown gidnumber's real 400 rejection.

## Part 110 (done): container DNS resolution via an explicit `dns_servers` field (ADR-0143)

Answers a question deferred to the very end of the ADR-0141/0142 storage-placement work: does a container get DNS resolution via an explicit host mapping/binding the way some internal Kanxeo services do, or via a standard/global resolv.conf? Investigated directly rather than assumed -- neither exists. `create_container_from_body()` never wrote or bind-mounted anything DNS-related into a container's own overlay; `dns_server_register()` (the mechanism behind an internal `.internal`-zone server like `dns-1`) only ever pushed a hosts file *into* the server container itself. A container that needed DNS had to bake it into its own image or have an operator hand-construct a resolv.conf via the generic `files[]` field -- nothing purpose-built, nothing documented as the intended path. A real, previously-undiscovered gap, not a deliberate prior boundary.

New optional `"dns_servers"` field on `POST /v1/containers`: 0-3 IPv4 addresses, staged as a real `/etc/resolv.conf` into the container's own overlay upperdir before its process ever `execve()`s. Reuses two existing primitives rather than inventing new ones: the exact pre-clone3 staging mechanism `files[]` already established (written directly into upperdir on this daemon/host process, already there the moment the container execve()s), and `resolv.c`'s own `RESOLV_MAX_NAMESERVERS` cap (glibc's real resolv.conf `MAXNS`) rather than a second invented limit. `struct registry_entry` gained matching `dns_server_ips`/`dns_server_count` fields (the same "id not content, display-only" shape `file_paths` already has for `files[]`), echoed on every `GET /containers` response the same way `cmd`/`disk` already are.

**Deliberately explicit, no automatic wiring to a registered internal DNS server** -- ADR-0143 documents the full reasoning, but the short version: this project has no fixed notion of "the" internal DNS server (zero, one, or several `dns_server_register()`-registered containers can exist on different networks at once), so guessing which one a container should use would be inventing behavior nobody asked for. The same posture ADR-0076 already established for the *host's* own outbound resolution (`PUT /system/resolv` takes an explicit, operator-supplied list too, no auto-discovery) -- consistent with the rest of this project's own no-magic precedent, not a new design philosophy invented just for this field.

`kanxeoctl run --dns-server=A.B.C.D` (repeatable, up to 3) and a matching "DNS servers" field on the web dashboard's run-form and a container's own Options tab close the loop end to end -- API first, then CLI, then web, the same discipline every phase this session has followed.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings across 69 build targets). Full regression sweep (45 test binaries) -- zero failures (one confirmed pre-existing timing flake, `test_container_restart`, reproduced clean on immediate retry, unrelated to this change). New `test/test_container_dns_servers.c` proves the full real path -- unlike storage-placement work, this needed no real disposable disk: invalid IP rejected, too-many-entries rejected, a `files[]` conflict on the same path rejected, and on success the actual staged `/etc/resolv.conf` content read back directly from the host side and confirmed byte-correct, plus confirmation that omitting the field stages nothing at all. Real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`) confirmed a CLI-created container's real staged nameserver list renders correctly on its own Options tab, and that the run-form's new field exists and is wired into the create-request body. Deployed to 192.168.15.95 (v1.41.0); a throwaway verification container pointed at that box's own real internal `dns-1`/`dns-2` server addresses (192.168.15.101/.102) confirmed the full REST contract live -- `dns_servers` correctly echoed back on `GET /containers/{name}` against real production infrastructure -- before being removed.

## Part 109 (done): container-storage post-creation migration (ADR-0142 Section 4)

The one piece ADR-0141's own six-phase plan deliberately left out ("chosen once at POST /containers time, not movable after") and ADR-0142 Section 4 picked back up once the daemon-wide migration mechanism (fork+pidfd async bulk copy, then a synchronous finalize pass) had already been proven across three kinds. `POST/GET /v1/containers/{name}/migrate-storage` gives an operator a real way to move a container's own overlay storage after the fact, without a DELETE-and-recreate that would lose whatever that container had already written.

The genuine new engineering here is entirely in the multiplicity and the cutover, not the copy mechanism itself (reused as-is, `treecopy_recursive()`). Multiplicity: state/log/rebuildable-storage are each one fixed daemon-wide slot -- a state-storage migration and a log-storage migration are independent, but there's only ever one of each *kind* in flight. A container-storage migration is keyed by *container name* instead -- many containers could each have their own migration running at once -- so `daemon/src/containerstoragemigrate.c` tracks jobs in a small table (`CONTAINERDEF_MAX` slots, found/allocated by name) rather than reusing `storagemigrate.c`'s fixed per-kind array.

Cutover: every daemon-wide kind is read/written only by this single-threaded reactor itself, so repointing its own cached path mid-copy is safe -- nothing else is racing it. A container's own overlay is actively read/written by that container's own live process for as long as it keeps running, so the bulk async copy here is knowingly a first, possibly-stale pass. `finalize_container_storage_migration()` closes that gap the only way that's actually safe: once the bulk copy finishes, it stops the container (the exact same `registry_remove()`-plus-reactor-conn-teardown primitive `handle_stop()`/`handle_rolling_restart_timer_event()` already use, so a frozen cgroup is thawed before the kill the same way both of those already handle), runs one more synchronous copy pass against a now-quiescent tree, patches the persisted definition's own `disk` field, and replays `create_container_from_body()` to bring the container back up from the new location -- the identical replay `POST .../start` and a crash-restart already use, one source of truth for "how a definition becomes live" rather than a third bespoke version of it just for this. `containerdef_patch_disk()` (new) does the field-level surgery, generalizing `containerdef_patch_image_version()`'s existing "know the exact byte span, splice the new value in" technique to a value that isn't always a quoted string (a JSON `null` when migrating back to the default placement). A failure anywhere after the stop -- the final copy pass, or the restart itself -- brings the container back up from its ORIGINAL location instead of leaving it down: the whole operation is all-or-nothing from the caller's point of view, the same posture every other migration endpoint here already has.

A real, adjacent gap was found and closed along the way, not left for later: `is_active_storage_singleton_placement()`'s own 409 safety check (guarding `DELETE /diskroles/{name}` and `POST /disks/{name}/format`) never accounted for a `container-storage`-role disk that real containers were actually placed on -- a pre-existing hole from the *original* `POST /containers` `disk` field (ADR-0102), predating this ADR entirely. Removing the role or reformatting such a disk would have silently orphaned or destroyed that container's own live workload data, with no warning at all. New `disk_has_container_in_use()` closes it -- checking both live registry entries and persisted definitions currently between a crash and their next restart (the exact body-reparse fallback `DELETE`'s own crashed-container cleanup already established), the same discipline this project's storage-placement work has applied to every other kind.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings). Full regression sweep -- zero failures (one confirmed pre-existing timing flake, `test_container_restart`, reproduced clean on immediate retry, unrelated to this change). New `test/test_container_storage_migrate.c` proves every validation/rejection path reachable without a real disposable disk to format -- missing container, no persisted definition to restart from, missing/invalid `disk` field, unknown disk, wrong role, role-correct-but-unmounted, already-active -- the same honest testability boundary `test_storage_placement.c` already documents for the other three kinds. Real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`) confirmed the new Storage placement section on a container's own Options tab renders real placement data against a live container, and that a migration attempt against a deliberately invalid disk surfaces the real daemon-side validation error through the dashboard's existing status mechanism end to end. Deployed to 192.168.15.95 (v1.40.0) -- and because that box's own `sda` already carries the `container-storage` role and is mounted (Part 108's probe-mount fix, confirmed still holding across this reboot), the full real cutover path was exercised live against a throwaway verification container, not just the dev sandbox's validation-only coverage: the migration reached `state:"ready"`, the container came back up on a fresh PID with `disk:"sda"` correctly persisted in `GET /containers/{name}`, and the throwaway container was removed afterward.

## Part 108 (done): multi-disk storage placement Phase 6 -- disk I/O + capacity stats (ADR-0142), stale-fs_type probe-mount fix, full regression + deploy

Closes the six-phase ADR-0141 plan (Phases 0-5 shipped as Parts 100-107) with the ADR-0142 piece scoped alongside it from the start: per-disk I/O and capacity statistics, and a real fix for a disk left permanently unmounted since before this session began. Stated plainly rather than left implicit: the original scope note for this phase also mentioned a Host Stats page per-disk chart section -- that piece was not built, only the Disks table's own columns were added, and it remains open if wanted later.

`struct discovered_disk` gained two new families of field, both filled in during the same `disk_enumerate()` pass that already reads `/sys/block/<name>` for everything else it reports: `reads_completed`/`writes_completed`/`sectors_read`/`sectors_written`/`io_time_ms` from `/sys/block/<name>/stat` (fields 1/5/3/7/10, the standard kernel block-layer counters), and `used_bytes`/`free_bytes` via `statvfs(2)` when the disk is currently mounted. No new endpoint -- `GET /disks` was already the correct, single place for this, matching every other disk fact this project already reports there. `kanxeoctl disks` and the web dashboard's Disks table were both extended to surface the new fields (an indented second CLI line per disk; two new table columns).

The other half of this phase closes a gap that had been live and known since earlier in this same session: 192.168.15.95's own `sda` had been formatted during the original multi-disk management Phase C, before Phase 1 of this ADR added `diskrole_set_fs_type()` persistence -- so `diskformat_remount_present_role_disks()`'s boot-time remount pass had nothing to look up, and skipped it, silently, on every single boot since. Rather than requiring an operator to destructively re-format a disk that was already correctly formatted and simply predates a bookkeeping mechanism, the boot-time remount pass now probes the two filesystem types this project's own format code has ever produced (`ext4`, then `btrfs`) via `mount(2)` directly -- a mismatched fstype string just fails cleanly, the same technique real mount tooling already uses when a type isn't specified up front. The first successful probe both mounts the disk and persists the discovery, so every later boot uses the direct remembered path and never probes again.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings across all 69 build targets). Full regression sweep (36 test binaries) -- zero failures. Real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`) confirmed the new Usage/I/O columns render real values against a fresh scratch daemon. Deployed to 192.168.15.95 via the established recipe-add + hostbuild-upgrade + reboot sequence; confirmed live, directly, the one thing this fix exists to produce: `sda` now reports `mounted:true` where it had reported `false` for the entire rest of this session, and the new I/O/capacity fields report real, non-zero values for that box's actual disks.

## Part 107 (done): multi-disk storage placement Phase 5 -- backup-config end to end (ADR-0141)

Sixth and final new-resource phase for ADR-0141's multi-disk storage placement work. A genuinely different shape from Phases 2-4: those three each moved *where a live directory lives*, with a shared async migration primitive built once in Phase 2 and reused unchanged since. Backup-config isn't a migration at all -- it's a periodic write of an already-existing snapshot (`GET /system/backup`'s own JSON bundle, unrelated new code) to a `backup`-role disk, on a schedule. The `backup` disk role has existed since multi-disk management's original Phase B, a pure inert label the whole time; this phase is what finally gives it something real to do.

`GET/PUT /v1/system/backup-config` (with `GET .../status` and `POST .../snapshot-now` alongside it) follows `daemon-config`'s own partial-update convention exactly, per ADR-0141's own explicit design note -- setting `interval_hours` alone leaves `disk`/`enabled` untouched, the same UX every other small `*-config` resource in this API already has. The disk itself is deliberately *not* validated at `PUT` time (it doesn't need to exist yet, carry the right role, or be mounted) -- that check happens at snapshot time instead, the identical "pure bookkeeping now, real validation where the real action happens" split `storagemigrate_start()` already established for the other three storage kinds; `GET .../status` is where an operator actually learns what happened. The periodic auto-snapshot timer mirrors `pkg/repo-config`'s own sync-interval timer almost exactly (conditional arming -- `interval_hours <= 0` or `enabled: false` simply never arms it, manual `snapshot-now` remains available regardless), the established pattern for "a schedule that might not be configured yet" this daemon already has in two other places (NTP, pkg-repo-sync).

**A real accuracy gap was caught and fixed before anything shipped, not discovered after**: this phase's own early design comments -- mirroring ADR-0141's own summary text almost verbatim -- described the reused bundle as including "the same JSON+PKI bundle GET /system/backup already produces." Before writing a single line of REST documentation, `do_system_backup()`'s actual code was read directly rather than trusted from that summary: it bundles container definitions, networks, DNS records, installed-package state, every on-disk recipe version, and site config -- and has *never* included PKI at all. `kanxeoctl`'s own long-standing `backup` command help text already said so explicitly ("NEVER PKI keys"), and `docs/api/README.md`'s existing `GET /system/backup` section already documented the exclusion as deliberate, pre-existing security design (private key material is never returned over this API anywhere, by a rule this endpoint doesn't bend). Every doc comment and REST doc this phase actually shipped was corrected to match the verified real bundle, not the unverified ADR summary, before it ever reached a committed file -- an operator wanting PKI material preserved off-box has state-storage's own live migration (Phase 2) for that instead, which genuinely does carry the real PKI directory, a different concern (a live working copy, not a portable snapshot) that this module was never meant to solve.

`is_active_storage_singleton_placement()` picked up its fourth and final check -- the currently configured backup-config disk -- exactly as ADR-0141's own safety-check section specified from the very beginning, alongside the three storage-placement kinds, not tacked on as an afterthought once this phase actually arrived.

Verified: full clean rebuild (`-Wall -Werror`, zero warnings across 67 build targets). Full regression sweep (36 test binaries) -- zero failures (one confirmed pre-existing timing flake, `test_syslogfwd`, reproduced clean on immediate retry). New `test/test_backup_config.c` proves the full validation chain a snapshot attempt can hit without ever formatting a real disk: no disk configured, wrong role, role-correct-but-unmounted, plus the partial-update PUT semantics (one field set, the others provably untouched) and the 409 safety check, all exercised live against this dev sandbox's own real host hardware via non-destructive role assignment. Real headless-browser session (Chromium via `puppeteer-core`) confirmed the new Backup page section renders and the full save/snapshot-now round trip works through the dashboard's own UI, surfacing the real underlying failure reason rather than a generic error.
